# Retrospective log

Standing record of what this project's process gets wrong and right, kept so future decisions are made against evidence instead of memory. Rules:

- **Every closed build ticket appends an entry**: rounds it took, what each gauntlet round caught, findings refuted (with evidence), and the one process lesson worth keeping. The closing session writes it — no entry, no close.
- **Phase boundaries get a full retrospective** (below the running entries): metrics across tickets, doctrine changes, and what gets promoted to the `/campaign` skill or auto-memory (repo lessons stay here; transferable lessons go up).
- Product-level learning is separate and built-in: correction-rate telemetry per accepted object type (decisions #5/#17) is the product retrospecting on its own interpretations. This file is about *process*.

---

## Execution authority (#120 round-6 design consolidation)

Round 5 closed each execution finding with a separate mechanism — a lease claimed
in `runGranted`, an artifact rule scoped to the boot's verifier, an owner check on
settle, a report bounds-check in `commands.ts` — and the seams *between* those
mechanisms became the round-5 defect cluster both foreign lineages converged on.
This round replaces the scatter with ONE record and derives every guard from it.

**THE RECORD.** A session's *execution authority* is four facts, written in the
SAME ledger transaction as `session_opened` (`projectSessionOpened`), so a granted
draw is BORN with determinate authority and no window exists where a spent draw
has none:

- `execution_mode` — `provider` (a wired ExecutionProvider owns this session's
  execution and its terminal) or `external` (no provider this boot; an external
  member settles it — the documented external-settle mode). Decided at grant from
  whether execution is wired, carried in the `session_opened` event so replay is
  deterministic.
- `execution_owner` — the instance id of the granting process, stamped at grant
  for a `provider` session (NULL for `external`). Also carried in the event.
- `execution_authority` — an unforgeable capability token (a random UUID), minted
  at grant for a `provider` session, ROW-ONLY: never in the event, never on the
  wire (`toWire` broadcasts the whole event, so a secret in it would leak to every
  room member). Its value need not survive replay — a replayed session is already
  terminal or is a wedge the reconciler reads the row for — so minting it in the
  projection is correct, and it is the same class of process-liveness bookkeeping
  the lease columns already are.
- `execution_heartbeat_at` — stamped at grant so an unclaimed-but-granted provider
  session is reconcilable the moment its granting process dies.

**WHAT THE CAPABILITY AUTHORIZES.** `execution_authority` is the capability to
write a `provider` session's terminal — BOTH `settled` and `failed`. `settle_session`
of a `provider`-mode session is refused unless the caller presents the matching
token; a room member (opener included) never sees it, so cannot forge either
outcome. The token is held only by in-process trusted components: the coordinator
obtains it from `claim`, the reconciler reads it off the row. Neither is the wire.

**THE CLAIM (F5).** `ExecutionOwnership.claim` is a single guarded UPDATE that
transitions `unclaimed → running` EXACTLY ONCE (`execution_claimed_at IS NULL`
guard) and RETURNS the session's STORED context (`plan_id/harness/model`) plus the
authority token. The coordinator builds `SessionContext` from that return value,
never from its caller's `GrantedSession` payload, so caller-supplied plan/harness/
model can no longer override the row. A re-entrant claim matches zero rows.

**HOW EACH FINDING IS CLOSED BY THE RECORD:**

1. *Draw commits then leases separately → kill-between → spent, unleased,
   never-reconciled (campaign-stopping).* The lease (owner + heartbeat + mode +
   token) is written IN the `session_opened` transaction. There is no "between".
2/3/6. *Opener preempts the provider with a fabricated `settled` OR `failed`;
   artifact policy bound to the boot verifier not the session; a clean settle at
   settle-time boot state.* A `provider` session's terminal requires the capability
   token (covers both outcomes); a clean `settled` requires a provider-verified
   artifact keyed on the ROW's `execution_mode`, not on `verifyArtifact !==
   undefined` this boot.
4. *Guard optional via omitting `db`/`ownership` at construction.* `ownership` is
   REQUIRED on the coordinator; a test passes an in-memory fake, it can no longer
   be omitted into silence. `configureExecution`/`index.ts` always wire it.
5. *Re-entrant claim; caller data trusted over the row.* The claim is once-only and
   returns stored context (above).
7. *Malformed/`undefined` provider report leaves the session open.* The report is
   parsed with a runtime schema INSIDE the coordinator's failure boundary; an
   invalid or missing report synthesizes a failed receipt.
8 (+3). *Reconciliation tied to the boot's provider config.* The stale-lease sweep
   runs on a timer on EVERY boot regardless of whether execution is enabled; the
   heartbeat is scoped to `status='open'` (never a completed run); reconcile throws
   → `unreconciled` unless an exit actually appended.

**git (grok F2, git.ts:315).** Pinning `GIT_DIR`/`GIT_WORK_TREE` alone left the
per-worktree gitdir's `commondir` and `HEAD` FILES writable by a same-UID harness:
rewriting `$GIT_DIR/commondir` at a victim repo and `$GIT_DIR/HEAD` at `refs/heads/
main` redirected the adapter's OWN commit onto the victim's trunk. The adapter now
also pins `GIT_COMMON_DIR` (captured at resolve) so a rewritten `commondir` is
ignored, and re-asserts `HEAD` to the session branch before its own commit so a
rewritten `HEAD` cannot move a victim's `main`. Occupancy-not-provenance in
worktree mode stays a documented known boundary (needs the BUY sandbox).

## Campaign to date — day-one retrospective (2026-08-01)

**Shape of the campaign so far**: 1 research pre-map (design corpus, 6 prototype versions mined) → map #1 charted with 17 decision tickets → 15 decisions + 3 research tickets resolved in ~one day → 9 build tickets graduated → 3 gauntlets run, all FAIL on round 1, all producing fix rounds that made the work *more architectural*, not more patched.

**What the gauntlet rounds actually caught** (evidence for the method):
- Scaffold r1 (grok + codex, blind): reducer accepted pre-blessed proposals; acceptance ignored proposal lifecycle; both lineages independently converged on the missing durable event ledger — convergence across uncorrelated critics proved to be the strongest signal available.
- Scaffold r2 delta (grok): the r1 fix's *contract claim* outran its guarantee — watermark-on-refusal still allowed live≡replay divergence. Root-cause fix (r3): rejection is a command-layer result, never state. Lesson: **a fix round's own claims need the same adversarial treatment as the original**.
- Prototype r1 (opus rendered + codex source): the answer-binding path hardcoded the answer — recording the *opposite* of what the user typed while labeled "nothing is inferred". Caught in 3 clicks by a critic who actually drove the page. Lesson: **rendered artifacts need a critic that renders them**; source review alone missed half the story.
- Ingest r1 (codex): pagination could silently write a valid-looking partial corpus. Lesson: **the fail-open class (valid-shaped incomplete output) recurs in every layer** — it has now appeared in the reducer, the compose stack, and the fetcher.

**Findings refuted, and why that matters**: 1 of ~40 adjudicated findings was wrong (MinIO healthcheck — the image ships `mc`; the suggested fix would itself have broken the chain), refuted by probing the live container, not by argument. Treating critic findings as hypotheses is not overhead; it caught the one that would have introduced a regression.

**Process corrections made mid-campaign** (each now encoded in `/campaign`):
- Build tickets were born sparse (spec lived in orchestrator prompts) — corrected to Tier-2 enrichment at creation after Lars flagged it. A ticket an agent can't pick up cold is a defect.
- Critic output piped through `tail -c` truncated a verdict; grok's default `--max-turns` once exited 0 with no verdict emitted. Both now handled (file capture; resume with higher turns; missing verdict ≠ pass).
- Demo corpus discovered mid-build to be structurally flat (GitHub issues carry no threading) — the corpus decision was amended on its closed ticket rather than silently changed. Amendments to closed decisions leave the same receipt trail as decisions.

**Metrics to watch as the campaign proceeds**: rounds-per-ticket (currently trending 2–3; if it hits 4+ the builders' first-pass bar is wrong, not the critics'), refuted-finding rate (currently ~2.5%; if it climbs, critics are pattern-matching instead of verifying), and time-from-FAIL-to-fix-dispatch (currently minutes; the gauntlet only works if rounds are cheap).

---

## Closed-ticket entries

**Process note — before writing a check, name who supplies each value it reads** (#21 core r7, 2026-08-01). Two defects this round, found by two independent critics, are one class: a rule over a value the *proposer* controls measures the proposer's cooperation, not the world. (1) The correction scan's window gate asked whether any uncited message sat after the **earliest** citation — the index the scan starts at — so citing one extra *earlier* message flipped a reading from `pending` to `auto_accept` while the scan read nothing after the sentence. `provenance` is model-supplied. (2) `proposal.type` **selected the acceptance rule that judged the proposal**: one body, one quote, one author, confidence 0.95, `pending` as a commitment and `auto_accept` as a claim; and the same sentence as an `open_question` cleared the lowest θ in the table at 0.65 where a claim sat in the band. A receipt certifies provenance — these words are in the record, this person wrote them — and it says nothing about what kind of act they were. Lesson kept: **for every value a check reads, write in the source who supplies it and why the check is still meaningful; a check whose input the subject controls is a check the subject can turn off.** Practical form, now a standing round-zero task on this ticket family: enumerate the proposal's own fields (`provenance`, `type`, `quote`, `statement`, `confidence`, ids, ordering) and grep every branch that reads one.

**Process note — the layer a refusal lives in decides which cells it eats** (#21 core r7, 2026-08-01). The adjudicated discharge for the type gap was "an object whose provenance is certified and whose type is not lands in the third severity (`refer`)", and the obvious implementation is a `refer`-severity receipt problem. It was built and measured: **78 of 1027 tests fell, and the shape of the fall refused it** — a `refer` outranks θ, so a claim at 0.4999, which the table says to *discard*, came back `pending`, and the whole θ band came back `receipt_not_certifiable`. That does not stage uncertified readings for a person; it empties the discard cell into Needs-you and makes the θ table unreachable for the type. Moved to the acceptance row where the type actually selects the rule, the same closure cost 27 tests and moved exactly one cell. Lesson kept: **when a fix is specified as a severity, measure what else that severity outranks before shipping it** — "refer instead of accept" and "refer instead of discard" are the same word and opposite products, and only running it tells you which one you built.

**Process note — a defence can be deleted by being shadowed** (#21 core r7, 2026-08-01). The same fix was first written as a named human-only gate on claim acceptance at the reducer, which reads better than a threshold. It ran *before* the verified-claim check, so a gate on every claim swallowed `claim_verification` whole: the matrix's own coverage test — "every gate was reached for by a model somewhere in the corpus" — went red and named it. The gate was reverted and the floor now refuses in words instead. Lesson kept: **adding a broader check upstream of a narrower one deletes the narrower one without touching its code**, and the only thing that notices is a test asserting every guard is still reachable. Keep those tests; they are the ones that look redundant.

**Process note — two refusals in one round make each other's tests vacuous** (#21 core r7, 2026-08-01). The strongest evidence this campaign has produced for the mutation ledger's strictest mode, and it caught the person extending the ledger. r7 landed two refusals hours apart: a window gate, then a rule refusing readings whose words are an undertaking as easily as an assertion. The second one's marker fires on `will` — and the first one's fixture read *"We will deploy production Friday afternoon as planned."* So the reducer refused that proposal for its **type** whether or not the window gate existed, and the assertion `state.objects === {}` held for the wrong reason. Three tests stopped discriminating without anyone editing them: the round's own D1 test, a tokenizer test three rounds older whose rows carried the same sentence, and a test that covered *two* enforcement points and therefore measured whichever one was left. **A plain "did the suite go red" reading scored the tree 114/114 and was wrong three times.** Wrong-test mode — a catch requires the tests the ledger *names* to fail, built in round 3 after a mutation earned a tick because the suite failed to load — reported all three, twice, identically. Two lessons kept: **re-run wrong-test mode at the end of any round that lands more than one refusal**, because the vacuity does not exist until both are present and a check after the first cannot see it; and **choose fixtures minimal against the rule they pin rather than realistic** — realism is exactly what let a second rule claim the first one's tests, and every one of these fixtures was a plausible sentence carrying more than its rule needed.

**Process note — measure what a severity outranks before shipping it** (#21 core r7, 2026-08-01). The adjudicated discharge for a defect was "an object whose provenance is certified and whose type is not lands in the third severity (`refer`)", and the obvious implementation is a `refer`-severity receipt problem. Built and measured: 78 of 1027 tests fell, and the *shape* of the fall refused it — a `refer` outranks θ, so a reading at 0.4999 that the table says to **discard** came back `pending`, and the whole θ band came back `receipt_not_certifiable`. That does not stage uncertified readings for a person; it empties the discard cell into Needs-you. Moved to the row where the value actually selects the rule, the same closure cost 27 tests and moved one cell. A second draft then refused the whole *type* rather than the *text*, which deleted the auto-accept path the product is built on — measured on a plain declarative genuinely in the record, quoted verbatim. The third asks "could these words be an undertaking" and separates the honest claim from the laundered commitment. Lesson kept: **"refer instead of accept" and "refer instead of discard" are the same word and opposite products** — when a fix is specified as a severity, run it and read which cells moved, because only the measurement tells you which one you built. Corollary that saved this round: a predicate that refuses a whole *category* to catch a subset of it is almost always too coarse, and the case that makes the rule hard — here, text genuinely ambiguous between a claim and a commitment — is the one to design against.

**Process note — a green test can be green because of the defect** (#21 core r7, 2026-08-01). The window-gate defect had a test sitting on it since round 1: `escalation.test.ts`'s *"does not let a padded citation list flip attribution"* built a `[cited, uncited, cited]` window and asserted the receipt came back clean. It came back clean **because** the gate read the earliest citation; with the gate fixed, that window is correctly referred. Six review rounds read that file. Lesson kept: **a passing assertion is evidence about the code only if you know which branch made it pass** — when a fix turns an existing green red, the first question is whether the old green was ever measuring what its title claims.

**Process note — an undeclared timeout is a load-dependent gate** (#21 core r7, 2026-08-01). Three of this package's tests brute-force all 1,112,064 non-surrogate code points and a fourth sweeps 28,561 pairs; nothing declared `testTimeout`, so all four ran against vitest's 5000 ms default. The blind critic measured one at **5277 ms on an idle machine** and got exit codes 1, 1, 0, 1, 0, 0 from six consecutive `pnpm test` runs — and the two that flipped were the two carrying the round's entire evidentiary claim. The first green was luck. Repaired by sharding each sweep (seventeen Unicode planes; thirteen alphabet entries) with a companion test that adds the shards back up and fails if one did not run, plus a declared budget with the measurement written beside it. Lesson kept: **a suite with no declared timeout has a hidden pass/fail threshold set by the machine it happens to run on** — and the tests most likely to sit near it are the exhaustive ones a project cites as proof.

**Process note — freeze the tree before handing it to a critic** (#21 core r5, 2026-08-01). grok produced no verdict twice in this round, and both times the cause was mine rather than its sandbox. Its own transcripts name it — *"Source and `dist` diverge"*, then *"Src and dist disagree on `QUESTION_MARKS`"* — because I was applying the first critic's findings while the second critic was reading the tree. Its read-only sandbox also blocks vitest from writing a temp config, so it falls back to the built `dist`, which is exactly the artifact that goes stale when `src` moves under it. The campaign's standing rule was already "prepare the critic's tree (install + build)"; it needs a second half. Lesson kept: **commit, build, and then do not touch the tree until every critic has returned** — a moving target produces a verdictless run that looks like a tooling failure and is actually an orchestration failure, and three of grok's four verdictless runs on this campaign now have a diagnosed cause rather than a shrug.

**Process note — re-run the critic on what it made you change** (#21 core r5, 2026-08-01). The second blind pass, run against the fixes the first pass had produced, found four more defects — and one of them was a **comment this round had written to justify a fold**, which is the most expensive kind of comment to have. It claimed deleting a Unicode format character "cannot map two texts a reader sees as *different* onto one — a bidi override that reverses a sentence is deleted, and the reversed bytes stay reversed, so the comparison still refuses." The reviewer built the counterexample in one line: a message body of `Bob will \u202Eton\u202C deploy production Friday.` **renders as** *"Bob will not deploy production Friday."*, deleting the override normalized the source to `ton`, a quote of `ton` matched it, and the record minted the affirmative of what its author visibly wrote. The same pass found the new later-correction scan could be skipped by **padding the citation list** (it started after the *last* cited message, so citing the bearing message plus an unrelated later one hid the correction behind the scan's start — r1's attribution attack, one guard over), plus two omissions in a character set that the *first* pass had made me write. Lesson kept: **a fix round's own fixes are a new artifact and get their own adversarial pass — and a justification written in the same commit as the code it justifies has had no independent reader at all.** One pass would have shipped a receipt whose defence of its own normalisation was false.

**Process note — audit every documented limitation for what the code does with an input inside it** (#21 core r5, 2026-08-01). Round 4's blind review returned eight defects, and **two of the three worst were behaviours round 4 had already written into its own prose as deliberate**: `escalation.ts` explained that polarity in a neighbouring sentence ("I will deploy Friday. Not.") is invisible to any span rule, and `acceptance.ts` explained that the θ_min discard sits above the receipt check "on purpose". Both rationales were honest and mostly correct. The code still auto-accepted the negated sentence and still discarded the inverted receipt — while the same round had built the exact third severity (`refer`) both cases needed. Lesson kept: **a limitation stated in prose is not a disposition; the program still does something with every input that lands inside it, and "auto-accept" or "silently discard" is almost never the right something.** Practical form, now a standing round-zero task on this ticket family: before reading the defect list, sweep the package for every sentence of the form "this cannot see X" and answer, for each, what happens to an input inside it.

Run as a sweep it paid twice over. It found a **ninth defect nobody had listed**: `findDuplicate` scored similarity over `contentTokens`, whose stopword table `escalation.ts` documents in bold as never again deciding whether a reading becomes a fact — and `not`, `all` and `some` are on that list, so a reading contradicting an accepted object drawn from the same message scored 1.0 and was **discarded**, leaving what it contradicted standing. That is r3's gauntlet finding surviving in the one path nobody re-read, and worse there than where it was found: not referred, not refused, destroyed. It then found a **tenth in this round's own first draft** — the new later-correction scan stopped at its cap and returned "nothing found", defended by a comment arguing a miss was the safe direction. Same shape, same round, written by the fix. The cap fails closed now.

**Process note — enumerate the compliant forms, third and fourth arrival in one day** (#21 core r5, 2026-08-01). Two more instances of the denylist lesson, both closed by inversion. (1) *Where the scissors may cut.* r4 required a quote to be whole sentences; the residue was the neighbouring sentence. Four candidate allowlists were tried and each left the model holding the scissors — a length floor breaks on "Unless CI is red.", a terminator rule on "Not.", a reach-the-end rule on "Hypothetically.", and "must not open with a linking word" is a denylist of linking words wearing an allowlist's clothes. The only form with no scissors left in it is **the quote is the whole of what its author wrote**, and everything short of that is referred. (2) *Normalisation.* One function had been serving routing and the receipt with a single lossy fold, deleting backticks (a displayed sample became an assertion), collapsing link destinations (a statement naming the safe URL certified against a record linking elsewhere — a security defect, not a fidelity one) and applying NFKC (distinct hostnames compared equal). It is now an allowlist of five differences a quote may have, each with the argument that admits it, and two candidates were **refused entry with their counterexamples recorded**: case folding (`US`/`us`) and underscore emphasis (`__init__`). `packages/ingest` had already answered the same question the other way for message bodies; the core had re-introduced lossy normalisation at match time under a different name. Lesson kept: **when the same question is answered in two layers, check that they agree — a policy written down in one package does not travel to the one that consumes it.**

**Process note — the builder's own adversarial pass broke the builder's own fix, again** (#21 core r5, 2026-08-01). The mandatory self-review found the gap in the round's brand-new later-correction check: *"We will deploy production Friday."* followed by *"Correction: the deployment is cancelled."* shares not one content token with the statement (`deploy` and `deployment` are different tokens), aligns with nothing, and auto-accepted. The structural test only sees a correction that *reuses* the sentence. Fixed with the one marker list in the package that acceptance reads — admissible precisely because of the direction it can be wrong in: a hit adds a referral and can never add an acceptance. Lesson kept: **a check justified by "this direction of error is free" is a check whose direction of error has to be written down and defended, not assumed** — and it is the only condition under which a heuristic word list belongs anywhere near an acceptance path.

**Process note — the gate was red and three review rounds did not notice** (#21 core r5, 2026-08-01). `fix/core-engine-r4` does not build: `pnpm typecheck` and `pnpm build` fail on it as committed, on two type errors. The round-4 receipt claimed a green gate. Nobody caught it because the critic could not run anything (no `node_modules` in its sandbox) and said so, and the claim was taken on the builder's word. Lesson kept: **when a critic reports it could not execute the suite, the gate claim is unverified for that round and the orchestrator must re-run it, not just note the caveat** — and preparing the critic's tree (install + build) is a precondition for the review, not a nicety.

**#20 Build: replay ingest** (closed 2026-08-01, 2 rounds). r1 (codex) caught the silent-truncation class: pagination could write a valid-shaped partial corpus; r2 added four throw paths + whole-fetch dedup + per-parent-comment count reconciliation, and proved its new tests against staged round-1 code (which silently dropped 3 of 4 replies in the fixture). r2 delta (grok, rotated lens) passed with zero blocking. Findings refuted: 0; findings re-scoped: 1 (the "verbatim any path" claim was scoped to API bodies — the markdown converter is a parser and normalizes as part of tokenization). Mid-build discovery: GitHub issues carry no reply threading — demo corpus swapped to a threaded discussion via amendment on closed #2. Lesson kept: **absolute claims ("any path", "every secret") attract correct refutations — scope claims to exactly what is guaranteed, in the artifact itself.**

**#19 Build: repo scaffold** (closed 2026-08-01, 4 rounds — metric threshold hit; adjudicated as convergence, not churn: shell → trust boundary → ordering contract → consumption semantics, each round strictly narrower, both lineages used). Highlights: r2 refuted one critic finding with live-container evidence (MinIO ships `mc`); r3's builder proved the spec's own two requirements inconsistent and built to the goal (global cursor over per-room watermark); r4's builder found r3's property-test corpus provably never fired rarer gates under full shuffle (coverage measured, not assumed) and learned empirically that Node's `loadEnvFile` never overwrites set variables. Residuals routed to #21/#22, nothing dropped. Lessons kept: **when a spec's requirements conflict, the builder who proves the conflict and builds to the goal beats the one who implements the letter**; and **property-test corpora need coverage assertions on their own outcome classes, or the rarer branches silently go untested.**

**Process note — "safe under the dimension measured" is not "not a defect", and I published the conversion** (#10 prototype r19, 2026-08-02). A blind critic measured whether a positional claim — *"the parity run below"* — could be falsified by **scrolling**, found it could not, and reported it safe. I converted that into a **refutation** on the ticket, and then wrote *"Do not fix it"* into the next round's brief. Round 19 disproved it by hand in three clicks: expanding a collapsed group changes what is in the feed, the cited row leaves the viewport, and the only visible instance renders **19 to 139 pixels above** the sentence saying it is below, at five widths in both themes. The scroll figure I had repeated did not reproduce either. Lesson kept: **a measurement bounds the dimension it varied and says nothing about the others — so record a refutation as "not reachable by X", never as "not a defect", and name X.** The class here was *positional claims going false as the page changes*; scrolling is one way a page changes and expanding a group is another. Also worth copying: the round earned the right to override the instruction by **building the sentence a measured declaration channel first, specifically so it could survive** — the channel then reported the sentence 117 times — and it flagged the override explicitly rather than acting quietly.

**Process note — a repro with a fixed route cannot keep a class pruned** (#10 prototype r19, 2026-08-02). Round 17 fixed a positional claim and pinned it with a repro that drives one route and asserts on one surface. The sentence came back through the **synthesized branch of the same function**, four clicks from boot, in the exact surface round 17 had fixed — and **round 17's own assertion, run on that state, reports it verbatim.** The guard was correct and its route was one path through the code it guarded. Lesson kept: **a repro proves a path; only a render-time or type-level rule proves a class — so when a fix round closes a class, ask whether the check it leaves behind is anchored to a route or to the rendering.** Round 19's replacement is flat and exception-free at the render, and it states its own ceiling honestly: it is a closed word list, so a paraphrase ("two panels left", "the line you just read") walks past it. Second thing worth copying from the same round: it kept a count phrase **contiguous on purpose** because two older repros read that exact shape, noting that sliding a word between the numerals *"would have turned two live repros into checks that match nothing and pass in silence"* — the failure this campaign has now recorded three times, avoided in advance by someone thinking about the instruments while editing prose.

**Process note — a surface that holds a count and invents its symbol is the shape; the literal is the symptom** (#39 UI r10, 2026-08-02). I briefed *"a hand-written glyph should not typecheck"*, and the round told me plainly that is not achievable — `<span>◆</span>` is a `string` child on a `span` and no type system stops it. What it did instead is the right diagnosis: the rail's `owed` was a **`number`**, so the rail *could not* have derived a glyph, which is precisely why a literal was sitting there. Making it a branded tagged union carrying the count **and the hardest item's state** means there is no number to draw a glyph beside. Lesson kept: **when a value is hand-written, ask what the surface was *given* — a literal is usually the only thing a component could have done with the data it had, and the durable fix is upstream in the type rather than downstream in a ban.** The character sweep still exists and caught 12 literals in 8 files on the prior branch, but it is a net under the fix, not the fix. And the round was right to refuse my framing rather than let a commit message imply tsc was enforcing something a test enforces.

**Process note — a guard that would report thirty honest cases gets turned off, so its scope is a design input** (#39 UI r10, 2026-08-02). The round banned four of the seven glyph characters from every module but the vocabulary, with **no exemption list** — and stopped there, because `·` is simultaneously the seventh glyph and the corpus's universal separator (`list()` joins with it, `initials()` falls back to `'··'`) and `?` ends questions. Its reasoning: *"a blanket ban on the seven would report thirty honest separators and get turned off within a round."* That is correct, and the important move is what it did with the leftover — it recorded the gap in the file as a property of the **vocabulary**, not of the sweep: `·` was a poor choice for "routine" in a UI whose separator is `·`. Lesson kept: **a rule's achievable scope is evidence about the design it is checking. When a guard cannot cover a case without drowning in false positives, the case is usually where two systems collide, and the honest output is a ticket against the collision rather than an exemption list.** An exemption list would have looked like coverage and decayed silently; a stated four-of-seven with the reason is auditable and became a design question.

**Process note — anchor a guard to the one text the adversary did not write, and reject the fixes that would refuse honest input** (#21 core r12, 2026-08-02). Handed a defect where two structure guards compared a caller-supplied `quote` against a caller-supplied `statement`, I offered two repairs: give the guards a third argument, or make the receipt fold stop erasing structure. The round took neither. It changed **what the first argument is** — from the quote to the cited message's own body, the only text in that function an author actually wrote — with an empty-string fallback so a missing bearing message reports every structure rather than skipping. And its reason for refusing my second option is the part I had not thought of: **a client that re-wraps a message produces a quote with different line breaks, which is exactly the case the fold's whitespace entry exists to admit** — a structure-sensitive quote/body comparison would have refused honest quotes. Lesson kept: **when a guard's operand is attacker-controlled, the repair is to re-anchor it, not to tighten the comparison — and before tightening any normalisation, name the honest input that normalisation was admitting.** A fold entry is usually load-bearing for a real client behaviour, and removing it trades a forgery for a false refusal, which is the worse failure in a system whose refusals send readings to people.

**Process note — the fix you propose can be wrong even when the defect is real** (#21 core r12, 2026-08-02). On a second defect — a signal-driven producer resolving one user's item on another user's signal — I wrote in the brief that *"the examination must carry the user, exactly as the item id does."* The round refused it with an exact argument: **every signal handed in raises an item**, so a user-scoped declaration could only ever name a subject already in the computed set, which the resolve rule never reaches. Scoping the key would have decided nothing. The correct fix was for the producer to declare **no** examination at all — matching a sibling producer in the same file, whose comment I had *quoted in my own brief* without following it to its conclusion. Lesson kept: **state the defect precisely and the remedy tentatively; a brief that specifies the fix converts the builder from a designer into an implementer of your first idea.** Where I have written a remedy, mark it as a hypothesis and say what would falsify it — this round, and the two before it, each produced a better answer than the one I supplied, and in every case the evidence was already in the file.

**Process note — a test asserting the defect is the strongest form of the artifact-states-the-error pattern** (#22 realtime r11, 2026-08-02). Five times in two days a correctness artifact has sat beside the defect it describes: a docblock one line above a fresh instance; a file answering one question twice in opposite directions; an authority matrix cell marked *allowed*; a correctly-scoped comment the reader over-generalises. This is the fifth and the sharpest, because it executes. `corrections.test.ts` held a case named **"is confirmed the moment a person corrects it"** in which a human rewords a model-accepted claim standing in somebody else's name, and asserts `epistemicGlyph(record)` is **`✓`**. I verified the mechanism: `epistemic.ts:36` is `isHuman(acceptedBy) || humanTouchedAt !== null`, and `reduce.ts:882` sets `humanTouchedAt` on *any* human correction. So the ratification path was pinned as a feature, in green, in the file the round was about. Lesson kept: **when hunting a class, read the tests for it as claims rather than as coverage — a passing assertion is evidence the code does what the test says and never that what the test says is right.** Practical form: for each test touching the subject, restate its `expect` as an English sentence and ask whether you would sign it. Four tests in that file turned out to have a non-named party rewording somebody else's sentence — *the defect written as a fixture* — and three were incidental while one was load-bearing.

**Process note — when a fix round overturns your reasoning, take the substitution** (#22 realtime r11, 2026-08-02). I overturned a blind critic's SHIP and gave three reasons. The fix round sustained the verdict and demolished two of them: the forged `✓` I called aggravating is, after the fix, a **legitimate third-party judgement over the victim's own words**, and gating it as I hinted would have *inverted* the invariant it was meant to protect; and "the current-state table is what people read" is true but does not distinguish the case from any honest projection of an honest log, which was the critic's actual point. The ground that decides it was stated by neither of us: **third-party `amend`-of-text is not defacement, it is ratification** — corrections are human-only and set the bit that flips `~` to `✓`, so rewording somebody's reading does not fix an attribution, it confirms one, and confirms whatever you just typed. Lesson kept: **being right about the verdict is not being right about the reason, and the reason is what the next round inherits.** A brief that ships a weak justification propagates it, because the builder scopes the fix to the argument rather than to the defect. So: state the reasoning in the brief precisely so it can be attacked, invite the attack explicitly, and when a better ground comes back, replace yours in the receipt rather than appending it.

**Process note — an infrastructure failure takes every agent at once, so uncommitted work is a correlated risk** (campaign infrastructure, 2026-08-02). Three fix rounds died inside one minute — two on `ENOTIMP` from the API, one on a 600s stall the watchdog could not recover. Between them they were carrying **48KB, 40KB and 32KB of uncommitted diff**, including a new untracked test file that was the whole point of its round. Nothing was lost, but only because the worktrees survived; had cleanup run, an hour of work in three lanes would have gone with them. The failure was not three independent events with independent probability — it was one event, and every agent in flight was exposed to it simultaneously. Lesson kept: **tell builders to commit WIP early and often, and treat "how much uncommitted work is in flight right now" as a number worth knowing.** An agent naturally batches its commit until the work is coherent, which is right for history and wrong for durability; the resolution is a WIP commit that costs nothing and can be squashed. Orchestrator's own move when it happens: **back the work up before touching anything else** — `git diff HEAD` to a file plus a copy of every untracked file, outside the worktree — then resume from transcript rather than re-dispatching, because a resumed agent keeps the context that produced the work and a fresh one re-derives it. Watch the filenames: branch names contain `/`, and the first backup attempt silently wrote nothing because the target directories did not exist.

**Process note — a guard that compares two caller-supplied fields proves nothing** (#21 core r11 exit review, 2026-08-02). Two consecutive rounds built guards against a machine reading that adds markup its author never wrote. Both ask *does the statement carry structure the **quote** does not?* — and the quote is a proposal field the proposer writes. So the attack is to put the forged markup in **both**: the multiset difference cancels and the receipt comes back clean. Measured separation was total — with an honest quote both forgeries refuse, with the proposer's own quote both pass, and a 250k fuzz found **363 distinct fabricated markers clean under a forged quote and all 363 refused under an honest one.** The reason validating the quote did not help is the part worth keeping: the quote *is* checked against the body, twice, and both checks run `normalizeForReceipt`, which collapses whitespace runs and unfolds link punctuation. **The receipt's fold was built to ignore exactly the dimension the structure guards depend on** — two mechanisms, each correct alone, and the defect is the seam. Lesson kept: **when writing a guard, name the one piece of text the adversary did not write, and anchor to that. If both operands are caller-supplied, the comparison is a consistency check between two of the attacker's own choices.** And the second-order rule: if the guard's operand was itself validated, check *what fold the validation used* — a validation that erases the property you are guarding is worse than none, because it reads as coverage.

**Process note — the field nobody thinks of as an input is the adversary's degree of freedom** (#21 core r11 exit review, 2026-08-02). Two rounds built the structure guards, both wrote tests, and — as the critic put it — *"the suite does not contain a case where the quote carries structure the body does not; every structure test drives the statement against an honest quote."* Both authors supplied an honest quote because that is what you do when the thing under test is the statement. The quote was scenery in every test, and scenery is exactly where an attacker looks. Same shape as the mention defect in the same review: the item id is keyed per-user, and the examination declared one line below it is keyed per-object, because the user was not the subject anybody was thinking about. Lesson kept: **for every guard, list all its inputs and ask which one the test author treated as setup rather than as an input — then write the case where that one is hostile.** The cheap version is a rule about test data: any field a test fills in "correctly" without varying it is untested, and if a caller can set it, it is an attack surface.

**Process note — a correctly-scoped comment invites the over-generalisation, and the reader commits it** (#39 UI r9 exit review, 2026-08-02). A file's docblock reads *"a pin holding one ■ and eight ✗ headed by a hand-written ◆ is a claim dressed as a fact… Every aggregate glyph **below** is derived from the items it counts."* It is **true**: it scopes itself to its own file and holds there, and two tests guard it. Meanwhile the rail — a different file — prints a literal `◆` beside its owed count, over a set whose derived glyph is `■` or `✗`, and it is the only report of what a room owes you when you are not standing in it. I read that comment during the previous round and took it as a property of the application. Lesson kept: **this is the fourth instance in two days of the artifact that establishes correctness sitting beside the defect, and the first where the artifact is accurate — the error is entirely in the reading.** A scoped claim ("below", "in this module", "on this path") is *evidence about its scope and silence about everything else*, and silence reads as coverage. Practical guard: when a comment asserts a property, ask what the sentence's scope word is doing, then grep the property's negation across the whole tree rather than trusting the file that raised the subject. The negation here was one literal glyph character in JSX and would have taken one search.

**Process note — an instrument's blind spots are a question set, not a mechanism** (#39 UI r9 exit review, 2026-08-02). The previous round built a cross-surface agreement check — no selector list, derived from the rendered DOM, with its own denominator stated. A blind critic extracted it, ran it verbatim across five states, and got **0 contradictions, 0 unparsed, 0 unlabelled every time — with four rendered falsehoods on screen.** Its diagnosis is the keeper: *"the check does what it says — it is the enumeration of **questions** that is short, not the mechanism."* Concretely: a glyph is a character with no letter and no digit, so it is neither a form match nor a wordless count and never enters the comparison; three count-words had no entry in the question table; a filter's "match" is a background colour; and the room rule fires only when a room named on screen is *also* named elsewhere. Lesson kept: **when a checker returns clean against a defect you can see, do not assume the mechanism is broken — enumerate what it is asking about and check whether the defect's subject is on that list.** The two failures look identical from the outside and have opposite repairs: a broken mechanism needs debugging, a short question set needs one more row. And the cheap diagnostic is to state, next to every checker, the list of questions it asks — a checker whose *questions* are undocumented cannot be audited even by its author.

**Process note — a ticket that collects N instances of a class must be scoped as a class, not raided** (#10 prototype r18 exit review, 2026-08-02). Round 15's review found three positional claims that go false in some state — "the cutover decision above", "the parity run below", "the date it moved from stays above" — and I filed all three in one ticket as notes not affecting the verdict. Round 17's brief then named **one** of them as a defect, fixed it, and its receipt says in my own words *"#62 keeps the other two deictics."* Round 18's blind critic made one of the remaining two **the sole HOLD blocking the lane**, having measured that no width or theme renders the cited date above the sentence claiming it. I had the class enumerated, in one place, in my own handwriting, and pulled a single instance from it. Lesson kept: **a ticket holding N instances of one class is a class, and a fix round either takes all N or states why the rest are different — raiding it for one leaves the ticket looking triaged while the class stays open.** The tell I ignored: I wrote the sentence that split them, so the decision was visible and unargued. And a correction that came with it: of those three, one — *"the parity run below"* — is **not a defect at all**; it holds at every reachable scroll position because the pane scrolls only 155px. So the same ticket over-reported one instance and under-scoped another, which is what an unexamined collection of "notes" does.

**Process note — pruning removes facts, and the difference is whether you name where they went** (#10 prototype r18 exit review, 2026-08-02). The taste round cut a quarter of the words a reader meets after acting, and I dispatched a review with "did this remove a fact a cold reader needs?" as its first question. Answer: **no** — every cut fact was verified reachable on the row itself, surviving supersession and every filter. But the same review found an **earlier** prune that did exactly what I had feared: round 12 removed the clause `beyond the N above` from the pin trailer, and that clause was the only statement of which set the trailer's count was scoped to. The number now reads `6 of 10 → 6 of 11 → 7 of 12` as a person clears their queue, beside a tab that says `13` throughout, with nothing anywhere naming the difference. Lesson kept: **the difference between a safe prune and a lossy one is not restraint, it is bookkeeping — r18 named every removed clause and where its fact still lived, and cost nothing; round 12 removed a clause and cost a fact.** So the discipline that made the taste round safe is transferable and cheap: for every clause cut, name the fact it carried and where that fact is now, and if the answer is "nowhere", say so deliberately rather than discovering it six rounds later.

**Process note — a guard that quotes an invariant should be checked against the whole sentence it quotes** (#22 realtime r10 exit review, 2026-08-02). The correction gate refuses with a message that reads *"nobody gets committed, **or quoted**, by someone else's sentence (#4)"*. It computes `arriving = after \ before` over **name sets only** and never looks at the text field — so it enforces "committed" and not "quoted", while citing both. Driven: a member re-worded somebody else's self-owned commitment into a confession, retyped it to a claim (legal — the name was already there), and marked it verified, ending at `claim, claimant=Bob, verification=verified` with every character written by the attacker. Lesson kept: **when a refusal message states a rule, read the rule as a specification and check the code against every clause of it — a guard's own prose is the most precise available statement of what it is supposed to do, and it is written at the moment the author understood the requirement best.** The failure mode is subtle because the message is *true about the case in front of you*; it only overstates on the clause nobody drove. Practical form: grep the guard's own refusal strings for conjunctions — "or", "and", "as well as" — and test each side separately.

**Process note — severity is the orchestrator's call, and a critic's SHIP is a hypothesis too** (#22 realtime r10 exit review, 2026-08-02). A careful critic drove ~90 attribution attempts, found the defect above, reported it in full — and rated it **not a HOLD**, on the grounds that the record stays complete and attributable and the renderer that would make it look like the victim's own words does not exist yet. Good reasoning, well evidenced. I overturned it, because the exclusion *"no renderer yet"* is the same one I had used earlier in the campaign to downgrade a different defect that then turned out reachable with a divergence surviving restart, and because `accepted_objects` **is** the current-state table by definition. Lesson kept: **the standing rule that a finding is a hypothesis applies to a finding's severity rating, not only to its existence — including a rating of "not severe".** A blind critic is positioned to establish *what happens*; the orchestrator holds the context on what has already gone wrong when a similar exclusion was accepted. Both halves matter: overturning without stating the critic's reasoning and the counter-argument would convert a careful judgement into an unexplained override, so the receipt records both and the fix round is explicitly invited to say I was wrong.

**Process note — a fact from one lane's receipt is not a fact about another lane's artifact** (#10 prototype r18, 2026-08-02). Briefing the prune round, I listed things it must not remove and included *"the five-glyph legend stays — round 8 already litigated this and was right."* The prototype has **no legend at all**: zero legend surfaces in the file, and one of the seven glyphs (`✗`) is unreachable because the only verification value that emits it appears zero times in the seed. The legend I was protecting lives in the **UI lane**, `apps/web/src/components/model/glyph.ts`, where a different round-8 review had found it enumerating five of seven. I had two lanes' round-8 receipts in context, both about glyph vocabularies, and wrote a protection order for the wrong artifact. No harm resulted — the round protected the thing that actually functions as the key there, every glyph printed beside its word — but the instruction was false on its face. Lesson kept: **when a brief cites a prior finding, cite the artifact it was found in, and check the claim against the file you are briefing about rather than against memory of a sibling receipt.** Running many lanes with parallel round numbers and overlapping subject matter makes this failure cheap to commit and invisible to the builder, who has no way to know the fact came from somewhere else. Cheapest guard: any "X stays" instruction should be verifiable by one grep in the target file, and worth running before it ships.

**Process note — a mechanical rule keeps a prune pruned; it does not perform one** (#10 prototype r18, 2026-08-02). The taste round removed roughly a quarter of the words a reader meets after acting, and built a checker that refuses any run of words a feed row repeats from an earlier row narrating the same object in the same tick — enumerating no forbidden phrasing, so a clause reworded next round is caught by the same line. Good rule, and the round stated its own ceiling precisely: **it cannot see a restatement.** `statement recorded verbatim, not worded by lars` sitting above `these are not lars's words` shares no words with it, and no word-level rule can catch that. Those were found by three separate people reading the screen and independently saying the page nagged. Lesson kept: **there are defect classes whose only detector is a person with the job, and prose redundancy is one of them — the instrument's role is to hold the ground after a human takes it, not to take it.** This is the counterweight to the campaign's own bias: eighteen rounds of building instruments made the artifact truthful, and the thing three reviewers converged on unprompted was something no instrument had ever reported. Practical form: when a class is only reachable by reading, schedule a reading — and treat unprompted convergence between independent reviewers as the strongest evidence available, because it is the only evidence that class produces.

**Process note — the artifact that establishes correctness can contain the statement of the error** (#22 realtime r10, 2026-08-02). Hunting the routes by which a member could put an obligation on another member's name, a fix round found a fifth I had not listed — an acceptance citing **no proposal at all**, which is the self-staged shape with the second party *removed* rather than absent. It had escaped every round because the guard sat inside `if (proposalId !== null)`. What makes it worth recording is where the route was documented: **the authority matrix had a row for it, marked allowed.** `human accepts commitment via none` was a passing test cell asserting the defect. This is the third form of one pattern in two days — a docblock describing the defect one line above a fresh instance of it, a file answering one question twice in opposite directions, and now a test matrix whose cell *is* the bug. Lesson kept: **when hunting a class, read the artifacts that claim the class is handled — the matrix, the allowlist, the exhaustiveness table — and check each entry against the invariant rather than against its own header.** An enumeration asserting a case is fine is indistinguishable from an enumeration that has covered it, and only the invariant tells them apart. Corollary: a passing test is evidence that the code does what the test says, never that what the test says is right.

**Process note — count the answers to a question before fixing any of them** (#22 realtime r10, 2026-08-02). The brief said there were two answers to *"which payload fields name a person"* — a narrow resolver and a wider table — and asked for one. There were **three**: a third hand-written pair sat in the escalation path deciding whether a *model* reading must quote the person it names, and nothing connected it to either of the others. The fix derived all three from a table that classifies **every key of every payload** behind a mapped type with `-?`, so an optional field cannot escape and adding a field fails to compile until somebody classifies it. Lesson kept: **before consolidating duplicate answers, grep for the question rather than for the two answers you already know about.** The reliable search is the *predicate's shape* — here `type === 'claim' || type === 'commitment'` — not the identifier, because the third copy had a different name and lived in a different file. Wider form recorded across three lanes now: any question a codebase answers more than once will eventually be answered inconsistently, and the count of answers is almost always one higher than the count you can name.

**Process note — a measurement should name its operands, not copy them** (#10 prototype r17, 2026-08-02). The defect was `open 3h` on a question asked one minute earlier, minted from `{ read: "verification", post: " 3h" }` — a real record read with a literal riding along, which passes a point-read mutation test by construction. The repair is one design decision worth carrying everywhere: **`elapsed(o, "askedAt")`, never `elapsed("09:11", "09:12")`.** Handing a measurement the *name of the field* rather than its *value* is what makes it re-derivable — a checker can go back to the record on every paint, where a copied pair of strings only ever proves the subtraction was done correctly once, against operands the mint chose. The round's audit then found **five more sites of the same shape** (`" by lars"`, `" 09:07"`, `" by checks"`) — only one was false, but every one was the shape that made the false one invisible. The generalisation: **any parameter that lets literal text ride alongside a derived value is a hole in whatever instrument checks the derivation**, and the fix is not to police the literals but to remove the parameter — the replacement here is an allowlist of `read | none | label | also+join | when`, where `join` may hold no digits and `also` must name a field the object actually has. Same rule as everywhere else in this campaign: allowlist the compliant form, never denylist the violations.

**Process note — a new instrument's first finding is about the instrument, four for four** (#10 prototype r17, 2026-08-02). The duration checker's inaugural run reported **222 violations, every one spurious**: it flagged `retention is 90 days` as "a duration that measures nothing", which was true only because the string is not a measurement at all — it is a policy the record holds and the page reprints verbatim, and the quoted-span detector could not reach it because card wordings are nested objects rather than string fields. So one checker's short reach became another checker's false accusation. This is now **four consecutive rounds** on this artifact where a fresh instrument's first execution produced a false positive its author caught before reporting — a TypeScript parser run over `tsconfig.json`, an occlusion rule fooled by a `pointer-events:none` overlay, a repro that printed evidence contradicting its own headline, and this. Lesson kept, promoted from anecdote to rule: **budget a debugging pass for every new checker's first run, and verify its inaugural findings by hand before believing any of them.** The corollary is sharper: when instrument A's output feeds instrument B, B's false positives are evidence about **A's reach**, not about the code — the 222 findings were a correct report of a gap in a *different* checker.

**Process note — a fix proved against the reported route is not a fix of the class, and my receipt said otherwise** (#22 realtime r9 exit review, 2026-08-02). Round 9 closed a forged-commitment attack with a real live proof: the exact two commands that minted an obligation on another member's name now refuse, zero rows in `accepted_objects`. I read the proof, verified the mechanism in source, and wrote a receipt saying the round had **closed the class**. It had closed **one route**. A blind critic then reached the same end state three other ways — `reattribute` (whose own comment says *"the field each type puts a person's name in; reattribute moves this one"*, and which gates on `isHuman` and nothing else), a `decision.decidedBy` that the attribution resolver does not know about, and a `retype` from a type that names nobody — and found a fourth defect meaning even correctly-refused attempts are broadcast to every other participant and persist in their durable journals. Lesson kept: **"the class is closed" is a claim about every route to an end state, and a live proof only ever covers the route it drove. Before writing it, enumerate the verbs, fields and paths that can reach that state and say which ones were tested.** The cheap version costs one question — *what else changes this?* — and it is exactly the question `ATTRIBUTION_FIELD` would have answered, since it already listed all three fields and one of them had no guard. Second-order lesson, the reason this was caught at all: I nearly skipped this review because the fix looked good and the lane's receipt was already published. **Every lane re-reviewed this session came back HOLD, twice on work I had already called good.**

**Process note — "it needs a schema change" is a claim about a type nobody opened** (#21 core r10 → r11, 2026-08-02). A fix round documented that closing a defect would require adding a field to a stored type, reaching another lane's storage surface. I read that, agreed, wrote it into a ticket as the general answer, and scoped the next round to "say so plainly if the schema change is the only honest fix." It was not. The information was **already stored**: the type's `reason` field had been a discriminated union with one variant per producer since round 1 — introduced precisely so a rendered string could not be passed off as a rationale — so the producer was derivable from what was on disk via one total lookup, and the fix cost zero new fields. Both the earlier round and I had been **reading the union as opaque.** Lesson kept: **before accepting "this needs a schema change", open the type and ask what is already derivable from it.** A discriminated union is a stored fact about which case you are in, and treating it as a bag of rendering data throws that away. The tell that this was a reading error rather than a real limit: the union's own docblock said why it existed, and the reason it existed was exactly the distinction being claimed as missing. Corollary for orchestration: a builder's "this is blocked on another lane" is a hypothesis with the same standing as a critic's finding — verify it against the type before you ticket it as scheduled work, because a wrong one converts a one-round fix into cross-lane coordination.

**Process note — carry an example only as far as you have checked it** (#21 core r11, 2026-08-02). A reviewer gave five examples of markup being injected into a stored statement, and I passed all five into a fix round's brief as rendering claims. The round **rendered them** — through `marked` in GFM mode and `markdown-it` in commonmark mode — and two of the five do not build block structure in any renderer: CommonMark forbids an indented chunk from interrupting a paragraph, and GFM needs a delimiter row before a pipe is a table. The guard is right and refuses all five, but on the mechanical ground that the statement re-broke the author's lines and turned their next character into a line-initial marker — **not** on the rendering claim I had repeated. Lesson kept: **an example inherits the confidence of whoever last verified it, and relaying one without checking launders a hypothesis into a premise.** When a finding's argument is "this is how it renders", render it; when it is "this is what the bytes are", read the bytes — and say in the brief which of the two you actually did. The round also caught its own misreading of that measurement mid-flight (it had stripped newlines from the HTML for display and briefly concluded words were being fused) and reported the correction, which is the standard.

**Process note — a regression test inherits the narrowness of the reproduction it was handed** (#21 core r10 exit review, 2026-08-02). Round 10 closed a silent-resolution defect and pinned it with a test named *"a cycle that went blind does not resolve anybody's item"*. The test cites **one** message. Measured by the next critic: with one citation the item is preserved; with **two** it resolves, because a different problem kind fires when the window still holds some of the cited messages. **The repo's own canonical fixture cites two.** So the fix was real, the test was green, and the test sat exactly on the boundary where the fix works — because it was written from the reproduction the fix round was given, and that reproduction happened to use the narrow case. Lesson kept: **a repro is one point in the input space and a regression test written to match it inherits that narrowness. Before pinning, ask what the *typical* shape of this input is in the codebase's own fixtures — and if the test's shape is narrower than the fixture next door, widen the test.** Cheap general form: for any test whose subject is a collection (citations, members, attachments, roles), write it at n=2 unless n=1 is the thing under test; the singleton case silently skips whole branches.

**Process note — independent convergence between a builder's disclosure and a blind critic outranks both** (#21 core r10, 2026-08-02). A fix round documented a residue it had chosen not to close, and I ticketed it as scheduled work rather than a defect. A blind critic with no tracker access, told to write its own probes, then found the same thing and rated it a real defect — a live item, owed to a real person, marked done with nobody acting. That agreement moved it from "scheduled" into the next round's scope. Lesson kept: **a builder's honest disclosure and an independent critic's finding are not the same claim twice — the builder is saying "I know about this", the critic is saying "this harms someone", and only the second is a severity judgement.** When both land on one thing, the builder's framing as a known limit is the weaker of the two: this repo's own standing rule is that **a stated limit is not a disposition.** Practical form: when triaging a round's "deliberately not fixed" list, mark each item for whether a critic has independently reached it; convergence promotes it out of the backlog regardless of how well the builder explained it.

**Process note — a demonstration written against a type that cannot express the distinction demonstrates something else** (#21 core r9 review → r10, 2026-08-02). A blind reviewer produced a table of dedup rows showing readings destroyed by a comparison that could not see who a sentence was about. I verified the mechanism, verified the type, published the table in a receipt — and the fix round then read the reviewer's scripts and found **two rows do not test what their labels say**: the retracted-object row built its reference with an empty override, byte-identical to the plain-duplicate row above it, because *the type had no field to mark retraction with*; and the different-claimant row passed the same claimant on both sides. Both underlying defects were real and reproduced end to end — but that script demonstrated neither. Lesson kept: **when the finding is "the type cannot express X", every demonstration of X written through that type is suspect, including the reviewer's — the defect and the evidence share one root.** I should have caught it at the moment I was scoping a fix whose whole premise was *the type has nowhere to express this*, and instead I republished the rows as evidence. Practical rule: re-derive a reviewer's demonstration through a path the defect does not pass through, or accept it as a hypothesis with the mechanism as the only confirmed part. The round's own phrasing is the keeper — *"the reviewer could not have written those rows correctly; that is the finding, which is why the fix had to be a type."*

**Process note — a hand-written mutation ledger rots, and the rot reads as escape** (#21 core r10, 2026-08-02). A full run of the 152-row ledger found **eight mutants whose `find` text had drifted out of the code entirely**, plus **two whose *replacement* had drifted into a silent no-op** — one mutated only one side of a two-sided comparison, one passed a string where a payload now goes. Both no-ops showed as ESCAPED, which is the honest direction, but only if somebody runs the whole ledger and reads the result. Nine rounds of receipts quoted "N of N caught" from a `RESULTS.md` that was itself stale (it claimed 139 while the ledger had grown to 144). Lesson kept: **a mutation ledger is source that no compiler checks — its `find` strings are references into a moving codebase and they decay silently.** Require a full run, not a `--only` run, whenever the ledger is quoted; treat a stale `RESULTS.md` as an unverified claim; and prefer mutations anchored on something the type system can see over free-text `find`/`replace`. The strongest available signal that a ledger is current is that its own escapes are zero **on a run somebody executed this round**, not on the number written in the file.

**Process note — mutation proves the read is used, not that the sentence came from reads** (#10 prototype r16 exit review, 2026-08-02). This campaign's strongest instrument is the point-read mutation: flip a query's answer and fail if the generated sentence does not change. It fired eight times against the code of the round that wrote it, and it is the basis of the auto-memory rule *use is checked by mutation*. It was defeated by construction, at boot, on the most-triaged number on the page. A card rendered **`open 3h` for a question asked one minute earlier**, from `{ read: "verification", post: " 3h" }` — a genuine record read with an invented literal riding along. Flip `verification` and the sentence moves, so the read is provably used; the false half never budges and nothing asks where it came from. The coverage artifact then filed the string as `form:duration`, "a declared non-count shape", so the instrument confirmed a duration *looks like* a duration while nothing compared it to the timestamp it was a duration **from**. Sixteen rounds, four instruments, five surfaces, zero clicks. Lesson kept: **the mutation obligation has a partner — for each rendered sentence, account for every span in it.** Ask what produced each fragment, not whether the sentence responds to its input. A concatenation of one derived value and one constant is the shape that survives, and the tell is any formatter carrying a `pre`/`post`/`prefix`/`suffix` parameter beside a real field name: that parameter is where an unfalsifiable claim hides. Wider form, and the campaign's own thesis turned on itself: a duration and its origin timestamp are two things, and no single-value check holds the relation between them.

**Process note — a fix reopens its neighbours, and the old rounds' repros are what catch it** (#10 prototype r16, 2026-08-02). Round 16 removed an overlay by giving the row a real grid track. That change silently re-opened **round 15's D3** — a verification input falling back under the width its own question needs at 1160 and 1120 — because the input's element had been sized against a column that no longer meant the same thing. It was caught by *r15's repro*, not by the builder, not by review, and not by the new occlusion rule the round was busy writing. Lesson kept: **a layout fix changes what every sibling is measured against, so the regression surface of a "nine CSS declarations" change is every prior round's finding, not the two defects in scope.** Practical form: keep every round's repro executable and run the whole set against the fix, not just the repros for the defect being fixed. The cheapest possible version of this — a suite of 24 assertions that each fire on the branch they were found on — is what turned a reopened defect into a caught one, and it costs one command.

**Process note — a new checker's first run is where it invents defects, and the author is the one who must catch it** (#10 prototype r16, 2026-08-02). The round's occlusion rule reported, on its first execution, that a message row was covering the toast's own words. It was false: the toast is `z-index: 40; pointer-events: none`, so it paints *above* the feed and `elementFromPoint` looks straight through it — the hit test nominated the wrong element and the rule believed it. Fixed by separating the two jobs: `elementFromPoint` may only **nominate**, and a `paintsAbove()` comparison **decides**. Second time in two days a fresh instrument's first run produced a false positive that its author caught before reporting; the earlier one ran a TypeScript parser over `tsconfig.json` and reported `';' expected` for valid JSON. Lesson kept: **the first thing a new checker finds is evidence about the checker, not about the code — verify the inaugural finding by hand before you believe any of them.** And the design rule behind the fix generalizes: when a cheap oracle stands in for an expensive one, let it *propose* candidates and make a separate, honest comparison *decide* — a hit test that resolves real paint order is worth having, and it is not the same question as "what is drawn on top here".

**Process note — an invariant asserted on one branch of a dispatch does not constrain the others** (#21 core r9 exit review, 2026-08-02). Round 9 closed a recurring silence by making one path total: every proposal whose verdict is `needs_you` must produce either an attention item or a refusal, enforced by a type whose affirmative arm is a non-empty tuple. It works — a blind 28-cell sweep over type × payload × proposer kind × roster found zero silent cases. The very next review found the same silence walking in through `discard / provenance_failed`, a **different verdict class that never reaches the invariant**, and a downstream rule reading the resulting absence as completion. Lesson kept: **a totality invariant is scoped to the branch you attached it to, and the dispatch it sits inside is the enumeration to check.** When you make one arm of a union total, list the other arms and say for each what it guarantees — otherwise the defect relocates rather than closing, and it relocates into the arm nobody wrote a rule for. Corollary that made this one land: the downstream consumer treated *absence* as a value, so the fix has to reach both the producer that went quiet and the consumer that interpreted quiet as done.

**Process note — the second denominator, on the field axis** (#21 core r9 exit review, 2026-08-02). The auth lane's looping ended when someone noticed a guard has two enumerations — shapes understood, and inputs looked at — and six rounds had audited only the first. The same finding arrived in the core lane on a different axis: `findDuplicate` had its *text* comparison narrowed twice, each time with a good argument, and the question never asked was **which fields the comparison consults at all.** `AcceptedObjectRef` is `{objectId, type, text, messageIds}` — no `owner`, no `claimant`, no `verification`, no retraction state — so two commitments drawn from one message naming two different people are the same object to it, and the second person's obligation is discarded with no proposal and no trace. The docblock argues the case away in its own justification, reasoning about two *decisions* from one message and never about two *commitments*. Lesson kept: **when a comparison keeps needing to be narrowed, stop narrowing it and ask what it can see. A predicate over a projection of the data is only as good as the projection, and a type with no field for a distinction cannot be fixed by any caller.** The tell across both lanes is identical — repeated rounds each refining the same axis, none asking what the axis leaves out.

**Process note — before pricing a relaxation, check whether the strict path can even produce the case it was written for** (#21 core r9, 2026-08-02). A dedup comparison folded case, on the argument that case cannot change what a sentence says. I scoped the fix as a trade — delete the fold, pay one extra staged proposal when somebody restates a measurement in different case — and told the round to argue whichever way it went. It went wider than I offered and produced the fact that makes it not a trade: `borne` requires statement ≡ quote, `quoteCoversOwnText` requires the quote to be a cited author's whole body, and the receipt normalizer does not fold case, so **an accepted object's text carries the exact case of a real message body.** Two readings of one message are therefore already byte-identical and dedup without any fold at all. A case difference between two statements means they came from two *different* bodies — which is the correction case. **The relaxation could never fire on what it was for; its entire live effect was the destruction it caused.** Lesson kept: when a guard is loosened "to admit a benign case", check whether the strict path upstream can still produce that case at all. Where it cannot, the loosening has no benefit term — only its failure mode — and the cost/benefit framing you brought to it is the thing to throw away. Generally: **a relaxation's benefit is a claim about reachable inputs, and reachable inputs are decided by everything upstream of it, not by the relaxation's own docblock.**

**Process note — a pinned test can be doing two jobs and only one of them is named** (#21 core r9, 2026-08-02). Deleting a deliberate pin looked like it would cost the coverage the pin asserted. It was doing two things: asserting the fold, and asserting *dedup is not dead code* — had the function degenerated to "never a duplicate" while an unrelated fix went in, its `.not.toBeNull()` was the only thing that would have caught it. The round kept the second job by rewriting the case as an exact re-proposal, so the coverage now survives the next change to what dedup forgives. In the same pass it found a **second** test passing on the wrong property: a case named *"the same sentence again, from the same message: a re-proposal"* compared `'The migration…'` against an accepted `'the migration…'` — it was passing on the fold, not on re-proposal, and would have gone green-to-green through the fix while silently losing what its name claims. Lesson kept: **before deleting or rewriting a test, ask what it catches, not what it asserts — and ask it of the whole test, because an assertion can be load-bearing for a property nobody wrote down.** The same question applied to the tests *around* a change finds the ones passing for the wrong reason; neither of these is visible in a diff, and both were found by asking for the mutation rather than the assertion.

**Process note — a docblock naming a hazard is evidence the author saw it, not evidence it is handled** (#39 UI r8, #21 core r8, #10 prototype r15 exit reviews, 2026-08-02). Three lanes, three independent reviewers, one day, same shape: **the code that causes the defect carries a comment that names the defect.** `railRooms` (`rooms.ts:317`) documents round 6's failure in the product's own terms — *"the rail marked one room and the head named another — the two sources of truth the whole product is about, disagreeing on screen"* — and the line immediately beneath it computes the owed badge from the unfiltered array, reproducing the class exactly. `findDuplicate`'s docblock says in bold *"a correction the room can never see is the failure this whole ticket exists to prevent"* four paragraphs above the `.toLowerCase()` that destroys one. The prototype's overlay comment measured a 1.4px clearance and predicted what would make it fire. Lesson kept: **grep for the prose, not just the code. A comment describing a failure mode is a marker that the author was thinking about it at the moment they wrote the line — which makes the adjacent code the highest-yield place in the file to look, not the safest.** Concretely: when a fix round closes a defect, search the surrounding docblock for the class it names and check every sibling field, call site and branch that comment governs. Round 7 fixed `current` by derivation and left `owed` beside it, in the same returned object, under the same comment. **Deriving one field of a record does not derive the record.**
