**Process note — every instrument compared a surface to the record; none compared a surface to its neighbour** (#39 UI r8 exit review, 2026-08-02). 830 unit tests, 82 e2e runs, typecheck and lint all green, with a **five-surface contradiction painted on screen**: the pin saying `NOTHING NEEDS YOU IN THIS ROOM — THAT IS A RESULT, NOT AN ABSENCE` forty pixels above a feed row tagged `◆ needs lars`, with the rail chip, lens header, objective group and trailer all agreeing with the feed and against the pin. Not one instrument was broken. Each checks a surface against the underlying record, and each was right about the surface it checked — the two registers diverged *after* the record, so a per-surface check cannot see it by construction. Lesson kept: **when a system paints one fact on N surfaces, the N-way agreement is a property no single-surface check can hold. Assert it directly: no two elements simultaneously on screen may give different answers to the same question.** The general form is the seam lesson at its widest — r14 found defects between two correct checks, r15 between two visible elements, and this is between N correct renderings — and the diagnostic is the same in all three: **ask what relation nobody is checking, not what predicate is missing.**

**Process note — a hazard filed against the wrong trigger is a hazard that will fire** (#10 prototype r15 exit review, 2026-08-02). Round 13 measured an overlay chip clearing a control beneath it by **1.4px**, could not reproduce a stolen click across 18 randomised sessions of 40 real mouse clicks at three widths plus a 12-width probe of all nine hit-test points, and closed the observation correctly — recording the mechanism in the source with the prediction *"a row-spacing change turns it into a repro."* No row-spacing change ever happened. Round 15 added a **new control** into the strip the overlay covers, and both halves fired at once: 42 of 60px of the new control's label painted over at every width, and a click aimed at its visible centre landing on the overlay and replying to the wrong message. Lesson kept: **when you file a near-miss with a condition attached, the condition names the change you happened to imagine — not the class of change that makes it fire. State the invariant that is currently holding by luck ("nothing else is drawn in this strip"), not the one edit you predict will break it.** The measured clearance is the tell: a number that small is not a safety margin, it is a coincidence with a unit.

**Process note — a complete pair can still be missing the third question** (#10 prototype r15 exit review, 2026-08-02). The page enforces a rule stated deliberately as a pair — *a control you can see must be reachable, and a control you cannot see must not be touchable* — and both halves genuinely hold on the defect that shipped, because **the occluding element is itself visible and itself reachable**. The legibility checker is also correct and also blind to it: `scrollWidth === clientWidth` on both elements, because nothing is clipped, it is painted over. Three instruments, none broken, one defect where a person's unverified claim is covered mid-sentence by the chip you hovered to read it. Lesson kept: **the completeness of a pair is an argument about the axis you chose, not about the space. Occlusion is a relation between two elements and every rule here was a predicate on one.** Practical form: when a rule is phrased as "a control that is X must be Y", ask what the rule says about two controls at once — the seam lesson from r14 (*defects live between two correct checks*) with the checks replaced by the things being checked.

**Process note — when one question is answered twice, check which answer sits on the destructive path** (#21 core r8 exit review, 2026-08-01). `matching.ts` answers "should this comparison fold case?" twice, five hundred lines apart, in opposite directions. The receipt refuses it in the file's own words — *"`US` / `us`, `Bill` / `bill`, `March` / `march` are different words… a model that re-cases a sentence has edited it."* The dedup embraces it — *"Case is the one difference that cannot change what a sentence says."* Both sentences are in the same file and cannot both be true, and eight rounds of review read past the contradiction because each rationale is persuasive where it sits. What resolves it is not which sentence is better written but **which direction each error points**: the receipt erring strict *refuses* a reading, which sends it to a person; the dedup erring forgiving *discards* one, which sends it nowhere. So a machine reading of `` `hunter2` `` was thrown away as a duplicate of the accepted `` `Hunter2` `` — no proposal, no issue, no trace, the stale password left standing as the room's record. Lesson kept: **find every place a system answers the same normalization question and sort them by what happens when they are wrong. The comparison whose failure mode is destruction must be the narrowest one in the system, regardless of how reasonable its local argument sounds.** The tell that this was reachable: the destructive comparison's docblock named the exact failure in bold four paragraphs above the line that caused it.

**Process note — a new fallthrough does not reach the early exits above it** (#21 core r8 exit review, 2026-08-01). r7 shipped a class of defect where an engine verdict of `needs_you` produced no panel item and no refusal — a promise painted on. r8 was shown one instance, `type_not_certified`, and fixed it correctly in shape: a generic fallthrough at the tail of the loop, so any type reaching `needs_you` raises something. It still ships two more instances, because the `commitment` and `decision` branches `continue` *above* the fallthrough that was added below them. The fix was structurally right and positionally wrong. Lesson kept: **when you close a "falls through to nothing" defect by adding a catch-all at the end, the surviving siblings are every early `continue`/`return`/`break` that precedes it — enumerate those, not the code after.** And the general form, which is the one worth carrying: a fallthrough is a claim about what reaches the end of a block, so it is only as true as the set of paths that get there. Third appearance of this defect; the round-9 brief converts it from a patch into an assertion at the loop's tail, because the two prior patches each fixed the instance they were shown.

**Process note — a property of the merged system is measurable on neither branch** (#22 realtime r8 / #26 auth r11 exit reviews, 2026-08-01). Two lanes disagreed about how long a revoked member keeps receiving anything. Both measured honestly at production defaults, on their own branch, and both were right about what they saw: auth measured **presence only, torn down at 13.8 s** by its sweep; realtime measured **full message bodies, unbounded**, because `broadcast` consults the subscription map and nothing re-reads membership. The content broadcast exists only on the realtime branch; the teardown exists only on the auth branch. So the shipped behaviour — **a ~15 s window in which a removed person reads the room's actual messages** — was visible from neither vantage point, and I published a wrong reconciliation ("merging the sweep does not close it") from the one branch I had checked. Lesson kept: **when two lanes report different values for one property, the first hypothesis is not that one of them is wrong — it is that the property belongs to the merged tree and neither branch instantiates it.** The diagnostic question is *which half of the mechanism does each branch have?*, and the ticket that owns the number must stay open until it is re-measured after the merge, because a branch-local measurement of a cross-branch property is not evidence about what ships. Companion to the seam lesson from the prototype lane: defects live between two correct checks, and this is the same defect one level up — between two correct measurements.

**Process note — sweep for the class, find the neighbours** (#26 auth r9, 2026-08-01). Asked to sweep the codebase for one pattern ("formats an unknown error before the bookkeeping a guarantee depends on"), the round fixed 11 sites beyond the two named — and the three most serious were not instances of the original pattern at all: a migration whose error formatting sat above `process.exitCode = 1`, so **a failed migration could exit 0 and boot the app against an unmigrated database**; an `unhandledRejection` handler where a throw is re-raised as uncaught, so the handler guaranteeing "exit and let compose restart us" could be argued out of it by the value it was reporting; and an auth path where the format sat above `return null`, the one place a database blip becomes "not authenticated". Lesson kept: **a class sweep is worth ordering even when you expect "none" — the pattern is a search key, and the neighbourhood it points at is where the unrelated serious bugs live.**

**Process note — a brand protects the shape, never the contents** (#39 UI r4, 2026-08-01, fourth address of one defect). The attribution guarantee was moved into a phantom-branded `Quotation` whose doc comment stated that spreading one value into another shape would not compile. TypeScript's object-spread type carries `unique symbol` keys through, so `{...genuineQuotation, actor: 'priya'}` keeps the brand, type-checks, and renders one person's name over another's sentence — and the same shape survives `JSON.parse` because the parser validates structure and never provenance. The render boundary passed because it re-derived the *body* and the forgery moved the *actor*. Lesson kept: **a nominal type proves where a value came from, not that its fields are consistent with each other — if two fields must agree (an actor and the message it came from), one of them has to be recomputed from the other at the point of use.** The defect's four addresses were the actor slot, the body slot, the factory, and the branded value itself; each round's guard governed what the previous round had moved.

**Process note — lexical overlap is not semantic support** (#21 core r3, 2026-08-01). The receipt rule bound a machine-minted claim to its evidence by requiring 60% of the assertion's content words to appear in the cited quote. `not` was on the stopword list, tokens were deduplicated, and the comparison was set containment — so a quote reading "Bob will **not** deploy production Friday" scored **100% support** for the commitment "Bob will deploy production Friday". Long, verbatim, correctly attributed, auto-accepted. Lesson kept: **a similarity measure used as an entailment check will accept the negation of the thing it validates** — polarity, quantifiers and modals are the content, not the noise, and word-overlap can never mean "this evidence supports this claim". Wider form: when a guard reduces meaning to a score, name what the score actually proves and refuse the cases it cannot judge, rather than letting a threshold stand in for judgement. This is the only defect in the campaign that would have written a false statement about a named person into the product's own record with every guard reporting success.

**Process note — doctrine written from one artifact must be swept across the others** (#10 r9 vs #39, 2026-08-01). `design/CONVENTIONS.md` gained two measured rules from the UI library's gauntlets — no alpha for inactive controls, and `--red3` rather than `--red2` at glyph sizes — each citing the exact contrast numbers that had been found there. The prototype then failed its own gauntlet on **byte-identical numbers**: a disabled surface tab at 2.49:1/2.43:1 and `✗` glyphs at 4.21:1. The doctrine was correct, published, and never applied backwards to the artifact that shares its token set. Lesson kept: **when a finding becomes a rule, sweep every existing artifact for the rule before writing it down — publishing doctrine creates the illusion that the class is closed, and the older artifact is the one nobody re-reads.** Practical form: a doctrine commit should name which artifacts were checked against the new rule, or it is a plan rather than a fix.

**Process note — the orchestrator reported lanes it had not dispatched** (2026-08-01, orchestrator error). For roughly fourteen hours I reported a live-lane count that included `fix/core-engine-r3` and `fix/ci-r6`. Both had been pushed and both were idle: I had written "noted for the delta reviewer" on #21 and never dispatched one, and had done the same on #28. Nothing was watching, because the staleness sweep designed to catch exactly this (#29 — "open tickets with no activity", "unmerged branches older than N days") is itself unbuilt. The error surfaced only because a process restart made me re-derive the board from the tracker instead of from memory. Lesson kept: **a status report assembled from memory is a claim like any other and decays the same way — derive the board from the tracker, and treat "I dispatched that" as unverified until the agent's own notification says so.** Practical form: after every dispatch, the next status must be read back from branch state and issue comments, never from the dispatch you remember making.

**Process note — a `pgrep` wait-loop matches its own command line, so it never exits and reports the work as unfinished** (tooling, 2026-08-01). `until ! pgrep -f "mutants/run.mjs"; do sleep …; done` never terminates: `pgrep -f` matches the full argv of the polling shell itself, which contains the pattern. The watched process had already finished and written `58 run, 58 caught`, and the loop reported STILL RUNNING indefinitely. Lesson kept: **a monitor whose pattern can match the monitor is measuring itself** — use `pgrep -f "pattern" | grep -v $$`, match on the executable rather than the argv, or drop polling entirely and wait on the process. This is the same family as every instrument defect found today (a selector matching nothing, a contrast probe comparing a colour to itself, a checker exempt via its own fixture, a witness testing callers instead of the callee): **the measuring apparatus is inside the system it measures, and that is the first place to look when a reading is surprising.** Related good practice from the same agent: it distinguished its own wait-loops from the work in its report, tore down what it had created, and left another session's processes alone.

**Process note — a builder may not take its evidence from the review that judges it** (#22 r7, 2026-08-01). A round finished with an honestly incomplete mutation tally (23 of 71 measured, the rest declared "unmeasured on this tree, not passing on it" in a banner). It then noticed that a *blind critic's* full `--check` was running over a byte-identical copy of its tree, and proposed to adopt those numbers and correct its tally upward — carefully, with the provenance stated. Refused. The instinct was right and aimed at the wrong axis: the issue is not attribution but **independence**. A verdict and the claim it assesses cannot cite one measurement; a byte-identical tree makes another run's numbers *applicable*, never *independent*. Lesson kept: **when a builder and its reviewer would share a source of evidence, the builder keeps the smaller honest number.** A true "48 unmeasured" is worth more than a larger figure with tangled provenance, and the next round needs to know it inherits an unmeasured set rather than a verified one.

**Process note — concurrent mutation runs must not share a database** (#22, 2026-08-01). Three runs were live against one Postgres on port 55445. A mutation run infers "caught" from a suite failing — so a failure caused by *another* run's state (schema left mutated, foreign fixtures, a migration applied mid-suite) is **indistinguishable from the mutation being caught**, and it inflates the count in the direction that looks like success. The result is also unreproducible after the fact. **Corrected and widened the same day**: the first bite was not a mutation count at all. The round then discovered its **integration gate had been run against port 55445 while another session's SQL mutants were altering that schema** — so a green suite had been taken against a mutated database, and a *pass* meant nothing. Re-taken on a private Postgres on 55447: 115 passed, three times. Lesson kept, in its wider form: **any run whose result is evidence — a gate, a suite, a mutation pass, a benchmark — needs exclusive ownership of every piece of mutable state it touches.** A shared database does not merely add noise; it makes both a green and a red unattributable, and the green is the dangerous one because nobody investigates it. Give each run its own database on its own port and assert nothing else is connected, or the result is not evidence in either direction.

**Process note — agents leak their infrastructure, and nobody reaps it** (orchestration, 2026-08-01). Lars flagged the machine feeling loaded. Measured: **load average 21.9 sustained on 4 cores** (5.5x oversubscribed), 25 of 31 GB used with 613 MB free, 48 Chrome processes, 14 vitest processes, 9 Postgres containers — and **one `next-server` holding 16.5 GB and 91% of a core for 2h33m**, whose cwd was `/tmp/atr10x`, the experiment copy of a critic that had finished hours earlier. Killing three orphaned dev servers took memory from 25 GB used to 7.7 GB, and 8.5 GB of stale review clones came back off disk.

Three failures, all mine:
- **I never set a concurrency cap.** Eight agents ran against four cores because eight lanes existed, not because the machine could take eight. **Cap concurrent agents by cores, not by lanes** — on a 4-core box that is two or three heavy reviewers, and a queued lane costs nothing.
- **I never reaped.** A completion notification is the moment to clean up, and I treated it purely as a signal to dispatch the next round. **Every agent completion is a reap point**: kill processes rooted in its temp dir, remove containers it created, delete its clone.
- **I never measured.** Nine hours of dispatching without once checking `uptime`. The whole campaign's rule is *measure, do not assume*, and I applied it to every artifact except the machine running them.

The builder-side lesson is simpler and belongs in every dispatch: **a subagent that starts a long-lived process or container tears it down and says so in its report.** One critic did exactly this ("container removed afterwards; the reviewed tree is unmodified") and it cost nothing. The rest left dev servers, databases and multi-GB clones behind, and none of them were wrong to — nobody had told them.

**Process note — an agent waiting on a long job polls the orchestrator unless told to block** (orchestration, 2026-08-01). A round waiting on a 114-mutant run sent **three near-identical progress reports**, each triggered by the notification that fires when an agent stops with no live children. Checking, reporting and stopping turns a wait into a poll loop that costs the orchestrator a turn per cycle and produces no information. Lesson kept: **instruct long-running agents to block on the process and send one message when they have the answer**, and list in the dispatch exactly what that message must contain — otherwise the natural behaviour is to report state, which is what a helpful agent does when it has nothing to add. Same family as the `pgrep` wait-loop that matched itself and the mutation runner that survived `TaskStop`: **a monitor that costs more than it measures is a defect in the monitor.**

**Process note — an adjudication's remedy must be verified to exist** (#21 r7 gauntlet, 2026-08-01). I ruled for the middle option on type certification specifically because a laundered reading would "go to Needs-you with its quote for a person to accept or decline" — that promise is what made the narrowing acceptable rather than product-breaking. Measured: `attention.ts:394` skips every proposal that is not a `decision` or `commitment`, so `projectAttention` returns **0 items and 0 refusals**, and `needs_you` has no consumer anywhere outside core. **The remedy I weighed the decision on does not exist.** Lesson kept: **when an adjudication accepts a cost because some other mechanism absorbs it, verify that mechanism runs** — an orchestrator's ruling is a claim like any other, and the part most likely to be assumed is the part that makes the ruling comfortable. Related in the same review: `authority.ts` prose states a floor is `+Infinity` for claims "since r7" while the runtime value is **0.7**, and a model lands a claim end-to-end at that confidence — found independently by two agents.

**Positive note — a scoped fix round, and what it proves about the fourteen before it** (#10 r15, 2026-08-01). Told to fix exactly three defects and **explicitly forbidden from building or extending an instrument**, the round fixed them at the rendering: the full string stays in the DOM so `textContent` remains the record, the clamp moves to CSS so the browser paints the ellipsis, the clamp is *declared* so it inherits the existing keyboard-reachability rule, and the preview becomes a control that jumps to the row it quotes. It then found the same defect in a second surface nobody had named and applied the same fix. Repro assertions 80 → 0, exit 1 → 0, states 204 → 204 with no coverage lost.

Two things it surfaced are worth more than the fixes. **A doctrine claim had been false for thirteen rounds** — the colour block asserting `--tx3` is referenced nowhere, while two live rules used it at 4.25:1 and 3.69:1. And the truthful disclosure in the reopen prompt was **497px of placeholder in a 129px box**, worse than the review measured, invisible to every rule on the page because placeholders do not contribute to `scrollWidth`.

Lesson kept: **a round told to fix three things fixes three things and finds two more; a round told to find defects builds an instrument.** Fourteen rounds of the second kind produced excellent machinery and left a reply quoting a person mid-word since round two. The framing of the brief determines the shape of the work far more than the skill of the agent doing it.

**THE EXIT RULE WORKED, AND WHAT IT FOUND WAS INVISIBLE TO FOURTEEN ROUNDS** (#10 r14, 2026-08-01). Instead of a fifteenth open-ended gauntlet, one review was asked a single question: *is there a defect both live in the shipped artifact — reachable by driving it, not by patching source — and something a user would experience as wrong?* Everything fourteen rounds had been made of was declared **out of scope for the verdict**: a checker with a gap no live surface exploits, doctrine overstating a mechanism, a test that could be stronger, a defect reachable only by editing source.

It returned **HOLD** with three defects, and the reason they survived fourteen rounds is the lesson. **A reply renders a person's sentence cut mid-word** — `slice(0, 60)`, no ellipsis, no title, no route to the rest, marked `data-voice="human"` with provenance, nine of fourteen rows affected — and **both instruments pass it honestly**: the provenance check is correct because a 60-character prefix genuinely is contained in the cited message, and the legibility check is correct because the string was cut *before* painting, so `scrollWidth === clientWidth`. **The defect lives in the gap between two correct checks, which is precisely where no amount of hardening either one will reach.** The other two are the same shape: a composer stating in present tense that interpretation runs on send when nothing in the file ever appends to the object store, and a reopen prompt whose truthful disclosure ("recorded as your words, verbatim") is clipped unreadable at every width while the readable hint says the opposite — and placeholders do not contribute to `scrollWidth`, so no instrument can see it.

Lessons kept:
- **Ask a review a question that can end the lane.** Fourteen rounds of "find defects" found defects, because that question always has an answer. One round of "does this lie to a person" found three things that mattered and excluded the class that would have generated round fifteen.
- **Between any two correct checks there is a seam, and it is where the user-visible defects live.** Both of this artifact's instruments were right; the string was shortened in one layer and measured in another. When you have two guards, ask what passes both.
- **The reviewer also said plainly that the product is good** — three questions answered in the first screen without a click, every write leaving a receipt with an undo it could not find missing, and *"NOTHING NEEDS YOU IN THIS ROOM — THAT IS A RESULT, NOT AN ABSENCE"* as the empty state. A verdict that can say *good* is worth more than one that can only enumerate.

**THE CALL I SHOULD HAVE MADE FIVE ROUNDS AGO — hardening a gate against its own author has no terminal state** (#40, 2026-08-01). Ten rounds. Round 10 fixed its headline genuinely — verified against r9 directly, 624 bytes identical in both worlds before, 1385 vs 1939 after — and its critic then reproduced **round 9's D2 verbatim against round 10's fix for it**, at a one-token cost (`checkSchema(expected, expected)`: 260 real comparisons performed on the wrong operand, green against a live stack with a table dropped).

Look at what every remaining finding requires: writing `checkSchema(expected, expected)`; rewriting an assertion file into 62 lines of tautologies; deleting three registry lines; aliasing an import. **All of them require editing the gate's own source in the same commit.** Meanwhile the same critic verified the gate against *real* drift — drop a table, the shipped check exits 1, and all 18 deploy steps pass on a live stack with every count matching the manifest.

**That is a different threat model from every other lane, and I ran it as though it were the same one.** The product lanes ask *does this lie to a user*, and their findings were live: a `.js` route returned real membership rows to a real unauthenticated request. This lane asks *can the gate's author weaken the gate*, and the answer is permanently yes — because the author can always change the gate. Each round therefore produces a genuine finding and cannot reduce the space, which is exactly what a loop looks like from the inside while feeling like progress.

Lesson kept: **before running a review loop, write down what the artifact is defending against and who the adversary is.** A gate defending against *accidental regression* is testable and terminates — you plant a real defect and require the red. A gate defending against *its own maintainer* is unbounded, and the correct control is not a further round but a different mechanism entirely: review of changes to the gate, ownership rules, or a check the author cannot edit in the same commit. **Ten rounds of increasingly clever self-defence bought resistance to an adversary who was never in the threat model.**

**RETRACTED — the merge train is NOT blocked by one prose file, and the code does NOT merge clean** (merge train, 2026-08-01). **This entry was wrong and the error was in my instrument.** My "simulated sequential train" **aborted each conflicted merge and continued**, so every later lane was tested against `main + readme + prototype` only — never against its predecessors. A train that skips a car is not a train. Re-run correctly by the reconciliation agent: **realtime-r8 conflicts on ~31 files** (`reduce.ts`, `attention.ts`, `authority.ts`, `escalation.ts`, `events.ts`, `policy.ts`, `packages/db/src/schema.ts`, nine core test files), **auth-r11 on ~15**, **deploy-serves-r10 on ~36** (`ws-server.ts`, `ws-auth.ts`, `packages/auth/src/org.ts`, `auth.ts`). Only **three** lanes touch `CONVENTIONS.md` at all; four contain none of it. `README.md` is a four-lane blocker and `RETRO.md` blocks realtime.

**Lesson kept, and it is the sharpest measurement error of the campaign: a simulation that discards a failure and proceeds is measuring a different system.** `git merge --abort` on conflict meant each lane met only the successfully-merged prefix, which is the one configuration nobody will ever be in. The correct simulation resolves each conflict (or stops at the first one and says so) — and the giveaway I ignored was that the result was *too good*: seven parallel lanes rewriting one package with zero source collisions should have been implausible on its face. **When a measurement returns the answer you were hoping for, check the instrument before reporting it.**

The original, now-retracted reading was: 2 of 7 clean, 5 conflicting, every conflict on `design/CONVENTIONS.md` — realtime additionally on `README.md` and `RETRO.md`. **Not one `.ts`, `.tsx`, `.mjs` or `.sql` conflict across all seven lanes.** Seven parallel lanes touching a semantic engine, a realtime layer, an auth boundary, a UI library and a CI gate, and the source code composes without a single collision.

The cause is the orchestrator's: **nearly every round's brief required a doctrine correction, which made one shared prose file the coordination point for seven parallel lanes.** This is precisely the lesson recorded earlier the same day about a README — *append-only edits compose, structural edits do not, and the dependency graph cannot see the difference* — applied to `README.md` and then not applied to `CONVENTIONS.md`. **A lesson filed against one file does not transfer to the next file by itself.**

Lessons kept:
- **When many lanes must amend shared doctrine, give each lane an append-only slot** (a dated section, or one file per lane reconciled later), or schedule the doctrine edits as a single pass after the train. Requiring seven independent authors to edit one prose file is a merge conflict specified in advance.
- **Simulate the train before you need it.** The simulation cost one command and converted "the largest unretired risk" into "one file needs reconciling" — and the earlier pairwise measurement, which showed 9 of 10 pairs conflicting, was *technically correct and practically misleading*, because it never modelled the sequence anyone would actually perform.

**SEVENTH INJURY, NEW MECHANISM — killing the wrapper does not kill the runner** (#21 r8, 2026-08-01). A round's own 10-minute Bash timeout killed the **wrapper** around `mutants/run.mjs`, not the runner. It kept mutating `packages/core/src` for another ten minutes, and a drift scan, a `tsc` run and a full suite run taken in that window **all measured a moving tree** — producing one false "live mutant" and one false two-test failure, both of which evaporated on recheck. Nothing was staged, and the round named it rather than quietly re-running.

This is the seventh distinct injury from one property, and the mechanism is new: every prior mitigation assumed that stopping the thing you launched stops the work. **A timeout, a `TaskStop`, or a killed shell reaps the process you can see, not the process doing the writing.** Added to the guard ticket: the harness must detect orphaning (parent gone, or a heartbeat file it owns going stale) and terminate itself, because the caller cannot reliably kill it. Corollary for any measurement taken near a harness: **a reading taken while something might be writing is not a reading** — one false positive and one false negative came out of the same ten minutes, which is the worst possible combination because each looks like the other's opposite.

**Positive note — enforce the invariant with a type, not a convention** (#21 r8, 2026-08-01). The critical defect was that a guarantee established over `normalizeForReceipt(text)` was consumed by a predicate reading the **raw** text. The fix is the strongest form available: `normalizeForReceipt` now returns a **branded `ReceiptText`**, and every certification predicate takes that type, so **the old call no longer compiles — and the compiler found the one call site the author had missed.** Kept as the model for this whole class: where a rule says "X must always be consumed in form Y", a nominal type makes the rule unforgettable and finds the instances for you. The sweep it forced then surfaced two more live defects on the same axis. Same family as "when a tool has repeatedly injured people who knew the rule, the fix belongs in the tool" — here the tool is the type system.

**NEAR-MISS, and the cheap guard nobody built — a mutation harness ran in the primary checkout** (tooling, 2026-08-01). A reviewer reported a live `mutants/run.mjs` belonging to another session and **correctly declined to touch it** — killing another session's run is not a reviewer's call. Checking every such process by `cwd` found the real problem: one was running in **`/home/lars/atrium` itself**, the primary checkout, which was parked on a stale branch. It exited before it could be caught and left nothing behind, so this is a near-miss rather than damage — but had it been interrupted mid-mutation, the primary checkout would have carried a mutant as an ordinary uncommitted edit on a branch nobody was watching, which is exactly how a live mutant got committed earlier in the day.

**Sixth injury from one property in a single campaign.** Lesson kept, and it is a two-line change rather than a discipline: **the harness should refuse to run outside a dedicated worktree** — assert its `cwd` is not the primary checkout, or require an explicit `--i-own-this-tree` flag. Every previous mitigation has been a rule for humans and agents to remember (review from `git archive`, stage explicit paths, reap and diff, don't stash during a run), and all six injuries happened *despite* those rules being written down. **When a tool has repeatedly injured people who knew the rule, the fix belongs in the tool.**

Second, procedural: **checking by process name found nothing; checking by `cwd` found it.** The processes were indistinguishable by command line — same script, same flags — and differed only in which tree they owned. When several agents run the same harness, identity is the working directory.

**HAZARD — `git stash` during a live harness run poisons it silently** (#10 r14, 2026-08-01). A round ran `git stash` / `stash pop` to compare a lint baseline **while a driver run was in flight**, briefly reverting the artifact under the driver. It discarded that run and re-ran from scratch rather than reporting it — the right call, and it recorded the incident. **Fifth distinct injury in this campaign from the same family: a tree mutated underneath something reading it.** The others: a mutation runner surviving `TaskStop` twice and leaving mutants; a reviewer reading a mutated file as source; `git add -A` committing a live mutant. Lesson kept: **any operation that rewrites the working tree — stash, checkout, rebase, a mutation harness, a formatter — is mutually exclusive with anything reading it**, and the reader usually cannot tell. Gate tree-rewriting operations on no harness running, and treat a run that overlapped one as void rather than as data.

**Positive note — generating the reach claim falsified the hand-written one immediately** (#10 r14, 2026-08-01). Four consecutive rounds shipped a real mechanism with its coverage described from memory, and all four descriptions were false. Round 14 generated the reach into a committed file compared against the live walk on every run — **and that generated file replaced a six-item hand-written list, one of whose entries was never true of itself.** Kept as the closing evidence for a rule this campaign arrived at three times before applying: **a claim about what a mechanism covers must be produced by the mechanism.** The moment it was, the prose it replaced was shown wrong without anyone looking for that. Two companions from the same round: **"the narrowest supported width" was a phrase nobody had measured** — it is 1120, below which the grid pushes a pane off-screen on the two previous rounds as well, so the page now asserts that floor itself; and the round's own new rules found **seven defects on the build that shipped them**, including a form that swallowed the number it was meant to check.

**HAZARD — `git add -A` while an in-place mutation harness is running commits a live mutant, and `git status` reports it backwards** (#39 r8, 2026-08-01). A round staged everything while its mutation harness had a component mutated, and **committed the mutant**. The working tree and every subsequent gate were correct, so nothing went red — the damage lived only in that one commit. Worse, the diagnosis inverts: **`git status` shows the correction as an uncommitted edit and the defect as nothing at all**, because HEAD holds the mutant and the tree holds the truth. Lesson kept: **never stage broadly while any harness mutates files in place** — and this is the fourth distinct injury from that one property across the campaign (a runner surviving `TaskStop` and leaving mutants twice, a reviewer reading a mutated file believing it was source, and now a committed mutant). Stage explicit paths, or gate staging on the harness being provably stopped. When something looks impossible in `git status`, check whether HEAD rather than the tree is the wrong one.

**Process note — three findings from running a round's ledger against its own fix** (#39 r8, 2026-08-01), each worth keeping on its own:
- **A test driven off a list cannot see the list shrink.** If the assertion iterates the same collection the code derives, removing an entry removes the check with it.
- **A sweep whose job is "catch the defect at any address" needs a self-test that does not depend on the library currently having that defect** — otherwise the sweep's own coverage evaporates the moment the artifact is clean, which is precisely when it is trusted most.
- **A wrong instrument invents defects as readily as it misses them.** Measured twice in one round: a ledger's syntax gate parsed JSON as TypeScript, and a new CSS enumerator **read a floor out of the prose explaining the floor**.

Also recorded by that round, and caused by this orchestrator: **its e2e suite is load-sensitive** — 82/82 three times, and one run with 8 failures, all `session closed` protocol errors in tests predating the round, on a 4-core box under the concurrency I had allowed. Not a regression, and the next round is told not to read a red run as one. **An overloaded machine does not only slow a campaign down; it manufactures failures that look like defects.**

**Corollary, and the sharpest phrasing of the cross-fix vacuity class: a test covering two enforcement points measures whichever one is left** (#21 r7, 2026-08-01). All three of that round's ledger escapes were diagnosed to one cause and closed. Two were fixtures containing "will" that, once a type rule shipped, were refused for their *type* rather than for the rule the row named — so the row stopped measuring the tokenizer. The third was different and more general: a test asserting an outcome that **either** of two guards produces cannot detect the loss of one, because the other still yields the assertion. Lesson kept: **assert against the enforcement point by name, not against the outcome** — split a test that spans an engine and a floor into one per layer, or the pair is a single test with a spare. Also worth recording as a causal chain: the timeout sharding done for an unrelated defect **also fixed the ledger's reproducibility**, because the 5000 ms flake had been polluting the recorded failing-test lists; `RESULTS.md` now diffs to zero lines across consecutive runs on an exclusively-owned checkout, so its stated promise — that a reader can audit the claim without rerunning — is true for the first time.

**A NEW CLASS — two fixes in one round can silently de-discriminate each other's tests** (#21 r7, 2026-08-01). The mutation ledger's strictest mode — *caught, but not by the test that claims to pin this rule* — reported **111 of 114 caught, 3 "wrong tests"**, and all three were the round's own work. The cause is an interaction between two fixes landed hours apart in the same round: the window-gate fixture is *"We will deploy production Friday afternoon as planned."*, which contains **"will"**, so the type-certification rule added later now fires on it. The reducer test asserts `state.objects === {}` — still true, for the *type* reason, whether or not the window gate exists. Same shape for the type rule's own mutant: disabling the engine row leaves the reducer refusing, so the assertion holds and the test stops discriminating.

**Both tests were correct when written, neither was touched, and the interaction destroyed them.** Lesson kept: **a test's discrimination is a property of the whole system, not of the test** — adding a refusal anywhere upstream can make a downstream assertion true for a new reason, and nothing in a diff shows it. Two consequences for practice. **Any round landing more than one refusal must re-run the ledger's wrong-test mode at the end, not after each fix** — the vacuity only exists once both are present. And **a fixture should be minimal against the rule it pins**: this one was commitment-shaped for realism, which is exactly what let a second rule claim it. Pick fixtures that trip *one* rule.

Worth recording separately: this is the strongest argument yet for the ledger design r3 built. A plain "did the suite go red" check reports 114/114 here and is wrong three times. **The distinction between *a* test failing and *the named* test failing is the whole value**, and it caught the people who built it, in the round whose entire subject is assertions that are green for the wrong reason.

**REFINEMENT TO THE CAMPAIGN'S CENTRAL RULE — when a denylist is admissible** (#21 r7, 2026-08-01). The standing rule, arrived at independently in four subsystems, is *allowlist the compliant form, never denylist the violations*, because a denylist's gaps are unbounded when whoever evades writes the next spelling. Round 7 shipped a **marker list** — words of undertaking — to decide whether a proposal's declared type is certifiable from its text, and argued the exception. The argument is correct and the conditions are worth stating precisely:

1. **The adversary does not control the input the list scans.** The proposer controls `type`; it does not control `statement`, because the receipt has already proved that text is the author's words, verbatim, whole sentences of the bearing message. **Evading the list requires the *author* to have written an undertaking containing no word of undertaking.**
2. **A hit can only add a referral, never an acceptance.** A false positive costs a human glance; a false negative is the pre-existing state. The list cannot manufacture trust, only withhold it.

**So: a denylist is admissible exactly when the adversary controls neither the input nor the direction of the failure.** **CORRECTED 2026-08-01 by #21 r7's gauntlet — a third condition is required and its absence broke this exact case: _the check must read the same representation the pin was applied to._** The receipt proved equality under `normalizeForReceipt`, which deletes zero-width characters; `readsAsCommitment` read the **raw** statement. So the proposer controlled the text freely *inside the normalization's equivalence class*, and `"I wi\u200Bll land the narrowing fix"` auto-accepted with a perfect receipt — **all ten shapes evaded by one deletable code point, swept over the whole finite domain**, both enforcement points failing identically because both call the same predicate over the same unnormalized text. Condition 1 was satisfied for the *words* and violated for the *bytes*. **Whenever a guarantee is established over a normalized form, every downstream check must consume that same normalized form, or it is reading something the guarantee never pinned.** All three conditions are load-bearing — drop the first and it is the unbounded case; drop the second and a missed spelling grants acceptance instead of withholding it. This is the same reasoning as an earlier round's retraction-marker list, and it is why *that* one was also fine. The residue is named in source and in a test rather than discovered later: *"I'm on it."* certifies as a claim and auto-accepts, rendering as `~` with its quote and in nobody's queue — the bounded, visible failure, deliberately kept over the unbounded invisible one.

**Process note — `TaskStop` does not kill `mutants/run.mjs`, confirmed a second time** (#21 r7, 2026-08-01). The round's teardown found **three `run.mjs` processes that had survived `TaskStop`** — one needing `kill -9` — and **each had left a mutant in `packages/core/src`**, which it restored. A prior round hit the same thing and was saved only by `tsc`. Lesson kept: **a harness that mutates in place must be reaped explicitly and verified, not signalled** — after any interruption, check for survivors and diff the source before trusting the tree. The teardown discipline added to dispatches today is what surfaced this: the round listed what it killed, which is how a recurring hazard became visible rather than being absorbed silently twice.

**Process note — I changed the instrument every round and destroyed my own trend data** (orchestration, 2026-08-01). Asked whether the campaign was converging or looping, I could not answer it from measurement. Attempting to: **severity counts are not comparable across the history** because the receipt format changed and early rounds carried no severity labels. The one signal that looked clean — mentions of what a critic *could not break*, rising 0,0,0,0,0 → 5 → 10 in one lane — is **confounded by me**, because I began asking critics to report what held partway through. Every axis available has my own changes in it: critic capability rose sharply (source-reading → prepared trees → real databases → live stacks), prompt structure changed most rounds, and the reporting requirements changed too.

**Exactly one comparison survives: the same method, same artifact, consecutive rounds.** It exists in two lanes and both improved — realtime, executing critic with a real Postgres both times, went from three majors to one MEDIUM and one LOW-MED with "the durable trust boundary held everything"; auth, same setup, went from a critical-by-consequence plus a HIGH to two HIGHs of which "neither is a live leak in the shipped tree". That is the only convergence claim currently defensible.

Lesson kept, and it is the campaign's own rule applied to the campaign: **if you want to know whether the work is improving, you must hold the measuring apparatus still for at least two rounds.** Improving the critics *and* the artifact simultaneously is the right thing for the product and makes the trend unreadable — so freeze the method deliberately, on a schedule, and accept one round of weaker review to buy an interpretable number. I recorded this intention when first asked and then did not do it, which is how the second measurement failed the same way as the first.

**THE DIVERGENCE, RESOLVED — parallel lanes are different systems, and a cross-lane claim does not transfer** (#26 / #22 / #53, 2026-08-01). Two executed reviews gave opposite answers on whether a revoked member keeps receiving room content. An arbitration ran both procedures against both branches on one dedicated database — four cells — and the separating precondition was **the branch**. `git merge-base --is-ancestor` returns NO in both directions: these are **parallel lanes, not successive rounds, and they are two different servers wearing the same filenames.** One has `hub.ts`, `reconciler.ts` and room content and **no sweep at all** (`grep -rn "sweep"` → exit 1); the other has a 15 s sweep and **no room content at all** (no `event` frame, `core_events` absent, `message.send` refused to a room owner). **Neither can answer the question, because no commit yet has both halves.**

Lessons kept, and the first is the orchestration one I most needed:
- **A finding measured on one lane is a finding about that lane.** I ran seven lanes in parallel for a day and treated results as transferable. They diverge enough that two reviews can be simultaneously right, contradictory, and both misleading about the product. **Before relaying a cross-lane claim, check whether the lanes share the code the claim is about.**
- **A guarantee measured where the thing it guards does not exist is not a measurement.** The sweep provably evicts — of a presence roster, on a branch with no messages. Proving a roster is swept says nothing about whether content fan-out would be.
- **A number produced under a test override is a property of the override.** "Zero further broadcasts" came from an e2e `WS_SWEEP_INTERVAL_MS` of 1 s; at the 15 s production default the same removal delivers **14**. Always ask which configuration produced a number before repeating it.
- **And the sharpest: the cited test passes at both 1 s and 15 s**, because it asserts broadcasts do not increase *after* eviction and never asserts none arrived *during* the window. **A test cannot support a number it is structurally unable to detect** — the question to ask of any cited test is not "does it pass" but "what would make it fail".

Recorded on both tickets with the four-cell table, and the unanswerable part is filed as **#53** as a merge-train requirement rather than a lane's round.

**THE DIVERGENCE — two executed reviews contradicted each other, and I had already published a correction based on one** (#26 / #22, 2026-08-01). Two critics, both running code, both specific, gave opposite answers to a security question: does a member with an open socket keep receiving room content after revocation? One deleted a `memberships` row and watched her receive message bodies twelve reconciler passes later, concluding "there is no sweep that drops her". The other ran an e2e case in which a removed member is evicted from the roster and closed with code 1008 by a sweep, receiving nothing further. I had already posted a correction on one ticket asserting the first as fact. Lesson kept: **convergence between independent reviews is the strongest signal this process produces, and divergence is the second strongest — but only if it is treated as a question rather than resolved by recency.** Two good reviews disagreeing has located something neither would have found alone; picking the more recent one and moving on would have destroyed exactly that. The correct move is to publish the contradiction *as* a contradiction, state that the bound is currently unknown, and dispatch a resolution that reproduces both procedures against one server and reports the separating precondition. Note also the near-miss in the shape: earlier the same day I asked a reviewer to check whether two lanes' differently-worded claims described the same bound rather than assuming they agreed — that instinct was right and I then failed to apply it to my own correction.

**Process note — a guard whose verdict depends on build state, failing open in the state it is run in** (#26 r11 gauntlet, 2026-08-01). An import into `packages/db/dist` — the package's **published** export surface, since its `exports` map points `./schema` at `./dist/schema.js` — is silent across all six of the round's new channels, and `curl` with no cookie returned all 36 membership rows. The same file **is** caught on a freshly cloned, unbuilt tree (`no-such-file`), and goes silent only after `pnpm --filter "./packages/*" build`, which is the first half of the artifact's own prescribed command line and what CI, `typecheck` and every reviewer runs first. Lesson kept: **a check whose answer depends on build state must be evaluated in the state it will actually run in, and the dangerous direction is the built one** — a guard that is strict on a clean checkout and lenient after a build is a guard that is strict only in the configuration nobody uses. Test both states explicitly and assert they agree.

**Process note — the suite proved the join existed and never proved it correlated** (#26 r11 gauntlet, 2026-08-01). Ten authorization predicates stubbed fail-open: **nine caught**, which is a genuinely strong result. The tenth — dropping only the workspace correlation from a join while keeping the join itself — passed **820 unit tests and 45 e2e**, and was then proven a real cross-workspace read with a three-row seeded fixture. Why nothing saw it: every e2e scenario removes the member's `workspace_members` row *entirely*, so a join requiring merely *some* workspace row satisfies them all. And the ledger's own `drop-join` mutant deletes all three `.innerJoin` lines — **strictly stronger, and therefore useless here**; the string `organizationId` appears nowhere in its 49 rows. Lesson kept: **a stronger mutation does not subsume a weaker one — deleting a check tests that it exists, while weakening it tests what it means.** For every join, predicate or comparison, mutate the *condition*, not its presence.

**THE LESSON THAT RECURRED — validate a union by its tag, not around it** (#22 r8 gauntlet, 2026-08-01). This repo's fifth-ever commit is *"RETRO: validate a union by its tag, not by key presence."* It came back, in the fix for an unbound-value defect:

```ts
proposer: draft.proposer.kind === 'human' ? { kind: 'human', userId: session.userId } : draft.proposer,
```

`userId` is now bound from the session. **`kind` — the field deciding whether it gets bound — is still written by the caller.** Spelling the forgery `model` escapes the entire fix. Executed on real sockets: a member forged a commitment owned by another member, quoting that member's genuine message, and got `auto_accept` / `attribution: self` / `awaitingConfirmFrom: null` where the honest path gives `pending` / `needs_you` / `third_party` / `awaitingConfirmFrom: <victim>`. **The trusted actor derived from the session was in the ledger row beside it, unused.** Lesson kept, sharper than the original: **binding a value conditionally on an unbound one binds nothing** — the discriminant is the first thing an attacker reaches for, and a branch keyed on it is a menu. Derive the whole union from the session as `actorOf` does; allowlist the compliant form rather than branching on the attacker's own label. Note the shape of the recurrence: the original lesson was learned about *schema validation*, and it reappeared in *authorization* — **a lesson filed against one subsystem does not transfer itself.**

**Positive note — an impossibility proof survived an independent attempt, and the negative result is worth keeping** (#22 r8 gauntlet, 2026-08-01). Round 8 argued no unforgeable token exists because caller and function share a session and role. A reviewer attacked it with a `BEFORE INSERT` trigger dumping every candidate fact through a SECURITY DEFINER function, a bare `DO` block, and a same-schema impostor — `current_user`, `pg_trigger_depth()`, `pg_current_xact_id()`, a private witness table, a sequence, `pg_stat_get_xact_function_calls`, and `pg_backend_memory_contexts`. **Every value identical.** The best candidate failed for a reason worth recording: the `PL/pgSQL function` memory context is a **session-lifetime compiled-function cache, not a live-frame witness**, so it appears in a bare `DO` block after any single legitimate call. Event triggers (DDL only), `pg_stat_activity.query` (caller-authored), constraint-trigger timing, `cmin` visibility and RLS with a `BYPASSRLS` definer were all considered and rejected. Kept because **a confirmed impossibility proof is a durable result**: it closes the question for every future round, and the list of rejected candidates is the part that saves the time.

**THE FOURTH ARRIVAL — the file-set denominator, in four subsystems on one day** (#26, #10, #39, 2026-08-01). Every one of these guards was correct about the shapes it understood and wrong about what it looked at:
- **auth**: the import analyser parsed `.ts/.tsx/.mts/.cts` only, skipped any directory named `test` or `build` *anywhere* including the App Router's own `app/test/`, and silently waved through anything resolving outside its roots — while `allowJs: true` was set and the framework served `route.js` as a route. Fixing it took the analysed set from **79 files to 127**.
- **prototype**: all four instruments in `design/` hard-coded `viewport: {width: 1440, height: 900}`, so thirteen rounds of numbers described one viewport and a live invariant violation sat three clicks away at every width ≤ 1279.
- **UI**: the printed-string sweep enumerates `*.tsx` under exactly two directories, so a `.jsx` component and a file one directory over render untraced strings and the sweep reports **226 sites, unchanged, green** — with `allowJs: true` and the bundler compiling both.
- **core**: a tokenizer's character class decided what counted as a token, deleting non-Latin script and negation particles.

Lesson kept, and it is now the first question to ask of any checker: **what does it look at, and how does that set relate to what actually executes?** Tie the enumeration to the build — the bundler's module graph, the framework's route conventions, the compiler's `include` — and assert the difference is empty. **A guard's file list is a claim about the world, and it is the claim nobody tests**, because every test is written against the shapes and the shapes are fine.

**Process note — a portal is not `isValidElement`, and a checked boundary said yes** (#39 r7 gauntlet, 2026-08-01). `slot()` walks strings, iterables and `isValidElement` nodes; a React portal's `$$typeof` is `REACT_PORTAL_TYPE`, so `isValidElement` is false, the walk returns, and the slot **accepts** — after which React renders the `<q data-quoted="msg:forged">` inside it, carrying the exact provenance token added a round earlier because "a token a slot can mint proves nothing". Identical in shape to the `Set` fail-open the previous review found. Lesson kept: **a walk that recurses on recognised shapes and returns on the rest is an allowlist that fails open** — invert it, refuse the unrecognised, and enumerate what that refuses. Companion from the same review: the sweep's type test passes on `Any|Unknown|String|TemplateLiteral`, so a **branded string** (`string & {…}`, an Intersection) is never traced — and it is the only branded type in the library, and the one whose raw render was the previous round's finding.

**Process note — a sentence that is only a number is invisible to every instrument that keys on words** (#10 r13 gauntlet, 2026-08-01). Thirteen rounds built increasingly good checkers over minted sentences: a mutation that flips each declared read, a counted-claims matcher, a vocabulary check, a quantifier check. **All four need a field word to latch onto.** The rail's owed badge is a mint declaring thirteen reads whose entire sentence is `"3"` — so `say()`'s point-read mutation is skipped for all thirteen (it is gated on the field's word appearing in the output), the counted-claims matcher matches none of its eight patterns, and both remaining checks need a word. Proven by mutation: one line patched to `return "7"` paints `◆7` against `NEEDS YOU 2`, `2 items`, `2 awaiting you` and two `1 needs you` headers **with the console completely silent**. Lesson kept: **a detector keyed on syntax has a shape it cannot see, and the shape is usually the shortest one** — bare numerals, single glyphs, empty strings. When a check matches patterns, enumerate what has *no* pattern and assert that set empty, exactly as the sibling lane's six unparsed/unresolved channels do.

**Process note — the viewport was part of the denominator and nobody declared it** (#10 r13 gauntlet, 2026-08-01). Every instrument in `design/` — the driver, the shot tool, the probe, the smoke test — hard-codes `viewport: {width: 1440, height: 900}`. So a live violation of the page's own legibility invariant, reachable in three deliberate clicks at **every supported width ≤ 1279**, was invisible for thirteen rounds and is present on r12 as well. The trigger is ordinary: clearing your work makes `· 40 unread` replace `◆3`, the wider badge squeezes the flexible name track, and **the room you are standing in truncates to `# users-migra…`**. The round's boundary section names three limits of its search and does not name this one. Lesson kept: **every environmental constant a harness fixes is part of its denominator — viewport, locale, timezone, theme, DPR, reduced-motion, input modality — and each must be either varied or declared as a limit.** A number reported without its environment is a number about one environment.

**Process note — twelve rounds of instruments and none of them reads English** (#10 r13 gauntlet, 2026-08-01). A permanent divider has ended mid-sentence since **round 2** — *"…and only this room's"*, a dangling possessive — through twelve review rounds, six of them with a reviewer driving the page. Every checker verifies that a sentence is *derived correctly*; none verifies that it is *a sentence*. Kept as the cheap reminder that **a verification stack optimises for the failures it was built from**, and the failure nobody had yet is the one it cannot see. Reading the rendered text aloud, once, at the end of a round costs nothing and is not covered by any amount of provenance machinery.

**THE ESCAPE FROM THE DENYLIST TRAP, AT THREE ALTITUDES** (#40 r10, 2026-08-01). After a corpus of wrong reds went stale the moment a fix changed a failure's shape, the next round did not add a fourth entry. It climbed:
1. **Generate the corpus from the mechanism.** `preconditionReds()` enumerates the *worlds* the code distinguishes and runs the shipped function over each — so the `N assertion(s) failed` spelling came out of the renderer itself, and that generated entry killed two controls whose `expect` was a bare failure count that nobody had thought to list.
2. **Stop matching strings entirely.** `distinguishProblems` runs each port-subject entry point against a closed port *and* a decoy that accepts-and-resets, and **requires the two outputs to differ**. Measured: the previous round emitted **615 bytes, byte-identical, in both worlds**; this one emits 1955 versus 1302. A control that cannot produce different output in different worlds is not a control, and this checks that directly rather than asking what its output looks like.
3. **Allowlist what counts as the thing.** `assertionConditionProblems` requires a recorded assertion to **read a value** — so `check(false, problem)` stays legal and the next tautology spelling needs no enumerating at all.

Lesson kept: **when a denylist goes stale, the ladder is generate → observe → allowlist**, and each rung removes a class of future staleness rather than an instance. Also from the same round, the shape that made its coverage fix real: it keyed the rule on **where a word resolves rather than how it is spelled**, while deliberately keeping the strict anchored pattern in the deploy allowlist — *"a miss means opposite things in the two places"*, which is the asymmetry the previous round walked past. It found a genuine uncontrolled entry point immediately (`--reporter=./scripts/ci/vitest-ci-reporter.mjs`). And the assertion floor now counts **work done** rather than call sites: the gutted schema check reports *"recorded 0 assertion(s) … says it makes at least 230"*, with the number coming from inside the walk that visited 260 tables, columns, constraints and indexes.

**THE CORPUS WENT STALE THE MOMENT THE FIX CHANGED THE FAILURE'S SHAPE** (#40 r9 gauntlet, 2026-08-01). I prescribed a corpus of wrong reds to enforce "a control must fail for the right reason". Round 9 built it, and the same defect survived a third time — because **the corpus is a denylist and the fix changed the shape of the thing it denies.** `WRONG_REDS` holds the *stack-trace* spelling of a missing CA. The round then converted that crash into a *recorded assertion*, which is the right change, and **wrote the new spelling into the control's `expect` as an accepted alternative** — a shape the corpus had never seen. Executed: a live HTTPS server and nothing listening produce **byte-identical** output. Lesson kept: **a corpus of counterexamples is only as current as the failure modes it was built from, and the act of fixing a failure creates a new spelling of it.** When a fix changes how something fails, the corpus must be extended *in the same commit* — and better, derive the corpus from the failure paths rather than listing them, so a new path enters it automatically. Also false and load-bearing: a comment asserting `expectationProblems` "refuses any control whose expectation could be satisfied by this message alone". Nothing did that, and three controls' entire `expect` **was** that message.

**Process note — a floor over a loop is a floor over nothing** (#40 r9 gauntlet, 2026-08-01). A new ratchet counted each assertion script's `check()` calls to stop a script being gutted. Four scripts record every problem through **one syntactic call site over a computed list** — `for (const problem of problems) check(false, problem);` — and carry a floor of **1**. A five-line replacement of the schema assertion was red cold, **green against the live migrated stack**, floor satisfied, and compared nothing: *"the campaign's founding defect, reachable in five lines at round nine, on the one assertion standing between `migrate` exiting 0 and the schema existing."* Lesson kept: **counting call sites measures syntax, not work** — a ratchet over a quantity the author controls in one line is a ratchet over the author's cooperation. Count assertions *executed* at runtime, or compare against an independently derived expectation. Companion in the same review: a coverage regex anchored as `scripts/ci/…` misses `./scripts/ci/…`, so the round's fix "works against the exact spelling it was written for and against nothing else" — its own comment diagnoses that class and then replaces one convention with another.

**Positive note — the corpus of wrong reds turns a principle into a mechanism** (#40 r9, 2026-08-01). The previous round's critical defect was a positive control whose `expect` patterns were bare script names, satisfied by any stack trace mentioning the file — so it certified assertions red for a missing CA rather than a missing deployment. The rule I gave was *"match on behaviour, not identity"*. The round did better than follow it: **`expectationProblems` runs every control's expectation against a `WRONG_REDS` corpus** — a stack trace naming the script, an `ENOENT` naming it, a syntax error, `node: command not found`, a bare `ECONNREFUSED`, `spawnSync docker ENOENT`, `Terminated` — and **refuses the expectation if any of them satisfies it, before any child is spawned.** The three defective patterns are refused; the shipped table is clean. Lesson kept, and it generalises past controls: **the way to enforce "this check must fail for the right reason" is a corpus of wrong reasons that the check must not accept.** Any expectation, matcher, or error-classifier can be held to it, and unlike a rule in a comment it cannot be forgotten by the next round. Companion from the same round: its first attempt at the controller's self-check **put the check inside the function the plant short-circuits**, and `CI=true … selfcheck` still exited 0 — caught by the round itself, and the fix was to report from `main` the ids actually run and cross-check them against the table.

**THE DEEPEST ONE — a positive control that passes for the wrong reason, inside the rule written to reject exactly that** (#40 r8 gauntlet, 2026-08-01). A round built a positive control: plant a real defect, run the assertions, and treat **exit 0 as the failure**. Correct design. Its `expect` patterns were **bare script names** — `/assert-page-serves/` — which any Node stack trace supplies for free. And the control runs at step 4 while the CA file it needs is written at step 10, so the cold failure in CI is `ENOENT … caddy-root.crt`, whose trace names the script. Measured both ways: CA absent → matches, "red as required"; CA present → `ECONNREFUSED` at `TCPConnectWrap.afterConnect`, **no frame naming the script**, would not match. **The control proved the assertion was red because a file was missing, never because the deployment was.** That is precisely the case `controlProblems`' own third branch exists to reject. A twenty-line replacement of the assertion then passed every gate in the repository, the control included.

Lesson kept, and it is the general form of a class this campaign has met all day: **an expectation that matches an artifact's *identity* is satisfied by any failure that mentions it; only an expectation that matches its *behaviour* proves anything.** A control must assert *which* failure occurred, not that a failure occurred and the file's name appeared somewhere in the output. Two companions from the same review: the control printed `CONTROLS[group].length` — the table's size rather than the number actually run — so one line returning `[]` produced a false receipt naming ten controls; and the round's widened exit rule was bypassed by moving a ternary into a named function, **which the round's own shipped code does**, under a comment conceding the rule cannot read the difference.

**Process note — a round's own sentence, turned back on it one revision later** (#40 r8 gauntlet, 2026-08-01). Round 7 wrote: *"A check that skips what it cannot find is a check with a hole shaped like a working directory."* Round 8 fixed the working-directory half and left `catch { return []; }`, so `rm README.md` leaves `gate-selftest.mjs` at **exit 0, 206 cases**, with every README count claim unchecked. The hole is now shaped like a missing file. Lesson kept: **when a round coins a sentence about a failure mode, that sentence is a test to run against the fix** — the wording generalises further than the patch, and the next instance is usually one substitution away.

**Measured — the denominator was the defect, and fixing it grew the analysed file set from 79 to 127** (#26 r11, 2026-08-01). Ten rounds hardened the *shapes* a guard understood; the eleventh installed one rule — **anything the analysis did not parse is reported, never assumed clean** — with six channels (`unparsed`, `unresolved`, `unmodelled`, `computed`, `incomplete`, `excluded`) each asserted empty against the real tree. **The file set went 79 → 127: sixty percent of the guard's own subject had been invisible to it.** The runtime proof is the one to remember: with a planted `.js` route in the tree, **r10's own "finds nothing under `apps/`" test passed** while `curl` with no cookie returned all 36 seeded membership rows. Kept as the campaign's clearest demonstration that **a guard has two enumerations and hardening the wrong one can run for ten rounds without the tests noticing** — because every test was written against the shapes, and the shapes were fine.

**Process note — an import that binds nothing runs everything, and a path alias that resolves to nothing hides whatever uses it** (#26 r11, 2026-08-01). Three findings from one round, all invisible to a binding-oriented analyser. A bare `import 'x'` **binds no name and executes the module** — and a test asserted the old reading, so the wrong behaviour was pinned. The repo's `@/*` path alias resolved to nothing, so six app modules importing through it were unanalysed, including a Server Action calling `db().query.memberships` that no check reported. And an import reaching outside the configured roots had no taint entry at all. Lesson kept: **an analysis keyed on what a statement *binds* is blind to what it *runs*, and one keyed on resolved specifiers is blind to every specifier that fails to resolve** — both must fail closed and be asserted empty, because the natural failure of a resolver is to return nothing, which reads as "nothing here". Related, and now the fifth instance of this class in the campaign: **the mutation ledger's own edit matched its own table entry**, so `edit()` had to be taught to refuse an ambiguous target.

**Positive note — proving the strong fix impossible is a better outcome than shipping a weaker one** (#22 r8, 2026-08-01). The guard defeated by a SQL comment had two honest options: narrow the claim, or replace it with something a caller cannot author. The round **took the second seriously and proved it empty** — every candidate unforgeable token (a GUC, an advisory-lock token, a temp witness, an unpredictable nonce) is mintable by a bare caller in a bare `DO` block with no CREATE privilege, because *checking which nonce is valid requires a store the caller cannot write, and caller and function are necessarily the same session and role.* The argument closes from the privilege side: a non-owner role gets `permission denied` on the exploit while the SECURITY DEFINER door still opens for it, **so the check's discrimination against an author is exactly zero and no rewrite raises it.** It then kept the check for the one accident the lock half cannot see, deleted both false sentences including the one inside the refusal message, and put **the argument rather than the conclusion** in the migration header. Lesson kept: **an impossibility proof is a shippable result** — it converts an open defect into a known, bounded, argued limitation, and it stops the next three rounds from attempting the same rewrite. State the argument in source, because a conclusion without it invites exactly that.

**Process note — the same unbound-value defect surfaced in two lanes within an hour** (#22 r8 and #21, 2026-08-01). A sweep on the core lane found `proposal.proposer` is self-declared and never pinned to the recording actor. The realtime lane's self-adversarial pass then found the live exploit: `acceptance.ts` decides `self` versus `third_party` — whether a commitment waits for its named owner — from `proposal.proposer.userId`, **which arrived from the socket bound to nothing**, so Alice can stage a commitment owned by Bob while claiming to be Bob and the confirmation is never demanded. Kept as confirmation that the standing rule is load-bearing rather than theoretical: **before writing a check, name who controls each value it reads** — and when one lane finds an unbound value, sweep the others for the same field before a reviewer does.

**Process note — a sampled search must report what it did not expand, and depth is a coverage axis with its own number** (#10 r13, 2026-08-01). The round rebuilt its driver to **bring a control into existence before clicking it** — switch room, force the card open, open the receipt — so a defect that had needed five writes became reachable in two, and it stabilised a denominator that had oscillated (225 or 226) by taking the **union over every state reached**: 230 controls. It then reported the shape of its own search honestly: complete breadth at depths 1–2 (701 transitions), depths 3–5 sampled with budget 5, **476 states reached and 354 never expanded.** Lesson kept: **a state-space search states its depth, its branching budget, and the count it did not expand** — "no violations found" over a sampled frontier is a claim about the sample. The payoff was immediate and is the argument for the discipline: the deeper instrument found a real defect at **depth 4 that nothing had previously been deep enough to reach**, and separately found that the *previous* round's headline mutation could not flip a predicate — live on r12, **308 times at depth 2**, invisible to every instrument that existed before this one.

**Process note — "could not have found it" is a claim about capability; "could not try" is the true one** (#10 r13, 2026-08-01). The new enumerator's own header asserted that the previous round's driver **could not have found** the defect being fixed. Its self-adversarial pass corrected this: the old driver **could** have found it, by luck — what it could not do was *try*, because it never brought the control into existence. Kept because the distinction matters whenever a round explains why its predecessor missed something: **a random or shallow instrument has non-zero probability on most defects, so the honest claim is about systematic reachability, not possibility.** Overstating a predecessor's blindness inflates the new instrument's credit and hides that the old one's failure was a coverage gap rather than an incapacity. Three other self-caught defects in the same pass are worth the same note: a first fix that **cried wolf** (one violation class became nine, five of them true sentences) was narrowed and re-measured rather than shipped, and a novelty ranking in the frontier turned out to be **dead code** reading a set after the level had already marked everything seen.

**THE DETECTOR RULE — validate a new instrument against the artifact it was written to catch, and check the count, not that it fires** (#39 r7, 2026-08-01). A round replaced a self-supplied denominator with a real sweep over every printed string, and **shipped it with the same class of hole twice**: a type narrowing asking whether a value *can be* a string, of values that *hold* one. It found this by running the new sweep against the **previous, known-bad** revision — where it reported **53 findings and 68 were due, and the fifteen it missed contained all four sinks it had been written for.** A detector that fires is not a detector that works; only the count against a known population distinguishes them. Lesson kept: **every new instrument runs against the version it was built to catch, with an independently derived expected count** — "it flags the reported defect" is the weakest possible evidence, since the reported defect is the one case you had in hand. Corollary from the same round: the instrument also had the *opposite* failure mode in an earlier revision, over-refusing until captions were forced through a voice door that rejected the word "says" — **so measure false positives against a known-good population too**, or a new checker simply relocates the defect it was written to end.

**Process note — the wrong mutation proves the wrong thing** (#39 r7, 2026-08-01). A mutation guarding "no row that carries page-authored words has a field a renderer could put a name in" added a **required** property and required `tsc` to fail. That proves adding a required property breaks the fixtures — nothing more. **A compiler cannot refuse an optional one**, which is exactly the shape the rule needed to forbid, and the field that falsified the claim was optional. Lesson kept: **a mutation must be the shape the rule forbids, not merely a shape the compiler rejects** — when a guard's claim is about what *can exist*, mutate toward the permissive case, because the restrictive one fails for reasons unrelated to the guard.

**Positive note — the structural rule self-corrected, which is the whole reason to prefer it** (#40 r8, 2026-08-01). A reviewer found `child-verdict.mjs` sitting at **2 importers**, one under the registry's ≥3 threshold, holding the entire CAUGHT-versus-crashed distinction with no row and no outside witness — the exact shape the registry forbids. The round did not special-case it. It built a positive-control file for an unrelated defect, that file imported `child-verdict.mjs`, the count became 3, **and the existing rule demanded the row without anyone remembering to add it.** Kept as the measured payoff of a principle recorded earlier the same day: **when a defect's root cause is a structural change, key the rule on the structure — a sweep is dated the moment it lands, and a rule keeps working while you are thinking about something else.** Two shipped files also moved to satisfy the widened scope rather than being exempted from it, which is the other half of the same discipline.

**Positive note — attacking a new mechanism's scope sentence found a defect inside the same round** (#40 r8, 2026-08-01). The round's organizing instruction was *write each rule's scope down as a sentence and attack the sentence*.
