It reports that **every defect but one came from doing that** — and then applied it to its own newly built positive control, whose scope sentence revealed that the covered set was *declared in a table* rather than *derived from the job's steps*. Deriving it immediately surfaced an uncontrolled assertion (`assert-migration-image`). Kept because it closes a loop this campaign opened three rounds earlier with "a claim about what a mechanism covers must be generated by the mechanism, not authored beside it": **the cheapest time to apply that rule is to the mechanism you are writing right now**, and doing so caught the omission before a reviewer had to.

**Process note — a guard that reads text the caller can author is not a guard, and a better pattern will not save it** (#22 r7 gauntlet, 2026-08-01). A migration'"'"'s append guard tests `position('function ' || v_expected || ' line ' in v_context)` against `PG_CONTEXT`, which carries the **verbatim SQL statement text of every caller frame**. So a bare `DO` block containing the expected frame label **in a comment inside its own INSERT** lands a row without calling the append function — control and exploit differing by one comment. Line-anchoring does not help: `PG_CONTEXT` echoes embedded newlines, so a caller can inject a byte-identical line. The critic'"'"'s framing is the lesson: **the instrument is wrong, not the pattern.** And the migration'"'"'s stated residual — "what is left costs CREATE on schema `public`" — costs no CREATE anywhere. Kept as the sharpest statement of a class this campaign has met all day: **before hardening a check, ask what its input is made of and who wrote it.** Text a caller authors can be made to say anything; the only fixes are to stop claiming the guard binds, or to read something the caller cannot compose.

**Positive note — the critic measured what the builder honestly declined to** (#22 r7 gauntlet, 2026-08-01). The round shipped with 48 of 71 mutants declared unmeasured, having refused to adopt a reviewer'"'"'s run. The reviewer then **discarded its own first result as unsound** (it had shared a database), re-derived the unit half in a private tree with no database involved at all, and re-ran the integration half against a Postgres it created, monitoring `pg_stat_activity` every 30s for foreign connections across a 45-minute run — zero. **71/71 from two runs neither of which shared anything.** Kept because it closes the independence question cleanly: **the number the builder must not borrow is exactly the number the reviewer should produce.** Refusing to claim it did not lose the measurement; it moved it to the side of the boundary where it counts as evidence.

**THE WORST INSTANCE — the proposer chooses which rule judges it** (#21, grok sweep, 2026-08-01). Asked to sweep for checks reading proposer-controlled values, a foreign-lineage pass found something larger than the finding that prompted the sweep. Acceptance is a per-type matrix — claims and open questions may auto-accept, commitments and decisions never — and **`proposal.type` is supplied by the proposer.** Same room, same body, same quote, same author: as a `commitment` it is `pending / never_auto_accepts`; as a `claim` it **auto-accepts**. As an `open_question` it clears at confidence 0.65 where a claim would sit in the theta band. The floor twin in `authority.ts` reads the same field, so both enforcement points consult the value being laundered. Lesson kept: **a rule table selected by an attacker-supplied key is not a rule table — it is a menu.** When a check dispatches on a value, ask who chose the value before asking whether the branch is right; the branches were all correct here.

**And the meta-lesson, which is mine.** I adjudicated this exact gap on this exact ticket earlier the same day — *"a receipt certifies provenance; it says nothing about what kind of act the words were"* — named two ways to discharge it, and **did not require either in the next round's brief.** An identified, written-down gap that is not converted into a required change is indistinguishable from an unidentified one, except that the record shows you knew. **When an adjudication names a discharge, the discharge belongs in the very next brief with a deadline, or the adjudication is a note.** Related finding in the same sweep, same shape: `findDuplicate` tests citation overlap and equal type, so re-citing a *different* message carrying the identical sentence, or re-labelling the type, mints a second accepted object for one reading.

**THE SHARPEST FORM YET — a guard whose input is supplied by the party it constrains is not a guard** (#21 r6 gauntlet, 2026-08-01). Round 6 added a gate refusing acceptance when the correction scan "read nothing it did not already control". It tests whether some uncited message sits after the **earliest** citation — and the proposal's `provenance` array is entirely model-controlled. So citing one extra *earlier* message turns the gate off: `["msg_c"]` refuses, `["msg_a","msg_c"]` **auto-accepts**, same room, same window, same words, same author, and the reducer agrees (`applied_with_issue` versus `applied`, object landed). Both enforcement points fall together because they share one implementation, exactly as that file scopes honestly. **And it is not an attack** — an ordinary extraction citing the two messages a reading was drawn from, in a window ending at the newest message, hits it on the first try. Lesson kept, and it generalises every scope lesson recorded today: **before writing a check, name who controls each value it reads.** A rule over an attacker-supplied parameter measures the attacker's cooperation. This is the same defect as the earlier custody-chain finding — harden the hop, and the adversary moves to the one that selects the hop — now at its most concentrated, because here the selection *is* the input.

**Process note — the tests carrying a round's central claim were the two that do not reliably finish** (#21 r6 gauntlet, 2026-08-01). `pnpm test` on a pristine copy: six consecutive runs gave **exit 1, 1, 0, 1, 0, 0**. The failures are always the same two — the brute force proving `orderedTokens` is total, and the property that a refusal always names a difference — both sitting on vitest's 5000 ms default while the code-point loop measures **5277 ms on an idle machine**. The round's whole evidentiary posture is *we measured it rather than argued it*, and the measurements are the part that times out; a slower box fails always, and the first green was luck. Lesson kept: **a test that carries a claim needs an explicit timeout and a measured margin** — a proof that only passes when the machine is fast is not a proof, and the failure mode is a red that looks like flakiness and gets re-run. Collateral worth copying: the committed ledger stopped reproducing (15 rows' failing-lists drifted under parallel load), and the critic **checked whether that could inflate a catch** — the flaky titles appear in no `catches` array, so the 104/104 verdict stands and only the file's auditability promise breaks. That is the right way to bound a flake's blast radius rather than dismissing or panicking about it.

**Process note — a mutation runner rewrites the source you are reading** (#21 r6 gauntlet, 2026-08-01). `run.mjs --check` mutates `packages/core/src/*.ts` **in place** and rewrites `RESULTS.md` even in check mode, so reading the worktree while it runs returns a mutant rather than the code — the critic caught itself doing exactly that in its first ten minutes, and a sibling lane had a green integration gate taken against a schema another run was mutating. Lesson kept: **review from `git archive HEAD` or a copy, never the live worktree, whenever any harness in the repo mutates in place** — and say so in the artifact, because the person most likely to be misled is the next reviewer.

**THE CONVERGENT LESSON — two lanes, one day: the rule was sound and its scope was the defect** (#40 r7 and #26 r10 gauntlets, 2026-08-01). Deployment round 7 built a genuine allowlist over the exit *value*, correct within its scope, and **scoped it to the guard body** — leaving `if (process.env.CI !== undefined) process.exit(0);` at module top level outside every rule, silencing 184 gate cases and 197 policy mutations for one line. Auth round 10 re-derived its enumeration from the expression grammar, correct within its scope, and **never re-derived which files were in the graph**, so a `.js` route served every membership in the database to an unauthenticated request. Different lanes, different reviewers, same shape: **the artifact was hardened *inside* a boundary nobody had examined the edges of.** Lesson kept, and it is the sharpest general form this campaign has produced: **whenever you write a rule, write down its scope as a separate sentence and attack that sentence.** "Governs the guard body" and "parses `.ts` files" are the defects; neither is visible while you are reasoning about what happens *within* them. A third instance in the same review: `sharedModuleProblems` keys `described` on `definedIn`, so a module clears the rule with a row for *any one* export — the rule is right, its unit of application is wrong.

**Process note — a threshold's edge is occupied by the thing the threshold was written for** (#40 r7 gauntlet, 2026-08-01). The registry requires a row for any module under `scripts/ci/` with **≥3 importers**. Measured importer counts: 19, 15, 15, 5, four at 4, two at 3 — and **`child-verdict.mjs` at 2, with no row.** Its `notAVerdict` is the entire CAUGHT-versus-crashed distinction for the mutation ledger; it is the *fix for the round-5 defect*. Its only witness is inside `scripts/` — sole enforcer, sole exception, one importer under the line, which is exactly the shape the registry exists to forbid. Lesson kept: **a threshold chosen to catch today's instances sits immediately above tomorrow's**, and the item most likely to be just under it is the one recently extracted to fix the last defect. When a rule uses a count, check what is at count−1 and say why it is safe there.

**Process note — the registry made the shared verdict machinery honest and nothing required an assertion to assert** (#40 r7 gauntlet, 2026-08-01). Replacing an entire assertion script with three lines — import `report`, call `report('assert-page-serves')` — prints `passed.` and exits 0 **with no stack running**, and every gate stays green. This is the deploy job's largest assertion, the one produced by the `/_next/static` lesson, reduced to a no-op by one file replacement. Lesson kept: **verifying that shared reporting machinery is used correctly says nothing about whether anything was checked before reporting** — an assertion script needs a rule that it contains an assertion, or better, a positive control the job itself runs (break the thing, require the red).

**Process note — an allowlist whose every entry has been individually broken should be deleted, not repaired** (#21 r6, 2026-08-01). `droppableTokens` began as four entries, each justified in prose. Over successive rounds reviewers broke them one at a time on the same shape; r6's reviewer took the last one — `.`, enforced by a `Set.has` over a character, so ``Load `.env` …`` bore ``Load `env` …`` and folded. The round's response was the right one: **the set is now empty and the mechanism is gone.** `borne` is literally `normalizeForReceipt(quote) === normalizeForReceipt(statement)`, asserted as an equivalence over 160,000 pairs. Lesson kept: **when every element of an allowlist has fallen individually, the defect is the existence of the list** — stop defending entries and ask what the comparison would be with none. Same family as "delete the field rather than guard it", reached by attrition rather than by insight.

**Positive note — make the derivation lossless so the comparison is exact by construction** (#21 r6, 2026-08-01). The root defect was that acceptance compared *tokens* while a careful normalizer preserved *text*, so token equality did not mean text equality. The fix is not a longer token rule: `orderedTokens` is now **exhaustive** (`WORD | [\s\S]`), and `orderedTokens(t).join('') === normalizeForReceipt(t)` is brute-forced over U+0000–U+10FFFF. Two equal token streams are two equal texts by construction, and the class cannot recur. Lesson kept: **when a comparison runs over a derived representation, make the derivation provably lossless rather than reasoning about what it drops** — a totality proof retires every future "what about this character" in one line. The same round also **separated verdict from diagnosis**, because "until now the diff *was* the verdict": the machinery that explains a refusal must not be the machinery that decides it, or every improvement to the explanation silently changes the outcome.

**Process note — enumerating causes was falsified by brute force three times in one round** (#21 r6, 2026-08-01). Six self-adversarial passes ran, each breaking the last, the final two changing nothing — and **three of them were the same mistake**: the round enumerated the causes of a spacing-only refusal, and a brute-force sweep falsified the list, three separate times. It now states the fact instead of the enumeration. Kept as the cheapest possible demonstration of this campaign's central rule: **if the domain is finite and small enough to sweep, sweep it — a hand-enumerated cause list is a hypothesis, and one loop refutes it faster than a reviewer will.** Also recorded: **codex was blocked by OpenAI's moderation filter on both the literal prompt and a softened one**, producing no report, so this round has one foreign lineage rather than two and the receipt says so. That filter is now a known property of shell-bypass and text-normalisation subject matter in this repo, not an intermittent failure — route those artifacts to grok and to executing Claude critics.

**THE AUTH LANE'S ACTUAL DEFECT — six rounds re-derived the grammar and nobody re-derived the denominator** (#26 r10 gauntlet, 2026-08-01). Two blind critics, different lineages, found escapes in the same guard by different routes, and both are enumeration failures rather than logic failures. codex found a `TaggedTemplateExpression` with no case, falling through to `false`. The executing Claude critic found something larger: the analyzer's stated invariant covers "any specifier shape, any re-export chain, any wrapper" — and **never re-derived which files are in the graph at all.** `SOURCE = /\.(?:tsx?|mts|cts)$/` skips `.js/.jsx/.mjs/.cjs` while `allowJs: true` is set and Next serves `route.js` as a route; `DEFAULT_SKIP` drops any directory named `test` or `build` *anywhere*, including the App Router's own `app/test/`; and a specifier resolving outside `graphRoots` is **waved through silently**. Demonstrated end to end: a planted `app/leaky/route.js` served by `next dev` returned **every room membership in the database, across all workspaces, to an unauthenticated `curl`**, while the byte-identical `.ts` control was caught. Lesson kept: **when a guard's claim is universal, it has two enumerations — the shapes it understands and the inputs it looks at — and hardening the first while leaving the second unexamined is the whole defect.** The unifying fix is the rule the same file already states for unresolvable subpaths and violates everywhere else: **fail closed on anything the analysis did not parse.** One rule covers an unmodelled node type, an unparsed extension, a skipped directory and an unresolved specifier — "we could not tell" must never render as "it was fine". Note also what this cost to find: the runtime boundary held **every** probe (28 raw WS upgrade lines, production boot refusals, Server Action CSRF, unicode confusables, schema premises read from a live database, full revocation interleaving against real containers) — the door was sound and the guard on it was not.

**Process note — a test whose assertion is a tautology of its own definition** (#26 r10 gauntlet, 2026-08-01). A test titled *"is the single list of commands — nothing is authorized off-table"* asserts `Object.keys(commandPolicy).every(c => isCommand(c))`, and `isCommand` is **defined** as `Object.hasOwn(commandPolicy, value)` — so it holds for every possible table and every possible implementation. Proven by replacing `isCommand` with `typeof value === 'string'` (fail-open: every string is a command) and watching that test still pass alone. The property it names *is* covered, by a different test three above it. Lesson kept: **when a predicate and the collection it tests are defined in terms of each other, an assertion relating them carries no information** — the check to write is against an independently derived list, and the way to find these is to replace the predicate with a fail-open stub and see which tests still pass.

**Process note — a subtractive round is not assertion-neutral, and "I added no new claims" is itself a claim** (#44 r4 gauntlet, 2026-08-01). The round removed 618 words, added 44 in one sanctioned expansion, and reported that no new assertion about the code appears anywhere. Its auditor found **six new assertions** — all of which happen to check out, and one of which, in the most-read sentence in the file, enumerates **four** object types where the document twice states five. Lesson kept: **rewriting a sentence shorter is writing a new sentence**; a prune's diff must be read for what it *says*, not only for what it removes, and a claim of assertion-neutrality is verified the same way any other claim is. Corollary the same review makes concrete: **a cut can leave a sentence that is still true and no longer covers the reader's situation.** The surviving credentials rule ("an unset `NODE_ENV` is treated as production") is accurate, and the README's own boot instructions tell the reader to `cp .env.example .env`, whose line 37 sets `NODE_ENV=development` — the one case the survivor does not address, with the explanation of why that is safe deleted along with the paragraph. Third prune in this campaign to under-report its cost, after one that declared none and one that declared one of two: **treat a zero-cost prune as unaudited by default.**

**Process note — a three-dot diff lies after a rebase** (#44 r4 gauntlet, 2026-08-01). `git diff r3...HEAD` on a rebased branch reports the merge-base, which here predated the file entirely, showing 540 insertions for a round that removed text. The real comparison is the two-dot `git diff r3 HEAD -- README.md` (112 insertions, 166 deletions). Lesson kept: **after any rebase, use two-dot to compare content and three-dot only to describe a branch's own history** — and when a round reports a diffstat that contradicts its narrative, suspect the command before suspecting the work.

**Positive note — a round caught itself estimating and corrected in public** (#44 r4, 2026-08-01). The round's removal ledger reported per-passage word counts. It then noticed those had been **eyeballed rather than measured**, re-measured them, found **five wrong — one by 40%** — and posted the correction as a second comment rather than quietly editing the first. Kept for two reasons. First, the specific trap: a ledger of measurements is exactly the artifact whose own numbers feel too obvious to measure, and this campaign has now been bitten by estimated-instead-of-measured counts in prose, in doctrine, and in a mutation tally. Second, the form of the correction — **a receipt that quietly changes is not a receipt**, and appending is the only honest repair.

**Positive note — a merge-cost decision made by bisecting and measuring, in both directions** (#44 r4, 2026-08-01). Two corrections had merge consequences, and the round took one and refused the other on measurement rather than principle: the `pnpm test` fix **costs** new conflicts and was taken because its string is byte-identical to what 11 of the 15 branches touching the file already write; `minio-init` was refused because it cost 11 new conflicts for text any auth or deploy merge overwrites on arrival. Both isolated by bisecting the change set and re-running `merge-tree` per hunk, with the net delta stated against a `main` control. Kept as the answer to a question this campaign kept meeting as a blanket rule: **"does this conflict?" is per-hunk and measurable, and "leave the file alone" is a proxy that costs real fixes.**

**Process note — "derived from the grammar" is itself a claim, and a hand-written case list wearing a better name** (#26 r10 gauntlet, 2026-08-01). Six auth rounds each closed "the sixth escape" by adding the shape the last critic found. Round 10 said it had broken the cycle by **re-deriving the limits from the grammar rather than from the code**. A blind foreign-lineage reviewer found the seventh in one pass: the analyzer has no `TaggedTemplateExpression` case and falls through to `false`, so `const db = tag\`ignored\`` launders a database handle past a boundary test whose whole assertion is that no app file names `memberships`. The fixture was injected, **343 tests passed**, and it was removed again. Lesson kept: **a claim to have enumerated a grammar is checkable against that grammar — so check it, or make the enumeration unnecessary.** The structural answer, which the reviewer states itself, is to **fail closed on an unmodelled node type** rather than to add a case: derive the handled set from the AST node union and make anything outside it an error, so the next unlisted form is refused by a clause that never heard of it. Same shape as the launcher allowlist and the ≥3-importer rule — the fix is a rule keyed on the structure, never another entry in a list. Also found, and the same class one layer down: a validator whose comment says arrays and lists are rejected returns `"member,admin"` for `['member','admin']`.

**Positive note — the diagnosed CLI fix is confirmed** (tooling, 2026-08-01). After three verdictless codex runs across the campaign, giving the relay a **prepared writable tree** (clone, install, build, *then* hand it over frozen) produced a complete 14,320-line run ending in a clear verdict, with the model confirmed from codex's own header. The earlier failures were environmental in three distinct ways — a network-isolated sandbox failing `pnpm install`, a tree edited while the reviewer read it, and a read-only sandbox denying the writes and spawns the suite needs — and all three are now handled by the same preparation step. This run also correctly labelled its WebSocket test failures as **environmental `EPERM` binding `127.0.0.1`, not assertion failures**, which is the discipline that makes the rest of its report usable.

**Positive note — the fix for "centralising concentrates failure" is a rule that detects centralisation** (#40 r7, 2026-08-01). The critical defect was that replacing fifteen guard copies with one shared predicate created a single point of disarmament the outside witness never called. The obvious fix is to register that predicate. The round did better: **`sharedModuleProblems` fails the build when any module under `scripts/ci/` has ≥3 importers and no registry row** — so the *next* module that becomes shared is caught on the day it becomes shared, not in the round after it is exploited. Registry 5 → 14 rows, with an independent audit putting five blanket stubs through every row returning 14/14 sensitive. Lesson kept: **when a defect's root cause is a structural change (de-duplication, extraction, a new indirection), the fix is a rule keyed on that structure, not a sweep of the instances it has produced so far.** A sweep is dated the moment it lands; a rule about ≥3 importers is not. Its own self-adversarial pass then found `sharedModuleProblems` **checking a name where a location was meant — inside the sweep written to generalise a defect whose root cause was a name where a location was meant**, which is the sixth instance today of a round committing the class it names and by now simply the expected cost of writing a fix.

**Positive note — a flake diagnosed to its cause instead of retried** (#40 r7, 2026-08-01). A 1-in-5 full-suite failure looked like an assertion failure and was **a 5-second Vitest timeout wearing an assertion's clothes**. Rather than raise the timeout or re-run until green, the round fixed the cause — a content-addressed parse memo took the warm path from 2.2s to 0.4s — then demonstrated eight consecutive clean runs. Kept because the alternative is how a suite becomes untrustworthy: **a retried flake is an unmeasured failure, and a raised timeout is a claim that nothing got slower.** Also worth copying from this round: the deploy job was run twice on live containers, whole and deliberately broken, and the *unexercised* set was reported explicitly — including that host ports were remapped because the machine already publishes 80/9000/5432 for an unrelated project, so the run was not on the shipped port configuration.

**Process note — a compile-time assert is code, and an inert one is worse than none** (#22 r7, 2026-08-01). The design decision "no durable frame can join the ephemeral bus" was backed by `EphemeralFrame['type'] extends 'event' | 'catchup' ? never : true`, beside a comment stating that `tsc` refuses the build. It resolves to `true` **whether or not `'event'` joins the union** — a union only `extends` a target when *every* member does, and this one is not a naked type parameter so it does not distribute. The guarantee guaranteed nothing. Lesson kept: **apply the campaign's synthesis to types — flip the input and check the output moves.** Every type-level assert needs a companion `@ts-expect-error` fixture proving it *fails* on the violating shape; without one it is a comment with syntax. Same class as an audit whose selector matches nothing and a mutation ledger crediting an already-red catcher, at the one layer that looks the most authoritative because the compiler is involved.

**Positive note — an incomplete tally reported as incomplete, in a banner rather than a footnote** (#22 r7, 2026-08-01). The mutant ledger grew 58 → 71; a full `--check` takes hours and none finished on the final tree. The round measured all 15 new or retargeted mutants individually (15/15), the first 8 of a full pass, and the drift guard, and then wrote **"the other 48 are unmeasured on this tree, not passing on it"** into `RESULTS.md` as a banner — rather than leaving a 58/58 table sitting beside a 71-entry ledger, which is exactly how a stale number becomes a claim. Kept as the model for what "unexercised is a third verdict" looks like in practice: **the artifact itself has to say so, in the place a reader would otherwise take the number from.** A caveat in a receipt nobody re-reads does not survive; a banner in the file does.

**THE NEXT LAYER — enumeration has a depth, and a control that does not exist yet is outside the denominator** (#10 r12 gauntlet, 2026-08-01). Round 12 replaced randomisation with enumeration and measured 1009-to-0 in its favour. Its critic then found two live invariant violations **at depth 5**: `Ask justin instead` only *exists* after two prior writes, because until other items are cleared the object renders as a compact row and the second button is never painted. The round's own repro of the identical round trip is clean for exactly that reason. The critic wrote an independent depth-3 sequence enumerator — 233 paths, 0 findings — and states plainly that these need depth 5. Lesson kept: **per-control enumeration covers the controls that exist at enumeration time; a denominator computed from one render is not a denominator over reachable states.** Enumerate *sequences* to a stated depth, report the depth, and treat "controls driven" and "states reached" as different numbers. Two companions from the same review: the driver tagged controls only on click-family events, so **three keyboard write paths were outside the 226-key denominator entirely**; and the denominator is render-dependent rather than structural — the same artifact enumerated 225 on one run and 226 on another.

**Process note — a prune must be audited for cost the same way a fix is audited for coverage** (#10 r12 gauntlet, 2026-08-01). The round declared exactly one cost of its pruning (a deleted record scan), which was real — confirmed by driving. It had **two**. Removing a reconciliation clause while *also* changing one of the two predicates it reconciled left **two "unverified" counts over the same thirteen records disagreeing on screen, 8 versus 7**, with no scope difference to explain it. Separately, an overlap caveat was not removed but **hidden** — moved into a `title` attribute gated on whether anything is owed — so an objective with 100% overlap and nothing owed now carries no overlap sentence at all: **the caveat is conditioned on the wrong predicate.** Lesson kept: **when you delete an explanation, re-verify the thing it was explaining is still true; when you move one, check the condition you moved it behind is the condition it was about.** Upgrades the earlier rule — a prune that reports no cost has not been audited, and a prune that reports one cost has not necessarily found them all.

**Process note — three consecutive rounds shipped a real mechanism with its reach described from memory** (#10 r10-r12, 2026-08-01). r10's doctrine claimed a derived surface list while shipping a hand-written one. r11 built the derivation and wrote a new comment citing an example the walk could not see. r12 built an enumerator of uncovered claims and documented it with examples it **structurally cannot produce** — control labels excluded by the walker's own `closest("[data-obj]")` guard, and bare integers carrying no vocabulary. Each time the mechanism was real and the sentence about its reach was written by hand. Lesson kept, and it needs a structural answer rather than a fourth round: **a claim about what a mechanism covers must be generated by the mechanism, not authored beside it** — print the actual set, commit the output, and let the documentation cite that file. Any hand-written "for example, this now catches X" is a claim awaiting falsification, and in this repo it has been falsified three times running.

**Process note — a quiet lane is indistinguishable from a finished one, and the quietest was the highest-risk** (#26, 2026-08-01). A scheduled board reconciliation found **#26 (auth) sitting at round 10, pushed at 16:04, with its gauntlet never dispatched** — hours of silence on the single highest-consequence boundary in the project, where a bypass is full access to another workspace's conversation rather than an information leak. Third stalled lane found today, and the mechanism is the same each time: **the orchestrator's attention follows notifications, and a lane that has stopped producing them looks exactly like a lane that is done.** The louder lanes crowded it out precisely because they were working. Lesson kept: **reconcile every lane against the tracker on a schedule, not when something feels wrong** — and weight the sweep by consequence, because the lane you are least likely to notice going quiet is the one nobody is actively arguing about. Both axes are now dispatched against it, one executing Claude critic with a prepared tree and one codex relay with a writable workspace.

**Process note — parsing proves well-formedness, never authenticity** (#22 r7, 2026-08-01). Round 7 replaced casts with schema parsing on both `LISTEN` channels, the client socket, and the client's `localStorage` journal — the right fix for a bus that broadcast whatever arrived. Its own self-review then made the sharper point: **a parsed journal entry is well-formed and still not authentic.** A forged entry that satisfies the schema is exactly as acceptable as a real one; validation constrains *shape*, and the question at a trust boundary is *origin*. Closed for the unbounded case by discarding a cursor the server's own head contradicts — i.e. by checking the claim against an authority rather than against a type. Lesson kept: **schema validation is a parser, not a guard.** Whenever parsing is introduced as a security fix, name separately what establishes provenance; the two are routinely conflated because both reject bad input. Same family as the campaign's synthesis — structure proves derivation, and something else has to prove the thing actually came from where it says.

**Process note — the round whose thesis was "an unheld sentence is an unwritten test" shipped four unheld sentences** (#22 r7, 2026-08-01). The round existed largely to pin prose claims that nothing tested, and pinned ten. Its two blind foreign-lineage reviewers then broke **four sentences of its own**: an **inert** type-level assert, an overstated "exactly one route" claim, a spoofable-origin claim, and a false "EXECUTE is granted to the application role only" **whose test passed for the wrong reason twice.** It also found a fail-open in its own new guard — `position(x in NULL)` is NULL, and `IF NULL` takes the false branch. Lesson kept, now the fourth instance today and no longer a coincidence: **a round is at its least sceptical about the sentences it writes while fixing sentences.** The self-adversarial pass must read the round's own new prose as adversarially as the code, and "a test that passes for the wrong reason" deserves its own check — passing is not evidence unless the failure mode was exercised.

**Process note — the door was hardened; the wall beside it was not** (#22 realtime r7, 2026-08-01). Six rounds went into making `core_events` unforgeable — call-stack guard, kind-discriminated room CHECK, derived receipt window, subject-room resolution — and the r6 critic reached durable client state without touching the table at all. `NOTIFY` needs no privilege in Postgres, the ephemeral bus cast its payload, the server fanned it out with no validation, and the client's `applyEntry` **commits to its own journal and advances `lastSeq`** — so a forged `event` frame published by anything that can open a connection made the real event at that position permanently unreachable, with `SELECT count(*) FROM core_events` still 0. Lesson kept: **"durable state" is every store a reader believes after a restart, not the one with the constraints on it** — enumerate them (the ledger, the client's journal, every channel feeding either) and give each the same door. The corollary that made the fix small: an ephemeral bus that *can* carry a durable frame is the design error, so the fix is a closed alphabet (`presence | typing`, with an assert that no durable frame joins it), not better validation of an open one.

**Process note — the sentence was the guarantee** (#22 realtime r7, 2026-08-01). `0003` conceded in prose that a role with CREATE could define its own `atrium_append_core_event` and satisfy the call-stack check. `0007` then wrote "in the only function that can put a row in this table" and hung the whole room-less-kind guarantee on it. The r6 critic executed the concession: PL/pgSQL labels a stack frame with `format_procedure` **at compile time**, which omits the schema qualifier when that schema is on `search_path` at that moment and caches it for the session — so `evil2.atrium_append_core_event`, compiled with `evil2` on the path, filed a rejection of room A's proposal into room B at `room_seq` 9999 dated 2020. Fixed twice over, because either half alone leaves the other's sentence load-bearing: the rules moved onto the table as row triggers, so they bind every caller; and the guard now compares against the schema-qualified signature it reads back out of the catalog, which no function outside `public` can render. Lesson kept: **a guarantee may not rest on a claim about who the caller is — put it where the write happens, or it is a comment.** And the smaller one that made the guard honest: derive the string you compare against from the same source the other side derived it from, and treat NULL on *either* side as a refusal — `IF position(x in y) = 0` is a fail-open the moment either operand goes missing.

**Process note — a comment's justification ages independently of the line it justifies** (#22 realtime r7, 2026-08-01). Swept for prose safety claims, the round found ten that nothing held — including one that had become *false* as the code around it changed: `IS NOT DISTINCT FROM` was defended by an argument about absent keys that a later clause had since made redundant, so the comment described a constraint that no longer existed. Others: a fails-closed wrapper no test could distinguish from its absence, an exhaustiveness assertion comparing a TypeScript constant against itself while claiming to read the database, a containment argument naming a rejection reason the reducer does not have, and a "no caller can put a row here that a replay would refuse to fold" that its own next paragraph refutes. Lesson kept: **re-read a comment's justification whenever the code it justifies moves, and prefer a pin to a paragraph.** Technique worth reusing: where a CHECK's behaviour is invisible from outside (NULL and `false` both pass), read `pg_get_constraintdef` back and *evaluate the deployed expression* against a fabricated row — it turns "fails closed" from prose into `expected null to be false`.

**Positive note — the campaign's first PASS, and what made it believable** (#44 r3, 2026-08-01). A blind fact-checker returned **PASS with no overclaim found** on the public-facing README, after two consecutive rounds that were each failed for exactly that. What makes the verdict worth trusting is the audit trail rather than the word: it **drove the shipped reducer** for all six per-type authority rows and all five actor-floor gates in both directions rather than reading `authority.ts`; recomputed every number in the document (test counts per package, three corpora, the token drift at 0-of-51 and 19-of-51, 51 variables per theme by parsing both blocks); opened every citation including a line range an earlier round had got wrong; verified all seven honest-state lanes with `merge-base --is-ancestor`; and **grepped all 70 remote branches** for LLM API usage to check a negative claim. Kept as the standard: **a PASS is only as good as the list of things the passer actually executed** — demand that list, and a verdict with no attack record is worth nothing regardless of which way it points. Note also the one defect it found ran the *safe* direction (a stale note that understates what exists), which is the posture the document was written to have.

**Process note — a builder that dies mid-run leaves recoverable state; check before restarting** (#40 r7, 2026-08-01). A round terminated on a mid-response API error. The instinct is to re-dispatch from the brief. Checking first showed **one commit already landed** on top of the base plus uncommitted work across six files, and the agent's context was resumable by message — so it picked up from its own last sentence (*"a contract could satisfy both runs by comparing identity rather than behaviour"*) instead of re-deriving several hours of work. Lesson kept: **on any agent failure, inspect the worktree and the remote before deciding to restart** — commits, uncommitted diff, and whether anything was pushed — and resume rather than re-dispatch when the context survives. Also worth passing back on resume: the exact state you verified, so the agent does not have to rediscover it, and a warning about untracked byproducts (a deploy run had left `built-images.json`, `caddy-root.crt` and `deploy-run.sh` in the tree, which a `git add -A` would have committed). Related good practice observed in that round: **every commit message named the defect class it closed** — "the file that enforced the guard was the only one exempt from it", "a registry row that reads nothing satisfies the graph rule for free", "presence is not use: a call that cannot run is not an invoker" — which made the resume brief writable in one pass and gives the round its own checklist to grep its diff against.

**Process note — the audit found four gaps, not one, and they share a cause** (orchestration, 2026-08-01). Having discovered one missing gauntlet receipt, I audited every lane's build-comment/receipt pairing instead of assuming it was isolated. **Three more round-6 receipts were missing (#22, #39, #40) and #44 was stalled entirely** — round 3 pushed, its gauntlet never dispatched. Every gap came from the same stretch, and the cause is one behaviour: once the loop got fast enough that adjudicating flowed straight into dispatching the next round, the receipt — the only step with no downstream consumer waiting on it — was the one that fell out. Lesson kept: **the step with no consumer is the step that silently stops happening, and it is usually the record.** Two guards now: write the receipt before dispatching the next round, and periodically diff every lane's comment history for build/receipt pairing rather than trusting the felt sense of having kept up. All four are repaired, each posted late and **labelled as late** — a gap in an audit trail is a defect, and a quietly-repaired one is worse than an admitted one.

**Process note — recording a lesson is not the same as leaving a receipt, and the richest reviews are the ones whose receipts get skipped** (#10, 2026-08-01). Round 11's gauntlet ran, its findings were adjudicated into round 12's brief, and **no receipt was posted** — the ticket jumped from the round-11 build comment straight to round 12, leaving no record of what the review found or why the next round existed. Found only by checking the comment list before posting a different receipt. The cause is specific and worth guarding against: that review produced unusually good *process* lessons, I wrote them into this file and dispatched the next round, and **dispatching felt like completion**. Lesson kept: **the receipt is owed to the ticket, not to me — write it before dispatching the next round, and treat "I already captured the lessons elsewhere" as the warning sign it is.** The richer the review, the stronger the pull to skip it, because the value already feels banked. Posted late and labelled as late rather than backdated or folded into the next receipt; a gap in an audit trail is a defect and a quietly-repaired one is worse.

**THE MEASUREMENT — 1009 violations from enumeration, 0 from 900 random clicks, same artifact** (#10 r12, 2026-08-01). Round 11 reported "0 errors across 900 randomised real clicks" and was failed by a critic who found five console errors per render three clicks in. Round 12 replaced randomisation with enumeration: **227 control keys on r11, 222 driven, 1009 invariant violations in 5 classes from the deliberate pass and 0 from the random pass.** On r12: 226 keys, 222 driven, 0 and 0. The four undriven are named (`scope=return` filter chips, disabled in every reachable frame) rather than absorbed into a total. Kept as the campaign's definitive evidence for a rule stated many times and never measured until now: **a count of actions is not a count of paths, and random exploration does not cover sequences.** Enumerate the affordances, drive each deliberately, report the denominator and the undriven set — then randomise on top if you like. The number to quote when anyone proposes a click-total as coverage is 1009 to 0.

**Positive note — a round that pruned, and named the one thing pruning cost** (#10 r12, 2026-08-01). The first round in this campaign told to remove rather than add. It deleted three arithmetic disclaimer lines and a six-statistic trailer, **naming each removal with the round that added it** (r2, r4, r6), and added the doctrine line *"a number that needs a disclaimer is the wrong number"*. Crucially it declared a real loss instead of claiming a clean win: **deleting `last check` deleted its record scan, and the remaining `updated HH:MM` is `S.now`, not a replacement.** Kept as the model for the pruning half of the youthfulness rule — **a prune that reports no cost has not been audited**, and the honest form is to name what the removed thing was doing that nothing now does. Also of note: the round answered a doctrine gap (a friction rule keyed on a glyph rather than on reversibility-in-fact) by **building the undo** rather than by rewriting the rule to excuse its absence.

**Process note — the comment/code mismatch appeared inside the pruning commit itself** (#10 r12, 2026-08-01). The round's own self-adversarial pass found "a comment in my own pruning block saying the trailer keeps three facts while the code beside it painted two." That is the fifth distinct instance today of prose disagreeing with the code it sits next to — a code comment citing a migration that says the opposite, doctrine asserting a derivation it had not built, a README citing tickets lacking the reasoning, a docblock false about JavaScript's own `\s`, and now a comment miscounting the lines directly beneath it. Lesson kept: **every comment stating a count, a set, or a property is a test that has not been written**, and the commit that edits the code beside it is the most likely place for the two to diverge. The round corrected it in place and said what it was, which is the right form.

**THE SHARPEST LESSON OF THE CAMPAIGN — de-duplicating a check concentrates its failure mode, and the witness watched the callers instead of the callee** (#40 r6 gauntlet, 2026-08-01). Round 5's critical defect: a guard enforced only from inside its own subjects has a switch on the inside of the door. Round 6 fixed it properly — an AST parser instead of a substring scan, and a real outside witness (`packages/ci-guard`) requiring every enforcement check to have an invoker outside the files it reads. It also replaced fifteen copies of the guard with **one shared predicate** — and the witness never calls that predicate. It tests the *shape* of the guard in each caller; nothing tests the callee's *behaviour*. Result: `if (process.env.GITHUB_JOB === 'verify') return false;` — **one statement, one file — silences 358 assertions** (176 gate cases + 182 policy mutations), scoped to leave the deploy job green, with the witness passing 49/49 and lint clean. Round 5's defect cost two `&&` across two files for 316 assertions; **the fix made the blast radius larger and the cost smaller.** Lesson kept: **every refactor that replaces N copies with one shared implementation converts N independent failures into one total failure, and the tests written for the N copies do not transfer to the one.** When you centralise, the new shared thing needs its own behavioural test that day — registered, invoked from outside, with a fixture that makes it fail. Corollary, and the reason this survived: the artifact *disclosed* the risk — "replacing `main-module.mjs` itself is the loud kind of change" — and the disclosure was **measurably false**; nothing goes red. A stated limit is not a disposition, and a stated limit that is wrong about its own detectability is worse than silence.

**Process note — three defects in one round, each the round's own lesson unapplied one line away** (#40 r6 gauntlet, 2026-08-01). (1) The guard-body rule checks statement *kind* — `isEntirelyConditional` demands every statement be a branch — so `process.exit(process.env.CI === undefined ? await main() : 0)` passes, and prepending one harmless unconditional line defeats it without touching the gate. **The rule must be about the exit value, not the statement shape.** (2) The headline `ENFORCEMENT` row names `check: 'mainGuardProblems'` and wires `violate` to a *different function*, so the round's own "a check gutted to `return []` goes red" is false exactly where it matters; nothing verifies that a fixture exercises the check it is registered against. (3) "Dead call positions are refused" is a **four-shape denylist** — `if (false)`, `it.skip`, after-return, false-ternary-arm — and `while (false)` walks past it, along with fourteen more spellings including `describe.each([])`, which runs zero tests while counting every call. The same commit records the allowlist rule for the guard and denylists here. Lesson kept: **when a round names a principle, grep the diff for every place the principle applies before reporting** — three times in one commit the fix was one line from being right, and the missing line was the one the commit itself argued for.

**Positive note — the unexecuted path is now executed, and it caught the real mutation** (#40 r6 gauntlet, 2026-08-01). The `deploy` job had never run — no compose stack existed in any prior round, so its assertions were backed only by fixtures. The critic stood up real containers, ran all 15 stages green, then deleted `COPY --from=build /app/apps/web/.next/static` from the Dockerfile, rebuilt, and re-ran: every earlier gate passed on the broken image and `assert-page-serves` **exited 1** with `GET /_next/static/chunks/05rwm-ecnpalw.js returned 404, not 200`, 11 assertions failed, teardown clean. Kept because it is the strongest evidence in the campaign that the *product* half of this gate is real, and because it converted the last "reported unexercised" item in that lane into measured fact. **Standing instruction confirmed: give a critic the resources to run the thing, and it will settle in one pass what fixtures argue about for four rounds.**

**Process note — hardening one write path is worthless until you enumerate every path that reaches the same durable state** (#22 r6 gauntlet, 2026-08-01). Round 6 made the ledger's room CHECK genuinely unbreakable — a critic threw the r5 exploit, JSON nulls, numbers, arrays, empty strings, uppercase and braced UUIDs, duplicate keys, and a live `ALTER TYPE … ADD VALUE 'ninth_kind'` at it, all refused. Then it walked around it: **`NOTIFY` requires no privilege in Postgres.** A forged notification is cast rather than parsed by the bus, broadcast by the ws-server with no membership or room-ownership check, and the client's `applyEntry` **persists it to the durable journal and advances `lastSeq`** — so the real event at that sequence is permanently skipped. Executed: a client received a forged frame and diverged durably from the ledger while `core_events` held **zero rows**. Lesson kept: **a trust boundary is defined by the set of paths that reach the protected state, not by the one path you were reviewing** — enumerate every writer of every durable structure (including the client's own journal) before declaring a boundary closed. Corollary from the same review: two channels `JSON.parse(...) as T` where the ledger door parses with a schema; **a cast is an unchecked assertion wearing a type annotation.**

**Process note — a guarantee asserted in prose in two files and pinned by no test is not a guarantee** (#22 r6 gauntlet, 2026-08-01). Both `schema.ts` and the migration state that a `coalesce(…, false)` wrapper is what makes an unknown ninth event kind fail closed. Deleting the wrapper leaves **108/108 integration tests green** and the 58-mutant ledger unmoved — and the property is behaviourally real, since with it gone a ninth kind lands cross-room. A second mutation of the round's own stated design (`IS NOT DISTINCT FROM` → `=`) is likewise undetectable, and its written justification is no longer true of the shipped shape. Lesson kept: **every sentence in a comment claiming a safety property is a test that has not been written yet** — mutate each stated design decision and require something to go red, or delete the sentence. Note the shape: the ledger was sound (it runs a full green baseline per suite before mutating and excludes its own guard from scoring) and still could not see these, because **a mutation ledger only covers the mutations someone thought to list.**

**Process note — a platform's compile-time behaviour can be the bypass** (#22 r6 gauntlet, 2026-08-01). A call-stack guard checks `position('function atrium_append_core_event(' in v_context)`. The bypass is not merely "substring": PL/pgSQL builds the signature via `format_procedure` **at compile time**, which omits the schema qualifier when that schema is on `search_path` at that moment, and the compiled function is then cached for the session. Executed — create `evil2.atrium_append_core_event`, set the path before first compile, and a row lands in room B for a subject minted in room A, which is exactly the misroute the round's subject resolution exists to close. Only table CHECKs survive it. Meanwhile the migration asserts the guard runs "in the only function that can put a row in this table", and the round's whole room-less-kind guarantee rests on that sentence. Lesson kept: **when a guard depends on a platform behaviour, verify the behaviour under the conditions an attacker controls — including compilation order, caching, and search path — not under the conditions your tests happen to create.**

**Process note — the commit that names a defect class is the most likely place to commit it** (#40 r6, 2026-08-01). Round 6 fixed the critical self-exempting checker, then found **seven further defects by attacking its own fix, and every one was the round-5 class committed again inside the commit that named it**: a guard scanner checking a predicate's *basename* where it meant its *location*; a freshness bound written as a calendar constant in the same change that argued against constants; and — sharpest — an invocation graph proving **presence** one file after the author had written that presence is not use, so wrapping the outside witness's calls in `if (false)` left both self-tests exit 0 and **defeated the fix for the critical finding with four characters.** Lesson kept: **while you are writing the fix you are thinking about the old defect, which is exactly the state in which you commit it in the new code.** Make the self-adversarial pass's first target the diff of the fix itself, with the named class as the checklist — not the surrounding artifact. The general form of the resolution is the campaign's synthesis: stop proving the check *exists* and prove it *fires* — every registry row now carries a `violate` fixture the check must go red on, so a check gutted to `return []` fails regardless of how good its graph looks.

**Positive note — a lead recovered from a truncated transcript was actioned mid-flight and found the defect** (#40 r6, 2026-08-01). A codex run cut off with no verdict had, at line 6712, written up two bypass classes: guard-body reachability, and a checker graph counting syntactic calls under a dead branch. Relayed to the still-running builder, the second one landed exactly — it is the `if (false)` four-character defeat above. **A missing verdict is never a pass, a truncated transcript is not waste, and a lead is worth more delivered mid-round than filed in a receipt afterwards.** Also kept: the round declined to score the codex axis as passing since no verdict was relayed, and declined to claim the `deploy` job was exercised since no compose stack existed to run it — both correct, and both stated as unexercised rather than assumed clean.

**Process note — a third distinct cause of an unusable review: the sandbox forbids what the suite requires** (#40 r6, 2026-08-01). A codex review of the CI gate could not run `gate-selftest.mjs` (19 cases need writable `/tmp` and subprocess spawning) or vitest (Vite writes `node_modules/.vite-temp`), failing with `EROFS`/`EPERM`. Codex labelled these **environmental rather than branch defects**, which is exactly right and is why its report remains usable. That is now three separate causes with the same surface symptom of a review that produces nothing: a network-isolated sandbox failing `pnpm install`; the tree being edited while the reviewer reads it; and a read-only sandbox forbidding the writes and spawns the suite itself needs. Lesson kept: **give the reviewer a prepared, writable copy outside the repository and freeze the repository itself** — read-only must protect the artifact, not disable the tests. And require critics to label environmental failures as such; one that reports `EPERM` as a defect wastes a round, and one that hides it reports a suite as passing that never ran.

**Positive note — the transcript-recovery rule paid for itself again, via a relay that refused to fake completeness** (#40 r6, 2026-08-01). A 606 KB / 9,828-line codex run was **cut off mid-attack with no final ranked report**. The relay declined to paste it, declined to claim it had, quoted all seven of codex's own turns verbatim with line numbers, gave the file path, and stated plainly that the run was truncated. Buried at line 6712 were two bypass classes aimed squarely at the round's headline fix — *"the AST scanner checks condition shape but not guard-body reachability, and the checker graph counts syntactic calls even when they are under a dead branch"* — i.e. a correct guard whose body cannot run, and an invoker under `if (false)` satisfying a proof that the checker has an invoker. **An invoker that cannot execute is not an invoker**, which is the standing synthesis reaching the checker graph itself. Relayed to the running builder. Kept as the clearest evidence yet that **a missing verdict is never a pass and a truncated transcript is not waste** — and that a relay's job is to preserve the record faithfully, including the fact that it is incomplete.

**THE PROCESS DEFECT — eleven rounds of critics demanding disclosure produced an interface made of disclaimers; the gauntlet has a ratchet and no prune** (#10 r11 gauntlet, 2026-08-01). The critic's product verdict: the page spends the top three lines of its most valuable pane on *arithmetic disclaimers about its own counts* — a summary, a reconciliation ("4 + 8 + 1 open = 13 of 13 · awaiting you overlaps them, it does not add to them"), and a caveat about double-counting — three lines explaining why the numbers do not add, above the numbers. Its diagnosis: **"every correction added a clause, nothing ever removed one."** Each clause was individually demanded by a round that was individually right. Lesson kept, and it is about this process rather than this artifact: **a review loop that only ever adds constraints converges on a compliant artifact nobody wants.** The "stay youthful" rule already requires pruning a process rule per phase boundary; it needs a second half — **every fix round must also name what its predecessors added that can now come out**, and a gauntlet must be allowed to report "this is over-disclosed" as a defect. The honest fix here is fewer numbers, not more prose about them. Related and equally mine: the critic notes the asymmetric-friction rule partitions by a glyph nothing on the page can produce, so *every* action defaults to the reversible tier — including one that sets an assignee, clears the owner, and leaves **no control anywhere that takes it back**. A one-way door the friction doctrine cannot see because it keys on reversibility-by-glyph rather than reversibility-in-fact.

**Process note — a round shipped the detector and the defect the detector finds** (#10 r11 gauntlet, 2026-08-01). Round 11 built a completeness walk that flags un-minted claims. On the unmodified artifact, three clicks — expand an item, Reschedule, open the receipt — fire **four distinct invariant classes, five errors per render**, all in code the same round touched. The detector was right; nobody ran it on that path. The round reported "0 errors across 900 randomised real clicks", and 900 random clicks never traverse one specific 3-step sequence; a prior critic's "console clean" had simply never clicked Reschedule, which is one of only two controls offered on that item. Lesson kept: **random exploration does not cover sequences, and a count of clicks is not a count of paths.** When a round ships a new detector, the deliverable includes *driving every control the artifact offers, once, deliberately* — enumerate the affordances and walk them, then randomise on top. Third instance today of an unasserted denominator.

**Process note — the fix for "doctrine claims a derivation it has not built" reproduced the defect** (#10 r11 gauntlet, 2026-08-01). Round 10's gauntlet faulted `CONVENTIONS.md` for asserting its surface list was derived from the object's shape while shipping a hand-written list. Round 11 built the derivation — genuinely, and the critic confirms it works where it reaches — and then wrote a **new** comment citing `last check 09:07` in the pin trailer as an example of what the walk now finds. That node has no declaring ancestor and the walk provably cannot see it. Six surface families (the lens summary, the sums line, the note, the pin trailer, objective headers, rail badges and tab counts) declare no object at all, and the doctrine still says "every element that renders an object declares which object". Lesson kept: **when correcting an overclaim, the correction is the highest-risk sentence in the commit — it is written in the glow of having just done the work, and it is the sentence reviewers will trust most.** Require the replacement claim to name the mechanism that makes it true and one example the mechanism *does not* cover.

**Process note — a correct normalization that nothing consults is worse than none, because its comment becomes a guarantee about a different function** (#21 r5 gauntlet, 2026-08-01). `normalizeForReceipt` genuinely passes code spans through byte for byte, and its comment says why: *"two spaces in a password are a different password."* True — and the acceptance path never compares normalized strings. It compares `orderedTokens`, which discards **all whitespace and all standalone ASCII apostrophes**, and that comparison is the only one made between a quote and the statement minted from it. So `` Run `rm -rf / tmp/cache` `` certifies as `` Run `rm -rf /tmp/cache` `` — a different, destructive command produced by deleting a space — and `We will deploy production Friday, promise.` certifies as `We will 'deploy' production 'Friday', promise.` Lesson kept: **this is the campaign's synthesis in its purest form — the machinery was correct, and nothing used it.** When a value is carefully derived, ask which comparison actually runs; a docblock describing the careful function will be read as covering the path that ignores it. Related and equally sharp: backticks and double quotes are correctly refused as mention-markers so that a *displayed* sentence is not accepted as an *asserted* one — but the plain ASCII apostrophe is invisible to the tokenizer, so `'We will deploy production Friday.'` is accepted as an assertion, and the typographic pair survives only because `’` folds to `'` and vanishes while `‘` does not. **A guard that fails closed by luck rather than by rule is not a guard**, and the mention/use distinction has to be enforced by the same comparison that certifies, not by a sibling one.

**Process note — a rationale comment can be false about the language, not just about the code** (#21 r5 gauntlet, 2026-08-01). A docblock justifying a character-deletion class stated that "Tab, newline, vertical tab, form feed, carriage return **and NEL** are `\s`". JavaScript's `\s` does **not** include U+0085 NEL, nor U+007F, nor U+0080–U+009F. All of them are therefore deleted, fusing the words either side — `deploy\u0085production` becomes `deployproduction` and certifies clean — which is exactly the failure the comment was written to describe having fixed. The mutation guarding it repeated the same false claim in its own justification and exercised only `\t`, the one control character that *is* in `\s`. Lesson kept: **a comment asserting a language or platform property is a factual claim and must be enumerated, not reasoned about** — brute-force the codepoint range, print the set, commit the output. Four more comments in the same package were found false as written, including one still quoting an argument the round had explicitly deleted and one contradicted by another comment seventeen lines below it.

**Positive note — the mutation ledger this repo needs already exists, in another package** (#21 r5, 2026-08-01). Core's `mutants/run.mjs` **runs an unmutated baseline first and aborts the entire run if any test is red**, excludes the ledger's own test from catch counting, marks a suite that fails to load as `unpinned` rather than caught, and requires every `catches` name to exist. That is precisely the defect found in #39's ledger, which credited two mutations through an already-saturated signal in a reported "101/101" (see the correction below — the mutations are in fact caught; the ledger simply could not have known). Kept as a reminder: **before designing a verification mechanism, check whether a sibling package already solved it** — two ledgers were written independently in one repo and only one was sound.

**Process note — reconcile the believed board against the actually-running agents, on a schedule** (orchestration, 2026-08-01). Two critics dispatched against the realtime room check disappeared without ever reporting. I only discovered it by trying to stop one and getting back the real list of running agents — which showed both gone, and showed that two agents I had not dispatched were children of other builders. **`fix/realtime-r6` therefore sat pushed, receipted, and ungauntleted**, which is the second stale-lane incident of this campaign; the first was mine reporting two branches as live for fourteen hours. Lesson kept: **a dispatched agent is not a running agent, and the orchestrator's mental board drifts silently in exactly the direction that looks like progress.** Enumerate actual running tasks periodically and diff against what you believe is in flight — the failure mode is always a lane you think is covered. Related: those two critics were also dispatched *before the branch they were meant to review existed*, so even a verdict would have been about superseded code. Re-derive what a critic is pointed at from the tracker at dispatch time, never from memory of the plan.

**THE SYNTHESIS — "a query was run" is not "the sentence came from the query"; provenance is checked by construction, use is checked by mutation** (#10 r11, 2026-08-01). Round 11 wrote the missing history-quantifier rule, made its second half structural (a query exists only on a view; a view exists only because `reads()` took one, so quantifying over an unread field becomes unwritable), and then found the eighth door **in its own fix**: minting a sentence from a query proves nothing about whether the sentence *used* the answer. The check is mutation — `say()` re-runs its own phrase with each answer flipped, and a sentence that does not move was decoration. **It fired 8 times against round 11's own code on the first run**, and the forgery suite then showed the mutation was owed to the read points too, catching two more.

This is the general form of nearly every defect in this campaign, and it belongs at the top of any future round's checklist:

- a CI gate whose assertion *ran* but whose result nothing consumed
- a flag required to be *present* whose value was never read (`--fail-if-no-match=false`, `VITEST_RUN_START=0`)
- a receipt line handed four record views that used **none** of them, rendering a hardcoded negation
- narration minted from correct values that printed a seeded literal
- a mutation ledger crediting catchers it never ran unmutated
- an audit reporting "0 failed" over a selector matching nothing

**Structure proves where a value came from. Only mutation proves it was used.** Ask of every guard, narration, gate and receipt: *if I flip the input, does the output move?* If not, the machinery is ornament regardless of how correctly it was assembled.

**Positive note — a round reported its own reviewer as unfinished rather than as a pass** (#10 r11, 2026-08-01). codex (`gpt-5.6-sol`) returned FAIL with three confirmed defects — an aggregate sentence speaking for ten objects that escaped both instruments, a sentence laundered through another object's mint, and two vocabulary omissions — plus **two corrections to doctrine the round had just written**. grok landed one true hit and had two findings refuted, and **both its runs ended without a formal verdict, so the round reported it as an unfinished review rather than counting it**. That is the rule working under the temptation not to: an incomplete review is not a second opinion, and a round that has already passed one lineage has every incentive to round the other one up. Also kept: the round again reported the asymmetric-friction rule as **unexercised rather than passing**, because there are still zero destructive objects on the page — third round running, and still not scored as a pass.

**Process note — "the critic could not execute" is a signal to verify, not a caveat to record** (#21 r4/r5, 2026-08-01). Round 4 reported "full gate green (typecheck / build / test / lint)". `fix/core-engine-r4` **does not build** — two TS2345 errors, exit 2, verified by me on a clean clone. The round-4 gauntlet receipt I wrote *itself states* that the critic "could not run the suite (`vitest: not found`, no `node_modules`) and said so explicitly." I had the exact signal that no one had checked the builder's central claim, wrote it down as a property of the review, and filed the claim as fact anyway. Lesson kept: **an honest "I could not execute" from a critic converts every verification claim in that round from checked to unchecked — the orchestrator must then run the gate itself before writing a receipt.** The critic did its job by marking its evidence tier; the failure was entirely in the adjudication. Two changes made: prepare the critic's tree (install + build) before handing it over, and never repeat a builder's gate claim in a receipt without either a critic that executed it or the orchestrator running it.

**Correction — two distinct causes produce "verdictless run", and I diagnosed the second as the first** (#21, 2026-08-01). I recorded that grok's verdictless runs were caused by a network-isolated sandbox failing `pnpm install`. That was correct for the earlier runs (`EAI_AGAIN` in the session log). It was **not** correct for round 5's, whose transcripts say *"Source and `dist` diverge"* — the round was editing the tree while the reviewer read it, so grok stalled on an inconsistent checkout. Frozen and rebuilt, it returned a verdict immediately and found what four codex passes had missed. Lesson kept: **freeze the tree for the duration of a review**, and treat "same symptom" as the beginning of a diagnosis rather than the end of one. A single confident cause for a recurring failure is itself a claim that needs evidence per instance.

**Positive note — a self-adversarial pass that ran four times changed the work every time** (#21 r5, 2026-08-01). Each pass broke the previous pass's fixes, including **three comments the round had written to justify its own code** — a bidi-fold rationale that was false, a correction-scan cap that reproduced r4's exact failure in r4's own replacement, and an asterisk-emphasis rule where `2*3*4` folds to `234`, changing "how many" in the one direction the comment promised it could not. **Two bypasses were created by the fixes for earlier bypasses.** One adopted finding was then measured and **reverted**, because the fail-closed correction detector scored the benign case (0.50) higher than the defect (0.33) — the measurement is now the argument in the source for leaving that limit stated. Kept as evidence that the self-adversarial pass is not a formality with diminishing returns: **run it until a pass changes nothing, and treat a round's own justifying comments as prime targets** — they are written with the most confidence and the least scrutiny.

**Process note — a mutation ledger with no baseline run manufactures passes** (#39 r5 gauntlet, 2026-08-01). `pnpm test` exits 1 on a clean clone of the round that reported "101/101 mutations": a render throw escapes to React 19's global handler after the test's own `catch` swallows it, so `vitest` counts an unhandled error while the test itself passes. `test/mutations.mjs` decides "caught" purely from `execFileSync` throwing and **never runs the command unmutated**, so the two ledger entries naming that file are credited regardless of what the mutation does. **Corrected 2026-08-01 by #39 r6**, which re-ran them: both mutations **are** genuinely caught on r5. They were not phantom passes. The rule crediting them had a signal that was **already saturated** — it would have reported "caught" regardless — so the verdict was **uncorrelated, not wrong**, and the number was accidentally right. My original phrasing ("two entries nothing had checked", "inflated by two") overstated it. The sharper lesson: **a saturated signal produces a verdict that carries no information, which is worse than a wrong one because it is indistinguishable from a correct one.** Lesson kept: **a mutation ledger must run each catcher unmutated first and refuse the entry if it is already red** — otherwise a broken test is indistinguishable from a perfect one, and the ledger converts breakage into evidence. This upgrades the existing "mutation claims ship with a re-runnable ledger" rule: the ledger existed, was re-runnable, was committed, and was still wrong, because *green-on-mutation* means nothing without *red-on-baseline*. Corollary: a suite's exit code is part of the deliverable — check it on a clean clone, not in the worktree where it was built.

**Process note — the round-level diagnosis: every lesson applied to one site and one neighbour** (#39 r5 gauntlet, 2026-08-01). The critic's closing sentence is the most useful thing in the review: *"The failure of this round is not the row it spent five rounds on; it is that every lesson it learned was applied to that row and to no more than one neighbour of each kind."* Concretely — the resolved-id action bus landed on the timeline row and not on the receipt's provenance row; the record-checksum landed on one of five citation-rendering boundaries; the renderer-side voice check landed on one of two page-authored string types; the caller-supplied-id fix landed on the authored arm and not the chosen arm. Every one of those was the *correct* fix, applied once. Lesson kept: **when a round names a defect class, its deliverable is the sweep, and "the class" must be enumerated mechanically — every call site of the pattern, listed in the receipt — not sampled by whichever instance the critic happened to report.** This is the third framing of the same rule today (#40 r4 "a rule applied at one site", #10 r10 "derive rather than enumerate"); the new part is that it can be checked at review time by asking the round for its enumeration, not its diff.

**Process note — a source-grep can match a strict subset of a construct's syntax, which is a third failure mode** (#39 r5 gauntlet, 2026-08-01). A meta-check enumerating guards with `/if\s*\([^)]*\)\s*continue/g` cannot cross a `)`, so **any guard whose condition contains a function call is invisible to it** — and the accompanying `guards.length > 0` assertion is still satisfied by an unrelated legitimate guard elsewhere. Inserting `if (effectiveOpacity(el) < 0.999) continue;` left 51 unit tests and 32 e2e contrast assertions green while a real 1.36:1 string went unreported. Lesson kept: **beyond "matched nothing" and "matched the wrong occurrence", a pattern can match a strict subset of the syntax it claims to enumerate — the failure is silent and the count is non-zero.** Parse, or enumerate the constructs from the AST. Related from the same review: an overflow audit exempting elements with a clipping ancestor exempted **98.7% of the page**, because the app root is `overflow: hidden` — 33 of 2542 elements inspected, and a 3000px-wide element in a 1124px viewport passed all four assertions. **An exemption written for a special case becomes universal the moment a structural ancestor satisfies it.**

**Process note — a checker's own fixture exempts it from any check that scans text** (#40 r5 gauntlet, 2026-08-01). The rule requiring every script to carry the canonical main-module guard is implemented as `source.includes('if (isMainModule(import.meta.url)) {')` — a substring test over the whole file. `gate-selftest.mjs` stores that exact string as a fixture (`CANONICAL_GUARD_LINE`), so the file satisfies the check no matter what its *real* guard says. Its real guard can therefore read `if (isMainModule(import.meta.url) && process.env.CI === undefined) {` — green on a laptop, silent in CI, biome-clean, policy-clean. And `mainGuardProblems` is invoked in exactly one place in the repository: that same file. **Sole enforcer, sole exception.** It chains: with it disarmed nothing checks guards, so the policy self-test takes the identical edit, and **two `&&` insertions remove all 316 assertions with every step reporting success.** Causality was proven, not argued — re-spelling only the fixture literal as a joined pair of halves makes the scanner fire. Lesson kept: **a test suite necessarily contains examples of what it forbids, so any rule enforced by scanning file text will always exempt its own tests — enforce at statement position, or build fixtures from concatenated halves.** The file already documents the halves technique eight lines above the literal that defeats it, which is the pattern to watch for: **the correct method present in the same file as its own violation.** Third instance today of a text scan standing in for a parser (`reopened`/`open` substring, flow-style YAML, this), and the most expensive.

**Process note — distinguish "refused by the rule" from "did not happen to break"** (#40 r5 gauntlet, 2026-08-01). The critic found the baseline-fetch rule checks that some argument *contains* `refs/heads/main` rather than that `origin/main` results, and constructed four spellings the rule accepts. None reproduced — `actions/checkout` runs `git remote add origin …`, leaving `remote.origin.fetch` set such that git's opportunistic remote-tracking update writes `origin/main` regardless, and `-c` appends to that multi-valued key rather than replacing it. The critic reported it anyway, as a real gap **held shut by git's behaviour and not by the rule**, with the measurement showing why. Lesson kept: **a control that holds only because of an unrelated tool's incidental behaviour is not a control, and the day that tool changes is the day it silently stops holding.** Report and fix these; a reviewer that suppresses a finding because the exploit failed today is reporting the weather, not the climate. This is the standard to ask critics for explicitly.

**Process note — a query that matches nothing reports exactly like one that passes** (#39 r5, 2026-08-01). A registry selector written `.surf[disabled]` matched zero elements, because every class in that app is a CSS-module hash. The sweep printed a clean result and had inspected nothing. Two sibling defects in the same harness, both of which made it *unable to fail*: a filled graphic was composited against its own background (eighteen presence dots measured at exactly 1.00:1 and passed), and a border was measured against only the friendlier of its two adjacent colours — **which is how `--ambbd` survived four consecutive contrast passes.** Lesson kept: **every audit must assert its own denominator.** A check that reports "N inspected, 0 failed" is only evidence when N is also asserted against an independently derived expectation; "0 failed" alone is indistinguishable from "0 looked at". This is the same shape as the CI gate that passes when the suite never ran, reached through a CSS selector — and it is now the second instance today of an *instrument* being wrong rather than the artifact (see #40 r5's measurement harness). Audit the instrument on the same schedule as the thing it measures.
