**Positive note — delete the field rather than guard it, confirmed at a fifth site** (#39 r5, 2026-08-01). Four rounds each guarded the field the previous round had moved, because **a guard over a carried field is always one field behind**. Round 5 deleted the address instead: `Quotation` became `{ messageId }` plus a brand — no actor, no text, no timestamp, no room — and everything rendered beside quoted words is looked up from the page's record register at four render boundaries, with a spread that re-adds the actor now failing at compile time. This is the same structural move as #10 r10 (narration derived from the comparison that authorizes it), #21 r4 (allowlist the compliant quote form), #40 r4 (allowlist launchers), and the earlier tooling lesson that deleting an argument beats validating it. **Five arrivals, five subsystems, no shared context: this is no longer a lesson, it is how this repo fixes things.** State it as the first option in any fix round: *can the field, argument, or option simply not exist?*

**Measurement — the two foreign lineages produced nine defects with zero overlap** (#39 r5, 2026-08-01). gpt-5.6-terra broke the display path five ways (independent ledger and feed props; system voice checked at the constructor and parser while four components printed raw text; a payload exemption scoped to a span rather than a sentence shape; a geometry model false when a card grows; a row printing the caller's own id and timestamp). grok-4.5 **could not break the display path, said so explicitly, and went to the side channels**, finding four more (a field held to length while its comment claimed voice-checking; a node budget failing open; a missing entry on a reject list; an action bus still trusting caller-supplied ids). Kept as evidence for the routing rule: **the two lineages are complementary rather than redundant, and a reviewer that reports "I could not break X" and pivots is doing the job, not failing at it.** Ask for that pivot explicitly in critic prompts.

**Process note — the orchestrator's prescribed fix is a hypothesis, and mine was bypassable within minutes** (#40 r5, 2026-08-01). Adjudicating a fail-open where `pnpm --filter <nonexistent> exec` exits 0 having run nothing, I specified the remedy in the round-5 brief: drop the filter flags "unless paired with pnpm's own `--fail-if-no-match`". The builder's blind self-review found `--fail-if-no-match=false` satisfies that pairing and restores the identical fail-open — **both foreign lineages found it independently, within minutes** — and the separate-word spelling `--fail-if-no-match false` was a second instance. My rule required a flag's *presence* where it needed the flag's *value*, which is denylist thinking wearing the shape of a fix, in the same brief where I told the builder to allowlist. Lesson kept: **a fix dictated by the reviewer inherits none of the builder's context and all of the reviewer's confidence — write prescriptions as the property to hold, not the mechanism to add, and let the round attack the prescription first.** Two companions from the same round: `- { name: Checkout, uses: actions/checkout@v4 }` is legal flow-style YAML that GitHub runs and that a raw-line scan never sees (**a line scan over a structured format is a parser substitute, and the format will always have a second spelling**) — that one had been open since round 1 on the action that checks out the code; and `git fetch --dry-run` keeps every word the matcher reads while updating no ref, which is the *"invoked is not runs"* lesson arriving through a flag rather than a launcher.

**Positive note — the builder audited the instrument, not just the artifact** (#40 r5, 2026-08-01). Before fixing anything, the round enumerated every table in the repo that *admits* something and confirmed the manager table was the only one that never received round 4's criteria — then applied the same sweep to every rule round 5 itself stated, which caught two further sites in its own commits. It also found the launcher table's measurement harness was wrong: it claimed `bash … -eo pipefail -c` was the runner's shell, and for `setsid` — the one entry whose whole point is waiting — that harness answers backwards (3 ms under `-c`, 3003 ms under `bash -e <file>`). Every number in the table was re-taken. **This is "a wrong instrument invents defects, not just misses them" being applied prospectively rather than learned again**, and it is the first time in this campaign a round has swept its own new rules for the defect class it was dispatched to fix.

**Process note — "clean against main" is not "clean in sequence"; measure the merge you will actually perform** (merge train, 2026-08-01). I surveyed every live lane with `git merge-tree main <branch>`, got zero conflicts across the board, and reported the merge train as de-risked. A builder then warned that its own first conflict check had produced false positives, which prompted a re-check — my detector held, but the *question* was wrong. Measured pairwise, **9 of 10 lane pairs conflict**, because a merge train applies branches in sequence and each lane carries a different stale merge base, so main's own evolution reads as conflict between them. Lesson kept: **an N-way integration is not N independent two-way merges, and testing it as though it were produces a true measurement of the wrong thing.** That is the same failure shape as every defect this campaign has hunted — the number was real, the claim it supported was not. Practical remedy recorded on the map: rebase each lane onto current `main` immediately before merging it, one at a time, and verify `git diff main..HEAD` touches only that lane's files. Corroborating instance from the same hour: a round told it was 5 commits behind measured itself at 10, and merging without rebasing would have reverted 30 lines of this file.

**Process note — a verification that is not committed and re-run is a claim with a timestamp** (#9/#48, 2026-08-01). `design/tokens.css` states its values are byte-identical to the source prototype, and #9 closed on "102 tokens verified byte-identical (negative-controlled checker)". Measured on `main`: `:root` is **0/51** byte-identical, `html.atr-dark` 19/51, 83 lines changed — Biome's CSS formatter lower-cased every hex and reformatted two rgba values. On the original branch the same comparison still returns 51/51. **The claim was true when made, the checker was real, and neither was wrong — the checker was never committed, so nothing re-ran it, and a routine `pnpm lint` falsified a verified property in silence.** Lesson kept: **commit the checker or drop the claim; a one-time verification of a property that later code can change is provenance theatre.** Two sharper corollaries. First, `CONVENTIONS.md:196` says "Never edit `design/tokens.css` values" — **a rule written to constrain people does not bind tools**, and the violation came from the build pipeline, which was never in scope of the sentence. Second, the false sentence exists in three files (README, CONVENTIONS, the token file's own header); fixing one leaves two, which is how this class survives. The open question worth more than the fix is now the ticket's gauntlet reference: *what else in this repository can a tool falsify without anyone editing it?* — formatters, codegen, dependency resolution and migration generation are all candidates.

**Positive note — an exemption that named its condition graduated on its own** (#28/#40, 2026-08-01). Auditing whether an orphan-history branch held unmerged work, the one thing that looked lost was a CI exemption: `apps/web` excused from the unit-test floor because "the Vitest workspace stays browser-free on purpose, so apps/web contributes zero unit tests by design." Downstream it is simply gone — `"exempt": {}` — with the reason recorded inline: *"apps/web left `exempt` and acquired a floor in #40's merge: fix/auth-r5 gave it a Vitest suite."* Nobody was asked to check it; the exemption carried its own expiry condition, the condition cleared, and a later round removed it and said why. **This is the anti-staleness rule paying off rather than being enforced, and it is the first instance in this campaign where the mechanism worked with no prompting.** Worth keeping as evidence that "everything expirable names its expiry condition" is cheap to write and self-executing when honoured — and worth noting that the audit which found it was only possible because the removal left a written reason instead of a silent deletion.

**Process note — an orphan branch is a bookkeeping problem or a data-loss problem, and only a content check tells you which** (#28, 2026-08-01). `fix/ci-r6` had no merge base with `main` and looked like it needed the documented three-step replay. Comparing trees rather than histories showed the successor branch strictly ahead: every file present, eleven byte-identical, higher test floors, and the one hand-checkable protection intact. The 81 lines unique to the orphan were reflow. Lesson kept: **when histories are disconnected, compare the trees — `git diff <newer> <older> -- <path>` across the shared file set answers "is anything lost" directly, where merge-base reasoning cannot.** And do the check before scheduling the replay: the expensive procedure was unnecessary, and running it would have resurrected superseded content, which is exactly how a renamed migration reappeared earlier in this campaign.

**Process note — a shared narrative file serializes its own restructuring behind every open lane** (#44, 2026-08-01). Nine branches touch `README.md`; each appends to a different region, so every pairwise merge is clean and the graph shows no conflict. But the best available improvement — moving the contributor half out and promoting two sections the first screen links to — is a *delete plus an add*, which conflicts with all of them at once. Twice in one ticket the highest-value critic recommendation was blocked this way. Lesson kept: **append-only edits to a shared document compose; structural edits do not, and the dependency graph cannot see the difference.** Practical rule: when many lanes touch one narrative file, let them append and schedule exactly one restructuring pass after the merge train (filed as #47). Say so out loud when refusing a restructure, because "out of scope" and "not yet" look identical to the person whose recommendation is being declined — the second is a promise and deserves a ticket.

**Process note — a permissive write path with a strict read path is a durable outage, not a trade-off** (#22 r6, 2026-08-01). The append boundary never validates `p_payload` against `RoomEvent`; `catchUp` then calls `RoomEvent.parse` on every row with no safe skip. One unparseable row makes a room permanently unhydratable, for every client, forever. Every guarantee the ledger offers is about what may be *written* — nothing defines what happens when something already written cannot be *read*. Lesson kept: **for any durable store, the read path needs a defined behaviour for data the write path should have refused, because rows predating a check, written by a future path, or repaired by hand will always exist.** Tightening the write side does not discharge this; it only shrinks the set of ways in. Filed as #46. Note also that the builder found this, verified it, and **reported it rather than fixing it**, because it was pre-existing and outside the round — that is the correct move and the reason it got a ticket with a proper scope instead of a silent scope-creep.

**Process note — prose that names its authority is not thereby correct; open the authority** (#22 r6, 2026-08-01). A paragraph in `schema.ts` claimed `UPDATE`, `DELETE` and `TRUNCATE` all raise, and named migration `0003` as the authority for that claim. `0003` says the opposite. **Both foreign lineages found it independently** — the strongest signal this process generates. Lesson kept: **a citation makes a claim more persuasive without making it more true, and reviewers read the citation as verification rather than performing it.** Every "as enforced in X" needs the enforcement opened and read; this is the same defect class as doctrine asserting a fix it never made (#10 r10) and a README citing tickets that do not contain the reasoning (#44 r1) — three instances today, in code comments, in doctrine, and in public prose.

**Process note — two oracles that agree in the common case are not one oracle** (#22 r6, 2026-08-01). The round claimed its new boundary "gives the same answer `resolveRoomId` gives". It does not: `resolveRoomId` asks the fold, the boundary asks the log, and for a minting event the reducer refused they diverge — the fold answers *no* room rather than a *different* one. The claim was corrected to state precisely that, plus the fact that live still equals replay and that closing the gap would mean running the reducer in SQL. Lesson kept: **when two components answer the same question by different routes, the claim to state is where they diverge and why that is acceptable — never that they agree.** Same shape as the custody-chain lesson from #21 r4: the interesting property is always at the seam.

**Process note — a sentence that quantifies over history must be produced by a query on that history** (#10 r10 gauntlet, 2026-08-01). Rounds 1-10 wrote rule after rule about *the pair that moved*: from/to, validated operands, headers minted by the branch that compared. A rotated-lens critic found four high-severity defects that are all one unwritten rule — claims about a field's **history** rather than its transition. "again", "still", "preserved", "nobody has", "nothing other than", "already" are quantifiers over an entire field history, and the round had already built `fieldView` with `.ever()`/`.last()` to answer them, then used it in three places out of many with no rule requiring it. Results: "it is unverified **again**" on a history that was never unverified; "the prior **sign-off** preserved" derived from *whether* a thing settled rather than *how*; "**nothing other than the claimant** has checked it" rendered directly above a green check by `checks`; and a reassign narrating the answer field it never read. Lesson kept: **when a fix introduces a query object because the old code could not answer a question, the same commit must find every sentence that was answering that question without it.** A new capability used at three call sites and required at none is a rule that was designed and never adopted.

**Process note — doctrine that documents a fix it did not make is worse than doctrine that documents a gap** (#10 r10 gauntlet, 2026-08-01). `design/CONVENTIONS.md:229` states the invariant's coverage "is taken from the object's own shape, every property the page paints words from, with the reason each excluded one is already covered by a named invariant." It is a hand-written selector list, one entry longer than the previous round's, derived from nothing — and the surfaces it omits are exactly where the round's defects were found. The document had correctly diagnosed that a remembered list is a denylist one layer up, and then shipped a remembered list while asserting it had not. Lesson kept: **a claim in a doctrine file is a claim, subject to the same evidence rule as a claim in code or a receipt — and a document asserting compliance is more dangerous than one asserting nothing, because reviewers read it instead of checking.** Every "this is derived from X" in a convention must name the code that derives it. Also recorded: my own corollary at line 283 ("an audit may not exempt the case its rule covers") needs its twin — **an audit may not fire on cases its rule does not cover.** A checker whose derived-vocabulary clause is a substring test flags `reopened` as stating `open`, cries wolf during ordinary use, and becomes the noise that masks a real firing; the same design was simultaneously blind in the other direction, comparing only each field's last value so a surface stating an earlier one was invisible. One design choice, a false positive and a false negative.

**Process note — a stated limit is not a disposition; documenting what you cannot prove is not the same as declining to assert it** (#21 r4 gauntlet, 2026-08-01). A blind critic found two High-severity paths, and verifying them showed the source *already described both*, in the builder's own prose, as deliberate. `escalation.ts:533`: "The residue is stated rather than hidden: polarity that lives in a *different sentence* ('I will deploy Friday. Not.') is not visible to this... it is why the guarantee is written as 'somebody wrote this sentence', not 'somebody meant it'." `acceptance.ts:428`: the theta_min discard sits above the receipt check "on purpose". Both rationales are honest and largely correct — and in both cases the code still auto-accepts the negated sentence and still discards the inverted receipt. The round had already built the exact third severity (`refer`) those two cases need. Lesson kept: **an acknowledgement in a comment changes nothing about what the program does with the input — when you write down a limit, the same commit must route the inputs that fall inside it to a disposition that reflects it.** Watch for this specifically in well-written code: candid prose about a weakness reads as having handled the weakness, to reviewers and to the author alike, and it is the most credible-looking way for a claim to outrun its evidence. Ask of every documented limitation: *what does the code do with an input that lands in it?*

**Process note — a review sandbox that cannot install dependencies produces no review** (#21 r4 gauntlet, 2026-08-01). grok exited 0 with no verdict for the third time; recovering its session log showed why — it tried `pnpm install` inside the network-isolated read-only sandbox, hit `EAI_AGAIN`, then tried to symlink `node_modules` from unrelated checkouts it found on disk, and was cancelled mid-attempt. Codex hit the identical wall (`vitest: not found`) but reported anyway with an explicit "static analysis only, not executed evidence" caveat. Lesson kept: **prepare the critic's working tree before handing it over — install and build, so a read-only network-isolated sandbox constrains writing, not running the suite.** Two corollaries: a critic that marks its own evidence tier (executed vs. read) is more useful than one that does not, so ask for it explicitly; and always recover the session log of a verdictless run before discarding it — this diagnosis came entirely from a transcript the CLI's stdout never showed.

**Process note — a subagent isolation flag is not implied by telling the agent to isolate** (orchestration, 2026-08-01). Two fix rounds were dispatched with prompts saying "work in your worktree" but without the `isolation: worktree` parameter. One created its own worktree anyway; the other ran `git checkout -b` **in the primary working directory**, moving the orchestrator's own checkout onto a build branch mid-session. Caught only because an unrelated scripted edit asserted its anchor text, failed, and refused to write — on the wrong branch that anchor did not exist. Lesson kept: **isolation is a property of the dispatch, not of the instructions; set the flag.** And the second half is why nothing broke: **every scripted edit asserts the text it expects before writing.** A script that had blindly appended would have written this file's history onto a branch that does not carry it, and the loss would have been silent.

**Process note — a rule stated generally but applied at one site is the most expensive kind of near-miss** (#40 r4 gauntlet, 2026-08-01). Round 4 wrote an explicit admission rule ("no value of any listed option may produce exit 0 without the command having run to completion") and applied it to the launcher table while its neighbouring manager table got ~25 option keys with no justification, no measurement and zero fixtures — so `pnpm --filter <nonexistent> exec node assert.mjs` exits 0 having run nothing and the policy engine prints *clean*, silently deleting nine real CI steps. Independently, the same round derived "an action is a program too" and guarded `uses:`, but left `jobs.<id>.container:` unguarded — an author-chosen image underneath every allowlist that reasons about `node` and `bash`. Lesson kept: **when a round states a general rule, the round is not done until every site the rule's own words cover has been enumerated and checked — grep for the siblings before reporting.** A rule applied once reads, in the diff, exactly like a rule applied everywhere; only the enumeration distinguishes them. Corollary confirmed here: `import.meta.url === \`file://${process.argv[1]}\`` fails open on paths with spaces or symlinks, and fifteen scripts — including both self-tests — exit 0 asserting nothing when it does; a main-module guard is a guard and needs the same scrutiny as the assertions inside it.

**Process note — hardening link N of a chain is worthless while link N-1 is unconstrained** (#21 r4, 2026-08-01). The acceptance path is message → quote → statement → accepted. Round 4 made the statement/quote comparison exact — every token present, in order, no denylist. A blind foreign-lineage reviewer then auto-accepted `"Bob will deploy production Friday under any circumstances"` as a quote identical to its statement, mined out of *"It is not true that Bob will deploy…"*, because the quote/message relation was still "any substring". Lesson kept: **an adversary picks the weakest link, not the one you just reinforced — state the end-to-end property (what the *author* asserted) and check every hop against it, or the proof at one hop is decoration.** Whenever a review hardens one relation, write down the full custody chain and name what constrains each remaining hop.

**Process note — a tokenizer is a policy, and a character class is a denylist** (#21 r4, 2026-08-01). Splitting on `[^a-z0-9']+` silently deleted fullwidth `ｎｏｔ`, Cyrillic `не`, `❌`, and `?` — a Russian sentence reduced to zero tokens was then refused as *empty*, i.e. the failure disguised itself as a different failure. Lesson kept: **any place text is normalized, split, cased, or stripped is an unwritten policy about what counts; write it as an allowlist of what may differ and test it against non-Latin script, marks, and negation particles.** This is the third independent arrival at the allowlist principle today — #40 r4 (launchers), #10 r10 (narration derived from the comparison), #21 r4 (tokens) — three subsystems, no shared context. It is now a standing design rule for this repo, not a lesson: **enumerate the compliant forms; never enumerate the violations.**

**Process note — a probe derived from the table it tests is vacuous, and this is the second time** (#21 r4, 2026-08-01). An alignment-cap probe written as `maxAlignedTokens + 1` moved with the constant it was meant to pin, so the mutation survived. Round 3's gauntlet had already found exactly this in the θ probes. Lesson kept: **a test's expected value must be written as a literal derived from the requirement, never computed from the code under test** — and a lesson that recurs in the same ticket means the first fix was applied to instances rather than to the pattern; sweep for the whole class when it reappears. Related: an escape showed the reducer's quote gate was masked by the validator one check later — **a boundary held up by another boundary is not a boundary**; test each in isolation with the other disabled.

**Process note — a defect fixed instance-by-instance grows doors faster than you close them** (#10 r10, 2026-08-01). The same defect — a narrating surface asserting a change the record denied — was fixed in five separate places across five rounds, and r10's own review found a sixth *inside the entry the other five had just been fixed in*. What ended it was not a sixth patch: the write path now takes its record reads once and every narrating surface is minted from those views, so **the branch that produces the word `AMENDED` is the branch that compared the two values**. Lesson kept: **while the count of affected sites is unknown, each fix is a sample, not a solution — derive the assertion from the same computation that authorizes it, and the enumeration problem disappears.** Note the convergence: #40 r4 reached the identical principle the same day from the opposite direction (allowlist the compliant form rather than denylist evasions), in a different subsystem, with no shared context. Independent arrival at the same structural rule is the strongest signal this process generates — treat it as doctrine, not coincidence.

**Process note — "unexercised" is a third verdict, and collapsing it into "passing" is the overclaim** (#10 r10, 2026-08-01). A rule-by-rule doctrine sweep reported seventeen rules holding, two drifted, and one — asymmetric friction on destructive actions — **unexercised rather than passing**: nothing on the page is destructive yet, so the press-and-hold case never arises. Lesson kept: **an audit over N rules must report untested rules as untested; a compliance score that counts inapplicable rules as satisfied is a claim outrunning its evidence in the shape of a number.** Same family as "invoked is not runs" and "a green gate whose suite never ran". Any sweep, ledger, or coverage claim in this repo states its unexercised set explicitly or the number is not accepted.

**Process note — clearing a field is an authorization change** (#10 r10, 2026-08-01). A foreign-lineage reviewer found that handing a question to someone *cleared* `owedTo` while setting the assignee — so downstream, "nobody owes this" was read as "anyone may answer it", and the feed row's one-click Answer had no check at all. The permission widened as a side effect of a reassignment nobody thought of as a permission edit. Lesson kept: **any field a guard reads is part of the guard; setting it to empty is a grant, not a cleanup.** Enumerate the readers of a field before writing null into it — and audit the *quiet* entry points (a one-click affordance in a feed row) as carefully as the deliberate ones, because the checks get written where the ceremony is.

**Process note — when every guard shares a runtime, one environment variable disarms them all** (#40 r4, 2026-08-01). After inverting a denylist into an allowlist in four places, the builder's own adversarial pass found `NODE_OPTIONS="--require ./nobble.cjs"`: every assertion in the deploy job is a node process, so a single environment variable makes each of them exit 0 with its error annotation still printed. Workflow-, job-, step-, one-shot and `$GITHUB_ENV` spellings were all clean against the round's headline fix. Lesson kept: **a set of guards that share an execution substrate has a shared kill switch — enumerate what the substrate reads (environment, config files, preload hooks, module resolution) and treat that surface as part of the guard.** An allowlist over *how a command is written* does not cover *what the runtime does before main runs*.

**Process note — an allowlist entry can be its own bypass** (#40 r4, 2026-08-01). The same review found `node --disable-warning <script>`: an option the builder's own freshly-written allowlist had just admitted, which makes node consume the script path as the flag's value and exit 0 having run nothing, while the parser still reports the step present. Lesson kept: **every allowlist entry needs the same adversarial treatment as the denylist it replaced — admitting an option is a claim about all of its argument forms.** The admission criteria that came out of this are worth copying: for the launcher *and* each listed option, establish that it execs the command, runs it exactly once unconditionally, and waits and exits with its status; quote two measurements (`<launcher> false` non-zero, `<launcher> sleep 2` ≈ 2s); ship an accepted fixture proving the entry is reachable rather than decorative, and a rejected fixture for the nearest option that breaks one property. Governing rule: **no value of any listed option may produce exit 0 without the command having run to completion.**

**Process note — a self-adversarial pass can pay off without producing a verdict** (#40 r4, 2026-08-01). Three review attempts yielded no verdict at all: codex's long run was killed by its own content filter (the subject matter is shell-bypass technique), its short run hit a read-only sandbox, and grok exited cleanly with no findings section on three passes including `--max-turns 60`. All three of the round's real findings came from *leads in transcripts that never became reports*, reproduced by hand afterwards. Lesson kept: **a review's value is in what it surfaces, not in whether it renders a verdict — read the transcript of a failed run before discarding it, and never let "no verdict" mean "nothing found."** Corollary: some artifacts cannot be reviewed by some lineages at all; when a subject repeatedly trips a model's own filters, route it to a different reviewer rather than re-running.

**Process note — denylisting ways to not-run is unbounded; allowlist what may run** (#28 and #40, six rounds, 2026-08-01). The question "does this step actually run the assertion?" produced fifteen distinct bypasses across two tickets: `false &&`, `; true`, `echo exec`, a decoy `echo git fetch`, `node --check`, `xargs -r`, `sudo -b`, a `PATH` rebind, a one-shot env assignment, `sh -c`, `docker-compose`, and finally `setsid -f`. Each round folded the newest spelling into a denylist and the next spelling existed. Lesson kept: **when a guard enumerates ways to evade, it is unbounded by construction — invert it into an allowlist of forms that provably do the thing, and justify each entry.** The general shape: prefer "only these launchers, each of which is known to wait for exit status" over "not these fourteen ways to avoid waiting."

**Process note — the builder's own blind cross-lineage review found more than the gauntlet would have** (#40 r3, 2026-08-01). Before submitting, the builder ran an independent adversarial review of their own fix. It broke three of their four claims, and all fourteen findings reproduced against the engine exactly as committed: `node --check <script>` (syntax-checks rather than runs), `xargs -r node`, `sudo -b` (backgrounds it), a `PATH` assignment through a step `env:` block that the `no-command-shadowing` rule **had claimed to ban for two rounds**, a one-shot variable assignment recreating the precise preflight/boot divergence the round was closing, `docker-compose` and `sh -c` invisible to the compose rule, and a ledger that executed unwrapped argv so `timeout 30 node x` would have run as `node x`. Lesson kept: **a builder who has just written a guard is the best-placed person to attack it and the worst-placed to trust it — commission the adversarial pass as part of the build, not only after it.** Promoted into `/campaign`: for any round whose deliverable is itself a verification mechanism, the builder runs a blind foreign-lineage review of their own output before reporting, and lists what it broke.

**Process note — a crashed mutation run leaves the tree mutated and green** (#22 realtime r5, 2026-08-01). An interrupted ledger run left a substitution in `ws-server.ts`; `pnpm test` was green afterwards and `git status` could not distinguish the leftover from a fix branch's real work. Only a drift check inside the ledger noticed. The fix records the in-flight mutation and the file's original bytes, recovers a leftover file automatically, and refuses to start on a leftover SQL record. Lesson kept: **a tool that edits the tree must be crash-safe about it — record what it changed before changing it, and refuse to run on evidence of an interrupted predecessor.** A verification instrument that can silently corrupt the thing it verifies is worse than no instrument.

**Process note — a validation over a union must discriminate on the union's tag** (#22 realtime r5, 2026-08-01). A constraint meant to prove that an event's column-level room matches the room in its payload was written as a `coalesce` over every nested key a payload might carry. Coalesce is order-of-keys, not order-of-kind — and the storage layer accepts extra keys that the schema strips only *after* the row exists. So a caller supplies the real room in the key its kind actually uses, smuggles the desired room into a key belonging to a different kind, and the check reads the smuggled one. Lesson kept: **when validating a tagged union, read the tag first and require exactly the shape it names — a check that accepts "whichever of these keys is present" accepts the one an attacker chose to add.** Corollary for tests: exercising honest shapes proves nothing about a check whose defect is which shape it looks at.

**Process note — deriving beats validating, and removing beats deriving** (#22 realtime r5, 2026-08-01). Round 4 stored the receipt window in an immutable column and validated its shape; round 5 removed the parameter entirely and derived the value inside the boundary, with the append returning what it stored so the caller's fold can be compared against it and the transaction aborted on disagreement. The exploit test then attacked both positionally and *by name*, on the principle that "the argument moved to the end" is not a fix. Lesson kept: **when a caller-supplied value must be trusted, the strongest fix is to delete the argument — validation invites a bypass, derivation invites a divergence, and only absence invites neither.** The same audit found a second instance the round had not been asked about: a room id that no one compared to the room the payload declared.

**Process note — a wrong instrument invents defects, not just misses them** (#39 UI r4, 2026-08-01). Four instrument failures in one round: `getComputedStyle` read mid-transition reported an unfixed stylesheet as fixed; a `@ts-expect-error` stayed satisfied after the brand was deleted, because inferred widening made the forgery fail for an incidental reason; a regex over a CSS guard broke on a nested paren, matched nothing, and passed. The fourth is the new one — a `textContent` sweep reported eleven "welded" accessible names that a screen reader never hears, and fixing them would have redesigned working code. The corrected sweep (Playwright's aria snapshot) found three genuine ones instead. Lesson kept: **every previous instrument lesson was about a tool that could not see a defect; a tool can equally invent one, and acting on a false positive damages working code.** Before fixing what a sweep reports, confirm the sweep is reading what the user's actual consumer reads — the accessibility tree, the composited pixel, the settled style — not a convenient proxy for it.

**Process note — the instrument had the defect it hunts** (#26 auth r9, 2026-08-01). A mutation named `unguard-logger` silently stopped matching when the code it targeted moved files, and a `> /dev/null` inside a shell loop turned its throw into a green suite with a ledger row reading "0 caught" — a fail-open inside the tool built to find fail-opens. A second row simply carried the number from the row above it, in a table whose own header warns about that. Lesson kept: **a verification instrument needs the verification it provides — make it detect its own no-ops (does every mutation still match its target?) and prove every recorded number was produced by the run that claims it.**

**Process note — enumerate from the grammar, not from the code** (#26 auth r10, 2026-08-01). Round 9 replaced an overclaimed guarantee with an honest "best-effort, and here are the five shapes that walk past it" — the right move, and the round-10 delta still failed it, because a sixth shape (`const db = cond ? createDatabase() : createDatabase()`) was not on the list. **A stated limit is a claim like any other**: a reader is entitled to rely on it, so an inaccurate narrowing is a worse defect than a wide gap honestly described. What fixed it was reversing the direction of the audit — round 9 had enumerated the limits by reading what the analysis handled and writing down what it did not, which can only ever rediscover the author's own blind spots. Round 10 enumerated what the *grammar* allows an initializer to be (every expression form that passes a value through, every declaration form that carries one), wrote the fixtures first, and then asked the code. That found the sixth the critic named and six more it did not, in two families the critic's list never reached — declaration forms (a parameter default, a binding element's own default, a destructuring pattern in parameter position) and the module graph (`export default createDatabase()`, one line that laundered a connection to every importer). Lesson kept: **when you write down a limitation, derive the list from the space of legal inputs, not from a reading of the implementation — the second method is the implementation grading its own homework.**

**Process note — the escape was in the denominator, not the grammar** (#26 auth r11, 2026-08-01). Round 10 re-derived its enumeration from the expression grammar (the note above) and closed seven escapes — and two blind critics from different lineages then walked past the same guard without touching the grammar at all, because it had never re-derived **which files are in the graph**. Three executed counterexamples: the file filter matched only `.ts/.tsx/.mts/.cts` while `allowJs` is on and Next serves `route.js`, so a planted `.js` route reported nothing and `next dev` returned all 36 membership rows to a request with no cookie; the skip list dropped any directory *named* `test`/`build`/`dist`/`e2e` anywhere in a path, and `apps/web/app/test/` is the route `/test`; and a specifier resolving outside `graphRoots` left the taint map with no entry, so "never read" answered *false* to "is it tainted". Asking the same question of our own tree found a fourth the critics had not: `@/*` — declared in `apps/web/tsconfig.json`, used by six modules, classified as a third-party package, so `import { db } from '@/lib/db'` was not a handle and `db().query.memberships` in a Server Action was reported by nobody. **One rule replaced six patches**: anything the analysis did not parse is *reported* — an unknown extension, a file that failed to parse, a specifier that resolves to nothing it read, an expression form nobody classified, a directory it declined to enter. Its first catch after being written was a real one within the hour (`tsconfig.tsbuildinfo`, left by `pnpm typecheck`). Lesson kept: **an enumeration has a denominator as well as a grammar — for every list a checker walks, name what it enumerates *from*, and make "we could not tell" a reported outcome rather than a silent pass.** Corollary, learned twice this round: a fall-through `return false` at the end of a classifier is that same defect in one line, which is how a tagged template (`const db = tag\`ignored\``) laundered a handle past 343 green tests.

**Process note — the fifth layer of the same fail-open, and it was the mutation matching itself** (#26 auth r11, 2026-08-01). The ledger's `edit()` replaced *every* occurrence of a search string. Round 11 added a row that mutates `mutation-ledger.mjs` itself — so the string it searches for appears twice, once in the function it means to change and once in the table entry that declares it. When the real line later drifted, the mutation went on applying cleanly **against its own definition**, with `--verify` printing `ok` for a row measuring nothing. Two fixes, and the general one is the useful one: a target matching more than once is now an error (`'all'` opts in where a mutation genuinely means every occurrence, which `drop-join` does — it found that immediately), and the self-referential row assembles its search string from two pieces so the file contains it exactly once. Lesson kept: **a search-and-replace mutation is only as good as the uniqueness of its anchor — count the matches and fail on ambiguity, and treat any tool that can edit its own source as a tool whose anchors are guaranteed to be duplicated.**

**Process note — a test that restates a definition** (#26 auth r11, 2026-08-01). `expect(Object.keys(commandPolicy).every(isCommand)).toBe(true)` where `isCommand` *is* `Object.hasOwn(commandPolicy, …)`: true for every possible table and every possible implementation, and proven vacuous by replacing the predicate with `typeof value === 'string'` and watching that test pass alone. This is the assertion-side twin of the #21 note above, and the generalisation is the useful part: the way to find the class is to **stub every predicate fail-open and ask which tests notice**. `scripts/predicate-sweep.mjs` does exactly that for the twelve predicates in `@atrium/auth` and fails if any can be stubbed with the suite still green. Lesson kept: **a test whose expectation is computed by the code under test is a tautology wearing a test's name — pin the expectation independently, and run a fail-open stub of every predicate to find the ones already rotten.**

**Process note — three layers of the same fail-open, and the instrument was the last one** (#26 auth r10, 2026-08-01). The ticket's mutation ledger has now had the class fixed at three depths, each found by the round after the one that fixed the previous: r6, the mutation never reached the artifact the suite imports (seven false passes); r9, the mutation silently stopped matching its target and a `> /dev/null` turned the throw into a green row; r10, the ledger applied and restored a mutation and **never ran the suite it credited**, so every "14 of 52" in it was a human transcription with the same standing as a comment. Fixed by making a row carry a real `mutate → build → suite-red` receipt, and the receipt logic is where the interesting part is — a red result requires *failures parsed from the runner's own JSON* (a non-zero exit is not a failing test), a *green baseline that ran more than zero tests* (an empty green is the most convincing false receipt available), and a *non-empty intersection of failing-after with passing-before* (equal-size-but-different failure sets read as caught to every count-based check). That decision is a pure function with its own unit tests, because the three previous layers went untested for exactly the reason this one nearly did: testing it end to end means spawning real suites, so nobody does. Lesson kept: **when a verification layer starts deciding, extract the decision from the machinery and pin it by value — otherwise the only test of the checker is the thing it is checking.** Cost, stated rather than hidden: a full `--prove-all` is ~9 minutes of which ~7 is the two Playwright rows, so the cadence is per-row on change and full at round close, not per commit.

**Process note — a guard must cover what is painted, not only what is stored** (#10 prototype r8, 2026-08-01, fourth appearance of one class). The round built a genuinely strong structural fix: one function mints every recorded change, marks it with an unforgeable token, and an invariant validates that the pair the record moved through actually exists. Every forgery attempt failed. And the defect persisted, because the renderer paints a *different* pair — a `was → now` string — that the invariant never inspects, so the checker was validating an invisible operand while a fabricated one was on screen. Lesson kept: **enumerate what the user can read, and check that; a guarantee that covers the stored representation and not the rendered one protects the database and lies to the reader.** The class has now moved outward four times — from the value, to the guard, to the guard's operands, to the operands the reader sees — and each round closed exactly one layer.

**Process note — a chokepoint is not a boundary** (#39 UI r2→r4, 2026-08-01). Three rounds enforced the same attribution guarantee at three different chokepoints, and each time a caller walked around it: the free string moved from the actor slot to the body slot; then a check went inside the only documented constructor, and a caller wrote the exported interface's literal directly and skipped it (`tsc` exit 0, a real person's name over words she did not write). Each fix was correct about the path it guarded. Lesson kept: **enforcing an invariant at a construction site protects only callers who use that site — close the type so no other value inhabits it, and re-derive at the render or write boundary, which is the one place a future call site cannot skip.** Ask of every guard: what is the *last* place this value passes before it becomes visible or durable, and is the check there?

**Process note — the audit-exemption class, three sites** (#39 UI r2/r3/r4, 2026-08-01). An audit skipping low-opacity elements hid two sub-3:1 controls; a doctrine paragraph (mine) authorized it; and a separate ring audit returned `null` for controls with no outline, dropping the app's only ring-less control — its primary input — from the measured set instead of failing it. Same shape, three files, one of them fixed in the same commit family that introduced the next. Lesson kept: **when a guard's rule is violated by exactly one element, suspect the guard before the element** — and grep the whole harness for `continue`, `return null`, and early exits whenever a rule appears to be universally satisfied.

**Process note — relocating a value into a trusted store does not make it trusted** (#21/#22, 2026-08-01, three appearances). The actor moved out of the event payload into a separate argument — and was still forgeable until the command layer *derived* it from the authenticated session. The receipt window moved out of the mutable `messages` table into an immutable ledger column — and is still forgeable, because the append boundary accepts it as a parameter and validates only its shape. Each move was correct and insufficient for the same reason. Lesson kept: **trust follows derivation, not location. When a value lands somewhere trusted, ask who computed it; if the answer is "the caller", the boundary must recompute or verify it, not merely store it.** Practical test for any new trusted column or argument: could a direct caller of the lowest-level write path supply a well-formed lie?

**Process note — the measuring instrument was wrong, and doctrine inherited it** (#39 UI r3, 2026-08-01). A contrast harness's block parser matched selector names inside `tokens.css`'s own provenance comment, so every "dark theme" assertion had been re-measuring light since the day it was written. Numbers from that harness reached two gauntlet receipts and then `design/CONVENTIONS.md`, where they were cited as universal floors. The practical rule survived (independent compositing measurements of real rendered rows agreed), but the justification was a light-theme argument stated as a general one. Lesson kept: **a measurement that becomes doctrine must record its instrument, and the instrument needs its own test — a self-check that fails loudly if two supposedly different configurations parse identically.**

**Process note — the orchestrator wrote the exemption it later criticized** (#39 UI r2→r3, 2026-08-01). I faulted an audit harness for skipping low-opacity elements; the harness was faithfully implementing an exemption *I had written into `CONVENTIONS.md` one paragraph above the rule* ("a disabled button at ~2.5:1 is an inactive-state exemption"). Two sub-3:1 elements survived two review rounds because of it, and one had never been named by any receipt. Lesson kept: **before faulting a guard for an exemption, check whether the doctrine authorized it — and when writing a rule, treat every exemption as an escape hatch someone will use correctly and unhelpfully.** The corollary now in CONVENTIONS: an audit may not exempt the case its rule covers.

**Process note — a house style can defeat source-grepping checks** (#39 UI r3, 2026-08-01). This repo's builders quote the defect they are forbidding directly above the fix, in a comment. A guard that greps source for the forbidden pattern therefore matched its own explanatory comment and passed on a mutated file. Every source-grepping check now strips comments first. Lesson kept: **a check that reads source must read what executes, not what is written — comments, strings and docs are not code, and this house's commenting style guarantees a collision.**

**Process note — "invoked" is not "runs"** (#40 r2 delta → #28, 2026-08-01). Six rounds of CI hardening asked whether a protected script was *invoked* in command position, and a parser answered correctly. `false && node scripts/ci/assert-page-serves.mjs; true` satisfies that question, skips the assertion, and exits green — while the mutation ledger, which recovers the script name by regex and executes it directly, certifies the assertion caught its mutation. Both halves of the verification stack agreed on something false. Lesson kept: **a guard on how a command is written must also ask whether it is reached; and a ledger must execute the validated command itself, not a name extracted from it — otherwise the ledger proves the script works, not that the pipeline runs it.**

**Process note — a timing test with generous slack measures "eventually", not the bound** (#26 auth r8, 2026-08-01). After I was corrected for accepting a bound without a measurement, the builder wrote one — a 40ms sweep interval with 250ms of slack, six intervals' worth, which passed against a sweep running four times less often. It was caught by `widen-sweep-window`, a mutation written to verify the fix, **which found the test instead**. Lesson kept: **a timing assertion's slack must be small next to the interval it measures, or it asserts liveness rather than the bound** — and the cheapest way to find out is to mutate the interval, not the code. Companion note from the same round: a ledger limitation recorded twice ("`--restore` is `git checkout --`") is a defect you have decided to keep paying for; the third encounter destroyed an uncommitted fix.

**Process note — a bug in the fix measured the vulnerability** (#40 r2, 2026-08-01). Building the image-identity binding, the first version ran `record-built-images` *with* the mutation overlay applied, so the `swapped-image` case recorded the doppelganger as "what this run built" and passed. That accident is a faithful model of the pre-fix state, and it answered the severity question directly: **without identity binding, a stack running an entirely different image passes every assertion in the job.** Found by running all 15 cases rather than by reading them. Lesson kept: **when a fix accidentally reproduces the defect it targets, that is the measurement — record what the accident proved rather than only fixing it.** Also recorded from the same round: the mutation ledger reads the working tree, so switching branches mid-run yields a false receipt; a ledger should assert a clean tree before trusting its own output.

**Process note — a checker can pass honestly on a fabricated input** (#10 prototype r7, 2026-08-01). The correction-chain invariant compares a change's `from` against its `to` and fires when they match. It passed on a chain entry asserting a change that never happened, because `from` was computed from the prior answer's *display text* — a system row reading "lars chose: X" — rather than from the statement the record actually held. The relationship the guard checked was true; its operands were wrong. The same class had already entered through two earlier paths, each time with a guard that was correct about what it compared and blind to where the values came from. Lesson kept: **a guard over a relationship is only as good as the provenance of its operands — validate where a value came from, not just how it relates to another value.** Practical form: when a check reads a field, ask which code path last wrote it and whether that path could have written a display string where a record value belongs.

**Process note — an accepted bound needs a measurement, not a paragraph** (#26 auth r6→r7, 2026-08-01, orchestrator error). I accepted a 15-second presence-after-removal window as a documented limitation and asked the builder to state it in code and the README. The delta review then showed the window was not a ceiling — a hung membership lookup leaves the sweep flag set and later intervals are skipped, so presence can persist indefinitely — and that the test written for it installed a 60-second interval and evicted via a command, never measuring the advertised window at all. Lesson kept: **accepting a limitation is a claim with a number in it, and numbers get measured. Require the test that measures the bound, and require it to fail when the bound is exceeded — otherwise "accepted" means "unexamined" and the documentation makes it look examined.**

**Process note — the guard written to excuse its own rule** (#39 UI r2, 2026-08-01). A contrast rule forbids unreadable de-emphasis; the audit harness enforcing it opened with `if (effectiveOpacity(el) < 0.999) continue;`, justified in a comment naming disabled chips as not-meant-to-be-read. So the one element violating the rule was the one element the guard skipped, and the suite stayed green. The critic's phrasing is the lesson: *the rule and the guard disagree, and the guard wins.* This is the same class as the prototype's sticky-footer whitelist, which had to be narrowed before the invariant could see its own defect. Lesson kept: **when a guard carries an exemption, the exemption is a claim about what the rule does not cover — check it against the rule's text, because an exemption written for convenience silently rewrites the rule.**

**Process note — a mutation ledger must run the real pipeline** (#28 r4 and #40 r1, 2026-08-01). Twice now a ledger has credited a mutation to a check that never ran: in CI, two mutations also tripped an earlier rule and would have gone red without the rule under test; in the deployment job, two cases were stopped by an earlier config assertion while the ledger invoked the later scripts directly and claimed those caught them. Both ledgers proved each check *can* fail in isolation — not that the pipeline catches each mutation by the named check. Lesson kept: **run every mutation through the real ordered pipeline and record which gate actually fired; when an earlier gate legitimately catches it first, say so.** A ledger that invokes checks directly is testing the checks, not the gate.

**Process note — deterministic code does not give deterministic replay** (#22 realtime r3, 2026-08-01). Receipt validation ran the same derivation function on the live-append and replay paths, and still diverged: its inputs came from a `messages` row whose `authorId` is `ON DELETE SET NULL`, so deleting a human author makes an acceptance that folded cleanly replay as a receipt failure and vanish from state. Grok's phrasing is the lesson — *same derivation code, different substrate*. Lesson kept: **an event-sourced system needs immutable inputs, not merely deterministic functions — anything a fold validates against must be snapshotted into the event at append time, or a later mutation of the source rewrites history.** Corollary: audit every FK with `SET NULL`/`CASCADE` that feeds a validation path.

**Process note — a mutation ledger against a stale build is a lie** (#26 auth r6, 2026-08-01). A `drop-join` mutation reported 7 passing suites; the Playwright tests import the package as its built `dist`, which had not been rebuilt, so the ledger measured the old artifact while claiming to measure the mutated source. Rebuilt, the same mutation failed 5 of 7. Lesson kept: **a mutation ledger must rebuild every artifact its suites consume, and say so in its header — otherwise "N mutations, N caught" measures whatever was on disk.** This is the second time the re-runnable-ledger rule has paid for itself in a day; the first (#21) turned 12 apparent catches into escapes.

**Process note — the orchestrator gave a builder a false premise** (#26 auth r6, 2026-08-01). The round-6 brief instructed the builder to register a workspace in `.github/ci-manifest.json`; that file and the whole `scripts/ci/` policy engine exist only on the CI branch, not on main or on theirs. They neither invented a gate nor silently skipped the item — they carried the manifest across, verified against the CI branch's own scripts, and reported the premise as false. Lesson kept: **a fix-round brief asserts facts about the tree, and those assertions are claims like any other — say which branch a referenced file lives on, and expect a builder to report back when a premise does not hold rather than papering over it.**

**Process note — an invariant enforced on half its surface** (#39 UI r1, 2026-08-01). Two blind critics from different lineages found the same architectural gap from opposite ends: the no-synthesized-speech enforcement covered the *words* and not the *attribution*. The excerpt type was branded, phantom-typed and constructor-guarded — while the primary message path stayed `actor + body: string[]`, so page-authored text rendered under a human's name in the shipped demo, and the quotation type deliberately discarded the actor it was minted from, leaving every name beside a quote an unchecked free string. Lesson kept: **when you enforce a rule with types, enumerate every path the rule's subject can travel and check the enforcement covers all of them — a guarantee on the secondary path reads as a guarantee on the primary one, to reviewers and to the next builder.** Corollary observed the same round: a claim of enforcement can be *narrower* than it sounds without being false, which is why receipts should record what a guarantee does NOT cover.

**Process note — a false safety claim is worse than no safety** (#39 UI r1, 2026-08-01). Destructive action buttons emitted `data-hold="2000"` with a tooltip promising a two-second press-and-hold, and fired on a single click — the attribute had no consumer anywhere in the codebase. The label and the tooltip both advertised friction that did not exist, on the one action class the design corpus singles out as requiring it. Lesson kept: **an affordance that describes a protection it does not implement is a defect of a higher order than the missing protection — ship it working or do not describe it.**

**Process note — four rounds fixing propagation when the fix was the read** (#26 auth r2→r6, 2026-08-01). Rounds 2 through 5 each closed a real revocation-propagation path: reconcile in the same transaction, evict the socket roster, order demotions before commits, serialize under an advisory lock. Every one was a genuine defect and every one left the class open, because room authorization read only the derived `memberships` table — so any cleanup failure still granted access. The structural fix (join `workspace_members` into the authorization query) makes authorization independent of cleanup succeeding at all. Independent confirmation arrived the same day from the Buzz research: their cross-community workflow leak and shared agent-nest bug are the same shape — authorization consulting a derived table rather than the source of truth. Lesson kept: **when successive rounds each fix a different path to the same failure, stop fixing paths and ask what the authorization actually reads — a guard that depends on cleanup having run is not a guard.**

**Process note — first-draft tests assert mechanisms instead of measuring them** (#26 auth r3/r4/r5, 2026-08-01). Three rounds running, a builder's own first-draft test was wrong about the mechanism it tested: one anchored on an invalid-JSON reply a closing socket cannot emit; one asserted `isSecureUrl('https:/host') === false` when WHATWG parsing of a special scheme tolerates the missing slash; one rebuilt the config block it was supposed to inspect and so survived its own mutation. Each was caught by the builder running it against the unfixed code rather than by reading it. Lesson kept: **a test's premise is a claim about a mechanism, and claims get measured — run every new test against the unfixed code before believing it, and read the value the system actually produces rather than the one the API docs imply.**

**Process note — the deployment nobody ran** (#26 auth r4 → #40, 2026-08-01). Three rounds of gauntlet receipts described a compose deployment that had never served a page: under `NODE_ENV=production` the app 500s on every request (a correctly-refused console mail transport; **corrected 2026-08-01 by measurement in #40** — the mechanism is not build-time inlining of `NODE_ENV` but `server.js` executing `process.env.NODE_ENV = 'production'` before any app code, so no runtime override of any spelling survives. The separate build-time inlining defect was real but applied to `NEXT_PUBLIC_WS_URL`, verified by finding a probe value baked into `.next/standalone/apps/web/server.js`), and no image had built at all since a new workspace package landed without being copied into the Dockerfiles. Every gate was green because every gate tested the parts. Lesson kept: **a deployment that nothing exercises end-to-end is a claim, not a deployment — put one assertion against the real shipped artifact in CI, and make it assert real behavior (a page, a flow) rather than a health endpoint.** Corollary for this campaign: the gauntlet reads code and runs suites; it cannot see that the product does not boot unless something boots it.

**Process note — two components that both "work" and never meet** (#22 realtime r2, 2026-08-01). The migration took advisory lock `1096041522`; the server took `0x41545232` = `1096045106`. Both took *a* lock, neither ever contended, and every single-process test stayed green — the mutual-exclusion guarantee was absent while all the evidence said it was present. Found by a test the builder wrote about the lock itself rather than about the behavior the lock protects. Lesson kept: **when two components must agree on a shared constant (lock key, queue name, topic, cache namespace), assert the agreement directly — testing each side's behavior in isolation cannot see the disagreement.**

**Process note — a fix that passes the rule you just wrote** (#10 prototype r5, 2026-08-01). Declaring the missing grid track fixed the reported defect but introduced a second: a `display:none` child is not a grid item, so hiding the optional region slid the composer onto a `1fr` track that resolved to 0 and pushed it off-screen. It passed both geometry invariants written moments earlier, and was found only by measuring boxes by hand afterward. Lesson kept: **an invariant written to catch a defect is calibrated to that defect — when your fix passes it, measure anyway, and treat the new invariant as untested until something else fails it.**

**Process note — hand-counted numbers always drift** (#28 CI, 2026-08-01). Three times in one ticket a number written in prose disagreed with the thing it counted: "15 rules" over 18, "five pairs" over nine, "four lookalikes" over five — the last one recurring within a day of the first being structurally fixed elsewhere. Each was corrected by *deriving* the number (declare the set, count it at runtime, print it from the self-test) rather than by fixing the text; the final round made the self-tests read the README's stated numbers back and compare. Lesson kept: **any count that appears in prose is a claim, and an underived claim drifts — derive it, or expect it to be wrong within the week.** Same round: a builder's first tightened matcher was itself satisfied by the decoy it was written to reject, caught only because they wrote the mutation before trusting the fix — **write the evasion first, then the guard.**

**Process note — a fix round can delete the coverage it appears to add** (#26 auth r3→r4, 2026-08-01). Rewriting a test that was theatre proved the new property (in-flight coalescing) but silently dropped the old property the theatre test had falsely claimed (sticky revocation): removing the `revoked` early-return would have passed. A critic found it by mutation reasoning, not by reading assertions. Three sibling tests had the same shape — one passes if the swept loop never runs, one is satisfied by an unrelated close handler, one is sequential where the risk is concurrent. **Standing rule adopted:** every test added or changed in a fix round must state the specific source mutation it now catches; a test whose mutation nobody can name is not yet a test.

**Process note — a receipt naming a syntax that does not exist** (#28 CI r4, 2026-08-01). The r3 delta critic faulted the source scanner for missing `test.each([...]).fails(...)`; the builder found that form is not a Vitest 4 API at all — it throws. But probing the real API surface, they wrote seven working evasions as actual test files, ran them, got 8 expected failures, and `vitest run` still exited 0: the line matcher missed five. Lessons: **a critic's named example can be wrong while its underlying finding is right — verify the mechanism, not the example**; and **when a witness is doubted, enumerate the real evasion set empirically (write the evading tests and run them) rather than reasoning about which forms exist.** The same round saw the rule-count mistake recur within a day of being structurally fixed elsewhere — a hand-counted number returns unless the count is derived.

**Process note — test theatre found by its own author** (#26 auth r3, 2026-08-01). A revocation test passed against both broken and fixed code: it sent its probe frames after the close handshake, and the `ws` client dropped them locally, so the assertion never reached the server. Rewritten as a ten-frame burst it failed against the *fixed* code too — `handleFrame` is fired-not-awaited, so every frame beat the cache check before the first lookup returned. Two lessons: **a test whose subject is a client-library side effect is testing the library, not the server**; and **a settled-verdict cache is worthless when the caller picks the burst size** — coalesce the in-flight lookup. The same round also refuted two of its own predecessor's claims in source (Next sets `x-forwarded-for` with `??=` rather than appending, so a hop count of 1 without a genuinely appending proxy is spoofable; a short forwarded chain clamped to the caller's own entry). Lesson kept: **builders auditing their own prior round find defects critics structurally cannot — schedule the self-audit, do not hope for it.**

**Process note — a test that could not fail** (#21 core engine, 2026-08-01). The acceptance rule-table test derived its probe confidences from the config it was testing, making it invariant under changes to those numbers: mutating claim θ_auto from 0.7 to 0.6 passed all 531 tests. Found by the builder's own mutation pass, fixed by pinning the table by value separately. Lesson kept: **a test that reads its expectations from the code under test proves only self-consistency — pin expected values literally, and mutation-test the numbers, not just the branches.** This is the assertion-side twin of the fail-open class the gauntlet keeps finding on the output side.

**Process note — orchestrator slips** (2026-08-01). Two in one merge: piping gate commands through `tail -1` masked a non-zero lint exit (pushed a red-lint main), and `git add -A` staged harness worktrees as gitlinks (pushed, then scrubbed). Both are the exact fail-open class the campaign hunts, committed by its orchestrator. Lessons kept: **never pipe a gate command's output — capture exit codes explicitly**; **never `git add -A` in a tree shared with harness state; add paths deliberately.**

**Process note — interpretation spike** (research, 2026-08-01). Measured on 6 live runs: model confidence carries no signal (0.937 on wrong vs 0.928 on right), so θ-band escalation routing was dead code as designed; #8 amended to deterministic pre-call text triggers. Lesson kept: **route on signals you compute, not signals the model self-reports; and test the cheap tier before building infrastructure around its assumed failure modes.**

**Process note — an invariant suite blind to geometry, and a fix that broke the other state** (#10 prototype r5, 2026-08-01). Round 4's blocking defect was a grid column declaring four tracks for five children: the cross-room trace resolved to 0px, the feed painted through it, and both trace controls failed hit-testing. Four state-shaped invariants (attention, row-scope, quotation, and every count) passed on that frame, because every one of them was true — the state was right and the pixels were not. Adding the fifth track then broke the *other* state: the trace is `hidden` most of the time, a `display:none` child is not a grid item, and with auto-placement the composer slid onto a `1fr` track that resolved to 0 and left the viewport at `top: 900`. That second defect passed the two geometry rules written minutes earlier (non-zero height, no sibling overlap) and was found by measuring the boxes by hand, not by the checker. Lessons kept: **an artifact whose failure mode is visual needs an invariant that reads the painted geometry — `getBoundingClientRect` and `elementFromPoint`, not the state that produced them**; **a conditionally-rendered child makes auto-placement wrong in one of its two states, so place every child by name and let hiding one leave an empty track**; and **when a fix passes the rule you just wrote, measure the thing anyway — the rule was written against the defect you already knew about.**
**A guarantee proved over a normalized form is not a guarantee about the raw one** (#21 core engine r8, 2026-08-01). r7 admitted a word list into the acceptance path on the argument that "the proposer does not control the input — the receipt has already proved the statement is the author's verbatim words". The receipt proves `normalizeForReceipt(quote) === normalizeForReceipt(statement)`; the predicate read the **raw** statement. So the proposer controlled the statement freely inside that equivalence class, and one zero-width space spliced into one word evaded all ten patterns at both enforcement points at once — the reading auto-accepted with the poisoned text landing in state. The argument was sound about a representation the code never consumed. Lesson kept: **whenever a guarantee is established over a normalized form, every downstream check must consume that same form — and enforce it with a type, not a habit.** The r8 repair brands the fold's output (`ReceiptText`) so a call site handing over the rawer string does not compile; three predicates were converted and the compiler found the fourth. The general sweep it implies — *for every predicate, name which representation the guarantee upstream was established over* — found a second live instance (a sed-correction marker reading the raw body while every marker beside it read the routing fold) and a third (an NFKC dedup discarding `10² ms` as a duplicate of `102 ms`, silently, on the one path that destroys a reading).

**A hand list called a published standard** (#21 core engine r8, 2026-08-01). `QUESTION_MARKS` described itself as "Unicode's own inventory of question marks, enumerated" — a closed published list, which is the repo's own bar for an admissible enumeration. Unicode publishes no such property. It was a hand list, it was missing the ordinary chat ornaments `❓`/`❔` (reopening an r5 defect outright), and it contained a mark that is not a question mark at all (U+AA5D is the Cham danda, given an invented name). The test that pinned it asserted the list against a hand-maintained copy of itself, so neither half could see a mark absent from both. Lessons kept: **a comment asserting a property of an external standard is a factual claim and must be measured, not reasoned about** (the third time this exact shape has cost this file a round — `\s` and NEL was the first); and **when completeness cannot be derived, convert it into a total classification a machine can check** — r8 partitions `\p{Sentence_Terminal}` into "a question mark" and "explicitly not one" and fails until every code point Unicode publishes lands in exactly one half. Also: **when two checks are mirrors with opposite safe directions, they need two instruments**, which is the same finding that split `normalizeForMatch` in r5, arriving again one function over.

**A refusal that names a remedy is a promise** (#21 core engine r8, 2026-08-01). Two shipped in r7 and neither remedy existed. The acceptance engine told the room a laundered reading "goes to Needs-you with its quote for a person to accept or decline" — and the attention projection skipped every proposal that was not a decision or a commitment, returning zero items and zero refusals; the adjudication that let the type narrowing ship rested on that sentence. Separately, the reducer refused to reopen a superseded decision and named "retract the decision that replaced it" as the route, which changed nothing at all: `supersededById` was written once and read by no other path. Lesson kept: **execute the remedy a refusal names, in a test, in the same commit as the refusal** — a stated route is a claim about behaviour and gets pinned like any other. The corollary found the same round: **a correction verb is a second way into every invariant the fold enforces at creation**, and `applyRetype` had no mutant and no test reaching past its own payload, so it was the one door into three states the reducer refuses to be handed directly.

**A guard's anchor is an input too** (#21 core engine r12, 2026-08-02). r10 built a guard refusing a statement that adds link markup its author never wrote; r11 built its twin for block markers. Both diffed the statement against the proposal's **`quote`** — and `quote` is a field the proposer writes, beside `payload.statement`. Writing the same forgery into both fields cancelled the multiset difference to empty, and a link whose visible text names `safe.example` while its destination is `evil.example` auto-accepted, landed in `state.objects` attributed to the author, and survived replay. Every entry in the block-marker inventory was reachable the same way. The quote *is* validated against the message body, twice — but both checks run through `normalizeForReceipt`, **and that fold was designed to ignore exactly the dimension the guards depend on**: it collapses whitespace runs and unfolds `[label](dest "title")` into `label dest title`. Two mechanisms, each correct alone; the defect was the seam between them, and a docblock promising the quote occurs in the body "verbatim under `normalizeForReceipt`" was that seam written down and believed for two rounds. Lessons kept: **a guard on what a person wrote must be anchored to text that person wrote — comparing two fields the same caller filled in proves nothing, whatever either of them says**; and **when a check's guarantee is supplied by a *different* mechanism, name the dimension that mechanism actually preserves, because the fold that proves your words are the author's may have been built to erase your punctuation.** The corollary the same round: r11's reader reported markers at line *beginnings*, so the two rendered breaks that carry no marker — a blank line, and two trailing spaces — were clean even with an honest quote. **A closed inventory is closed over the question it asks; ask the next question and it is a hand list again.**

**Two producers was the wrong axis; the other one is people** (#21 core engine r12, 2026-08-02). r11 stopped one producer of an attention class concluding on another producer's behalf, by putting the producer into the examination key. r12 found the same slide one axis over: `mentionItems` declared an examination per *subject* it was handed, and an `ExaminedSubject` deliberately has the person taken out of it — so a cycle whose only mention signal named bob on an object resolved alice's mention on that object, permanently, with the "keep the stored status" rule pinning it afterwards. No caller mistake; an ordinary sliding window past the message that named alice does it. The fix was already written in the same file, for the sibling case: *a signal-driven producer can only conclude about what it was handed, and what it was handed is what it raised*, so it declares nothing. Lesson kept: **"this source decides for everyone at once" is a claim that only a source reading shared state may make — a source reading caller signals knows about the people its signals name and nothing whatever about the rest.**

**Process note — the tool timeout killed the wrapper, not the runner** (#21 core engine r8, 2026-08-01). `mutants/run.mjs` mutates `packages/core/src` in place, and its own docblock warns that a hard kill leaves a live mutant. A 10-minute Bash timeout fired on a ~11-minute run: the shell wrapper died, the runner did not, and it kept cycling mutations for another ten minutes. Every check run in that window — a drift scan, `tsc`, the full suite — measured a moving tree, and produced one false "live mutant" report and one false 2-test failure, both of which evaporated on re-check. Nothing was committed. Lessons kept: **a timeout on a wrapper is not a timeout on the process** — `ps` for the real PID before believing any post-kill state; and **after any interruption of an in-place mutation tool, re-verify by re-running the tool to completion, not by inspecting the tree**, because a tree that is being written cannot be inspected. The existing rule (stage explicit paths, never `git add -A`) is what kept this from reaching a commit.
**Process note — when "read something the caller cannot compose" turns out to be an empty set, say so and narrow the claim** (#22 r8, 2026-08-01). The entry above leaves two fixes for a guard reading caller-authored text: stop claiming it binds, or read something unforgeable. Round 8 went looking for the second and proved it empty *here*, which is the part worth keeping. Every candidate — a transaction-local GUC, an advisory-lock token minted inside the function, a temp-table witness, an unpredictable nonce — was executed by a bare caller in a bare `DO` block with **no CREATE privilege anywhere**, because caller and function are the same session and the same role; and checking *which* nonce needs a store the caller cannot write, which does not exist on that side of the boundary. The argument then closes from the other end: the set of principals that can INSERT into the table at all is `{owner, superuser}`, anyone outside it is refused by a `REVOKE` one layer *before* the trigger runs (executed against a fresh role: `permission denied`, while the SECURITY DEFINER door still opened for it), and anyone inside it can disable the trigger. So the check's discrimination against an author is **exactly zero and no rewrite can raise it**. Lesson kept: **when a guard cannot bind, the fix is a proof that it cannot, not a better guard — and the proof is what stops the next round re-earning the belief.** The corollary that made it shippable rather than deletable: the check was kept, relabelled, for the one accident its structural half cannot see (a stray direct INSERT in a transaction that already appended legitimately, where the lock is still held but the function's frame is gone), and a mutant now removes the *description* rather than the code, because the description was the defect.

**Process note — a record validated against its own cursor is not validated** (#22 r8, 2026-08-01). The client's durable journal parsed every envelope strictly and then checked one thing about the record: `lastSeq === events.at(-1).roomSeq`.
